# Ask Me What I Want

*The Register of Uses · Book Four*

**Review copy** · chapters 1–30 · **94,431 words** · 30 chapters · average 3,148

*She has spent four years asking questions for other people. Now she has to ask one for herself, of the one woman in England who has spent forty years refusing to be asked: Ask me. I will answer.*

---

## While You Read

1. This is the last book of four. Does it stand on its own, or does it lean on the other three?
2. Esther and Tace both refuse the same thing for forty years. Did you believe both refusals?
3. Mark any sentence you wish you had written, and any passage where you were confused. Both are equally useful.
4. You do not need to remember the numbers. You need to tell me where you stopped.

## One · The Paper in the Drawer

The drawer stuck in wet weather, and it had been wet since Candlemas, and I had learned to open it with the flat of my hand instead of the handle, because the handle had come off in January and I had not put it back. The screw was in the saucer on the shelf. I knew which saucer. I had put the screw there myself and I had walked past the saucer every day since, and the walking past was a small daily decision that I had made so often that it had stopped looking like a decision and had become a habit of the house, the way the third stair had stopped being a stair and had become a noise.

Edward had offered to mend it. He had offered twice, once in January with a screwdriver in his hand and once in March without one, and both times I had said that it did not matter, and both times it had been true and not true at the same time, which is the way with small lies that are mostly housekeeping.

On the fourteenth of April, in the afternoon, with the rain coming down the valley in one grey sheet and the fire low because it was too warm for a fire and too cold without one, I put the flat of my hand against the drawer front and pushed, and it came out an inch, and then another inch, and then it came out all the way, and there was the paper.

It was at the back, under the tin box, and it had been at the back under the tin box since the night I put it there in the spring of the year before. It was a half sheet, folded twice, and the folds had gone soft the way folds do when a thing has been carried in a pocket and then put away, and one corner had worn through so that I could see the wood of the drawer through the paper, and the hole was the shape of a thumb.

I took it out and sat down on the edge of the bed with it, and I did not open it for a while, because opening it was a thing I could do at any time and had not done at any time for thirteen months, and a thing you can do at any time is the hardest thing in the world to do.

Then I opened it.

The hand was mine and it was smaller than I remembered. I had written it at a table in an inn with a bad pen, and the bad pen had blobbed on the first word, so that *What* had a dark head on it like a nail, and the rest of the line ran out thin.

*What I want is*

Nothing after it. Not a dash, not a full stop. The line simply stopped, the way a person stops in a doorway when they have forgotten what they came into the room for.

I sat with it in my lap and the rain went on down the valley.

I had thought, in the winter, that a year would make the sentence easier to finish. That was the theory I had been living on: that the sentence was short because the ink was fresh and the room was cold and there had been a man downstairs who was not asking me anything, and that with time and a warm room and no one waiting, the rest of it would come. It had not come. I looked at the paper for a long time and the space after *is* stayed the size it was, which is to say the size of the rest of my life.

Two things, for anybody who has come to this book without the other three, and I will say them once and not again. The first is what I am paid to do. I read things. A hand on a page, a knife taken along a gutter, a groove worn into a knuckle, the set of a door that has been lifted rather than dragged: out of marks like those I can say what a thing was used for, and by whom, and what it cost the person who used it. I have been paid for it since I was a girl, which makes it a trade and not a gift, and it is the only trade I have. The second is why I am alive to practise it. I was read myself once, when I was a child, by a woman in a lane by a ford, and what she read was the use I had been made for, and it had never been used, and so when I drowned in the spring of the year I was eighteen I did not stay drowned, and I came back into a room I had left as a girl. It was April of 1834 and I was twenty-one, and everything in this book comes out of those two facts, including a half sheet in a drawer that I could not finish.

***

The table under the window had a leaf in it that I never let down, because a table with the leaf down has decided what it is for, and I cleared the mending off it and laid the paper in the middle and pressed the fold flat with my hand, and the curl came back the moment I took the hand away.

The ink had dried in the pot to a skin. I poured water on it and let it stand and stirred it with the end of the pen until the skin broke and the black came up through the water in a slow cloud, and then I set the pot down and waited, because ink that has been woken too fast writes grey.

The pen was the spare one and the nib had gone blunt on the left side, which is where a nib goes blunt in a hand that turns the paper instead of the wrist. I cut it on the nail of my thumb with the knife off the shelf, and the paring came off in a curl.

I drew the paper square with the edge of the table and dipped the pen and lifted it, and the nib came out with a bead on it that swelled and did not fall, and I put the nib over the paper at the place after *is*, and my hand stopped.

It stopped in the air with the bead getting heavier and the paper waiting underneath, and it did not come down.

I have done that since I was a girl. At the copybook, at the table in the passage where the accounts were kept, at every table I have sat down at with something to write that mattered more than the writing of it. The hand goes up and the hand waits, and the waiting is not doubt, it is more like a room being got ready. A room got ready can be stood in forever. My mother said I wrote like somebody crossing a stream on stones and that I would get across if I did not look at the water, and looking at the water is the only part of the crossing I have ever been good at.

Then I wrote a word, because a hand that has stopped will come down on anything rather than hang.

*Bread*

It was a good word as words go. It was true, and it was true about somebody else.

I picked up the knife and scraped. The blade is held upright and drawn towards you and the paper is held down with the other hand, and the ink lifts first in a smear and then the paper lifts with it, and what is left is thin enough to see the table through, with a shaving of paper curled on the cloth. I blew the crumbs off. The place where the word had been showed whiter than the paper round it. The erasure is the brightest part of a scraped page, and a person who takes up the sheet sees the hole and not the writing.

I had the paper in my hand when the latch went.

The woman from the lower cottage came in with the rain in the wool of her shawl, and she stood inside the door and looked at the floor first, the way a person does in a house that is not hers.

"I have come for the loan of the flat iron, if it is not standing in the fire."

"It is in the scullery. Take it, and bring it back when you have done."

She came back with the iron in her hand and the shawl off her head, and stopped at the end of the table.

"You are writing."

"I am trying to."

"To who?"

"Nobody yet."

She looked at the paper. My hand went flat on it before I had decided to put it there, and I left it where it was, and she looked at the hand instead, which was politer and no better.

"My man cannot write his name," she said. "He makes a mark, and it is the same mark every time, and he says a mark that is the same every time is a name. I have never been able to fault him in it."

"That is a good argument."

"It is the only one he has." She turned the iron over and looked at the sole of it, and then her face changed. "You have scraped it."

"It was the wrong word."

"A paper with a hole in it is worse than a paper with a lie on it," she said. "A lie can be read."

She said it the way a woman says a thing her husband says at the table. Then she put the shawl back over her head and took the iron up under it.

"They tell me you went to Tindle about the money."

"I went on Thursday."

"And what did they do?"

"They voted."

"Then they have voted," she said, "and a vote lets a man go home." She stopped with her hand on the latch. "Do not put in your letter that the parish is unkind. They are not unkind. They are slow, and there is nothing in slow to take hold of."

"It is not a letter."

"Then it is worse," she said, and went out, and the door came to, and the latch dropped after it with the small slack sound a wet latch makes.

I took my hand off the paper. The paper had the shape of my hand on it in damp, and the scraped place stayed white.

***

The kitchen was on the north side of the house and it took the wet off the hill, and the kettle was on the hob with the trivet under it, and the table had been scrubbed that morning and had the pale look of a table that has been scrubbed while it was still warm.

Edward was at the table with the drawer handle lying on its face in front of him and the screw out of the saucer standing in the hole of it, and the screw leaned, because the wood of the drawer front had gone soft round the thread and a screw will not bite into soft wood.

He had a piece of apple wood in his left hand and the knife in his right and he was cutting a sliver off it to make a plug, and the parings came off in curls into his palm, and when the palm was full he put the curls in his pocket rather than leave them on a table we eat off.

"You have been at that table since dinner," he said.

"Since Candlemas."

He laid the sliver against the hole, cut it thinner, tried it again.

I had the paper in my apron. I had put it there when the woman came in at the door and I had not taken it out since. I laid it on the corner of the table, away from the knife, and smoothed the fold with the ends of my fingers, and the damp shape my hand had left in it had dried out of it by then, and there was nothing on the paper anywhere to show that anybody had been in the room that afternoon.

Edward did not look at it. A man who does not look at a paper lying on the table at his elbow has decided that looking would be asking, and he had decided it in the spring and it had become part of the way he sat.

"There is a page in the book," he said, "that has had nothing put in it since the spring."

"There is a great deal of it in the kitchen."

"A page waits in a book," he said. "A kitchen does not wait for anybody. It goes on and it does not fill." He set the plug into the hole and took the screw out of the handle and put the point of it against the plug and turned, and the plug went down a little way and then spun, and he took it out again.

"What did you write today," I said.

"A letter about a barrow wheel. A certificate. A sum entered in the wrong column and entered again underneath it, because a ruled book will not give a column back."

"I have done that."

"You have done it at the table. You have done a great deal of it at the table." He cut another sliver, going with the grain, laid it beside the other, did not use it. "A screw wants something to hold to. That is all a screw has ever wanted. Put a bigger screw in and there is a bigger hole, and a man can go on like that until there is no drawer front left to speak of."

I folded the paper once along the worn crease and stopped, because the other fold was the fold that had gone through, and the hole came round under my thumb and the thumb fitted it, and I took the thumb off.

"You will put it back," he said.

"I will put it back."

"Paper keeps. It is the only thing in this house that does."

He dropped the plug and the screw back into the saucer he had carried them down in and set the handle on its edge and put his head down along it, and the light off the window went along the brass.

I took the paper up and went out through the passage with it, and behind me the knife went on cutting, and the sound of it stopped at the door and did not follow me.

***

I said it out loud, which is a thing I do with difficult sentences, because a sentence said aloud has to be finished or it hangs in the room and everybody can see it hanging.

"What I want is."

The rain stopped for a moment, the way rain does, and then it started again, and in the gap I heard the clock in the passage tick twice, and I heard Edward turn a page in the other room.

Then I folded the paper and put it back in the drawer, under the tin box, and pushed the drawer shut with the flat of my hand, and went and stood in the doorway of the room where my husband was sitting.

He had the notebook open. He wrote in it with a pencil because ink was too permanent for a thing he was still deciding about, and the pencil had worn a grey shine on the page edges, and the shine was the only sign of how many times he had opened it.

"You are in the doorway," he said, without looking up.

"I am."

"Standing in doorways is a habit of yours. You have done it since Barrowfield."

"I am aware."

He wrote a line and stopped and looked at me over the top of the book, and his face had the patient look it had when he had decided to wait for something and was prepared to wait a long while and did not want me to feel hurried.

"The twenty-eighth is not written," he said.

"I did not ask."

"You did not have to. You were standing in the doorway looking at the book, and a woman who stands in a doorway looking at a book is asking about the book."

"I was looking at your hands."

He turned the book face down on the table, which was a thing he did when he did not want me reading over his shoulder, and also a thing he did when he wanted me to know that he was not hiding anything and the turning over was a courtesy rather than a lock.

"The twenty-eighth is yours to write," he said. "I told you that in the spring. The page under the twenty-seventh is blank because it belongs to you, and I will not write in it, and if you never write in it I will leave the book to the parish and let them wonder."

"That is a great waste of a good book."

"It is a great waste of a good blank," he said. "The book is nothing. The blank is the whole of it."

***

The carrier came at four with the Tindle bag and a letter in it, and the letter was addressed to me in the hand of the young clerk, which was a careful hand with the loops too even, the hand of a man who had been taught to write by a grandfather who had been taught to write by a man who counted everything twice.

I read it standing up, which is a thing I do with letters that I expect to be about money.

*Madam,*

*The vestry of this parish will meet on Thursday the twenty-fourth at eleven in the morning in the vestry room, to consider the report of Mr. Hesketh, who has been appointed by the ratepayers to examine the out-payments of the parish for the last forty years. Mr. Hesketh has raised a question regarding the sum of eight pounds a year paid in respect of a person maintained in the turning of this parish, whose name does not appear in the rate-book, and whose settlement has not been proved.*

*The vestry has directed me to write to you because you are the only person living who has examined the Tindle books, and because Mr. Hesketh has said, in my hearing, that he does not intend to be answered out of a woman's memory.*

*I am, madam, your obedient servant,*

*Isaac Thwaite*

I read it twice. The second time I read it for the words that were not in it, which is the way to read a letter from a clerk, because a clerk writes what the vestry directs and the direction is in the gaps.

Eight pounds a year. Forty years. A person maintained in the turning whose name does not appear in the rate-book.

I put the letter on the table and stood with my hand flat on it, and the paper was warm from my palm and cold underneath, and the cold came up through the warm the way it does when a thing has been in a cold room a long while and the hand only warms the top of it.

"What is it," Edward said.

"The vestry at Tindle has found out what it has been paying for."

He got up and came round the table and read the letter over my shoulder, and I let him, and his breath moved the paper at the corner, and when he had finished he did not say anything for long enough that I turned round to look at him.

"Eight pounds a year," he said at last. "For forty years. That is three hundred and twenty pounds."

"Three hundred and twenty."

"And nobody in that parish has ever asked where it went."

"Nobody in that parish has ever asked anything," I said. "That is the whole of the trouble with Tindle. It is a parish that has been not-asking since before I was born, and it has got very good at it, and now a man has come who asks about money, and the not-asking has nowhere to go."

Edward put the letter down and squared it with the edge of the table, which was a habit of his when he was thinking about a document and had not yet decided what to say about it.

"He will strike her off," he said.

"He cannot strike off a person who is not on."

"He will strike off the payment. It comes to the same thing. A woman of seventy-one in a cottage at the end of a lane, with no settlement proved and no name in the book, and the parish stops paying for her bread." He looked at me. "That is not a legal question. That is a question of how long a person lives without bread."

I went to the window. The rain had stopped and the valley was going grey and gold the way it does at the end of a wet afternoon, and there was a man on the road below with a barrow, and the barrow had a wheel that wanted grease, and I could hear the wheel from the window, which meant the wind was coming up the valley rather than down it.

"I will go on Thursday," I said.

"I know."

"You are not going to tell me to be careful."

"No," Edward said. "I am going to tell you that Hesketh is not a fool, because a fool would not have found the eight pounds, and a man who is not a fool is harder to be angry with and much harder to answer." He picked up the notebook and put it under his arm. "And I am going to come with you, and you are going to say that you do not need me, and I am going to come anyway."

"I do not need you."

"I know," he said, and went out to see about the barrow, because the man with the wheel was stuck at the bottom of the lane and Edward could not pass a stuck wheel without doing something about it, and that was one of the things about him that I had stopped noticing and had started being glad of, which is the whole history of a marriage in one sentence.

---

## Two · The Vestry at Tindle

The way to Tindle ran with the river for the whole of its length, and on the Thursday it ran with the river in the rain, and Edward drove and I sat beside him with the letter in my pocket and the hood of my mother's coat up, and neither of us said anything for the first mile because there is a kind of silence that two people who are married can hold without it costing anything, and we had learned it in the second year and were still surprised by it in the third.

At the second mile he said, "What will you say?"

"I do not know yet."

"That is not like you."

"It is exactly like me," I said. "I have never once known what I would say in a room until I was in the room and could see who was sitting down and who was standing up. A room tells you which it is before anybody opens their mouth. That is the first thing I look at."

"And if Hesketh is standing up?"

"Then he has decided already and I will be talking to the wall."

"And if he is sitting down?"

"Then he has a paper in front of him and he means to read it, and a man reading a paper is a man who has already told himself what the paper says, and the only way to him is through the paper."

Edward drove for a while. The rain came off the hedges in small showers when the wind took them.

"You have thought about this a great deal for a woman who does not know what she will say."

"I have thought about the room," I said. "The room is not the same as the saying."

***

We came in at the lychgate, because the vestry door was on the far side and the yard between was mud, and the mud had been crossed so often that it had set in ridges, and the ridges held the rain in the afternoon light the way a ploughed field holds it.

Edward took the horse round. I went under the porch with the letter in my pocket and my hand flat on the wall, because the stones inside a porch are the driest thing a parish owns.

A man was standing in the porch with a lantern.

He had it up on the nail in the arch and he was underneath it with his hat in both hands, turning the hat round by the brim, and the hat was a good hat with the nap gone at the edge, and the turning of it was not nervousness, it was what a man does with his hands while he waits for a door he has no right to open.

"You will be the woman from Collow," he said.

"I will be."

"I am churchwarden this year." He put the hat on and took it off again. "There is a thing I want said to you before you go in, and it is not a thing the vestry knows I am saying."

The candle in the lantern had guttered and the glass was black on the far side. The rain came off the porch roof in a sheet when the wind took it and the water ran down the ridge in the yard and stood in the low place by the gate.

"I have signed the book for the turning every quarter since Michaelmas," he said, "and I have never been down the lane. My father went down it. He carried coals in the winter. He carried them to the door and put them inside the door and came out again, and he never once said he had laid eyes on her."

"Never."

"Not in his life." He turned the hat the other way. "It is not fear of her. That is the part nobody will credit. It is fear of the Monday the money stops."

"Why that Monday?"

"Because on that Monday she will have to come up it herself. Nothing going down the lane, and she will come up the lane, and she will come to a door, and it will be my door, because I am churchwarden, and I will be the man at the door." He put the hat on then and pulled the brim down. "I have dreamt of it. I have dreamt of it a good many times and it is always the same dream, and in the dream there is nothing in the house to give her."

"Have you said that in the vestry."

"No," he said. "I have said it to you, and you are not the vestry, and you are going in there now, and I have no business keeping you in a porch in the wet."

"She has a name," I said. "The parish has it on a paper in the chest."

He stopped turning the hat. "Then why does the book say the turning."

"Because nobody has to answer for a turning."

He stood with that. The rain filled the mouth of the porch and went by.

"Aye," he said. "That will be it."

He took the lantern down off the nail and held it out, and the light went up the wall of the church and showed how the stones had been laid and how badly they had been pointed.

"The door at the end of the passage," he said. "Do not knock. They will not hear you, and you will knock louder, and then every man in that room will know you have been standing outside."

He went down the yard with the lantern held low and the light went with him, and the ridges in the mud took it and lost it and took it again.

***

Tindle vestry room was a low stone place at the back of the church, with a table too big for it and a window too high for anybody to look out of, and there were nine men in it and one woman, and the woman was me, and I was there because a clerk had written me a letter and because a clerk's letter is a kind of summons that a person may refuse and will not.

The nine men were the vicar, four farmers, two shopkeepers, a man from the mill who was new and had a watch chain, and old Ruddock, who had been on the vestry since before I was born and who had the face of a man who had agreed to a great many things in that room and had never once been asked which of them he had wanted.

The man with the watch chain was Hesketh. He was about forty and he had the clean hands of a man who had not been in a field since he was a boy and did not miss it. He had a book open in front of him and a ruled sheet beside it, and the sheet was covered in figures in a small upright hand.

He was sitting down.

"Mrs. Rooke," he said, without getting up. "You are kind to come. I understand you have examined our books."

"I have examined the Tindle rate-book for the year thirty-three, and the order book, and the overseers' book for the same year."

"Then you will know the difficulty."

"I know a difficulty," I said. "I do not yet know whether it is yours."

One of the farmers laughed, a short laugh that stopped itself, and Hesketh did not look at him, and the not-looking was a small public correction that cost him nothing and cost the farmer something, and I noted it, because a man who can correct a room without speaking is a man who has corrected rooms before.

"The difficulty," Hesketh said, "is eight pounds a year."

"That is not a difficulty. That is a sum."

"The difficulty is that the sum is paid out of the poor rate, and the poor rate is levied on the parish, and the parish is entitled to know what it is paying for. For forty years this parish has paid eight pounds a year in respect of a woman in the turning. She is not in the rate-book. She is not in the overseers' book as a pensioner. She is not on any list that this vestry has been able to produce. The payment is entered in the account as *the turning*, and nothing else, and when I asked the late clerk what *the turning* meant, he said it meant the turning, and when I asked him whether the woman had a name, he said that everybody knew her."

"Everybody does know her," old Ruddock said. "She is the woman in the turning. She has been the woman in the turning since I was a boy. You may as well ask whether the river has a name."

"The river is not paid eight pounds a year."

"The river does not need bread."

"Mr. Ruddock," Hesketh said, and his voice was patient in the way that a man's voice is patient when he has decided to be patient in front of witnesses, "I am not proposing to turn an old woman out of a cottage in the rain. I am proposing that this vestry establish, once and for all, whether it has any legal obligation to her. If it has, we will meet it and enter it properly and be done with the pretending. If it has not, then the money is a voluntary gift made by a great many ratepayers who have never been asked whether they wished to make it, and a voluntary gift that nobody has voted on for forty years is not a gift. It is a habit. And habits are how parishes go quietly to the wall."

He had a paper. He read it. He had told himself what it said.

I let him read it. There is no point in interrupting a man who is reading his own case, because he will only begin again.

The paper was a schedule. Forty years of eight pounds. Three hundred and twenty pounds in all. Beside it, in the same upright hand, the cost of maintaining a pauper in the house of industry, which was four shillings a week and came to ten pounds eight a year, so that the parish had, on Hesketh's arithmetic, saved two pounds eight a year for forty years by keeping her out of the house and in a cottage, and had saved it without a vote, without a resolution, and without a name.

"Two pounds eight a year," he said, and put the paper down. "That is what the arrangement has been worth to this parish. Not charity. Economy. And I do not say it to be cruel. I say it because a thing that has been economy for forty years is a thing that will be looked at again the moment the price changes, and the price has changed. The house of industry has raised its rate. The county has raised the county rate. And I have been asked, by men who pay, why this parish maintains a woman to whom it can prove no obligation whatsoever."

The room was quiet. The rain hit the high window.

"Mr. Hesketh," I said. "May I ask a question about the paper."

"You may."

"You have the payment entered as *the turning* for forty years. Have you looked at the first year."

"The first year is the same as the rest."

"The first year is never the same as the rest," I said. "The first year is where a thing is decided. After that it is only repeated. If you want to know what this parish did, you must read the year it did it in, and the year it did it in was not in your schedule, because your schedule begins at the earliest entry you could find in the account, and an account is not a decision. A decision is in the order book, or in the minutes, or in a letter, or in nothing at all, and if it is in nothing at all then the first year will tell you so by being blank in a particular way."

Hesketh looked at me for a moment. Then he turned over his paper and made a mark on it.

"That is a fair observation," he said. "I will put it to the vestry that the first year be examined."

"The first year is not in the chest," Isaac Thwaite said. He was sitting at the end of the table with the minute book in front of him, and he had not spoken until then, and he spoke carefully, the way a man speaks when he has been the youngest person in a room for eleven years. "The order book for ninety-three is in the chest. The account for ninety-three is not. My grandfather used to say that the ninety-three account was underwater."

"Underwater," Hesketh said.

"The river came up into the vestry in the year of the great flood, and the books that were in the chest that year were wet, and some of them were dried and some of them were not, and the ones that were not were copied out fair afterwards and the copies do not always say what the originals said, because my grandfather copied them and my grandfather copied what he thought they ought to say." He stopped. "That is a thing he told me himself. He was not ashamed of it. He said a parish that keeps every word it ever wrote will hang itself with them one day."

"Then the ninety-three account is a copy."

"The ninety-three account does not exist at all," Isaac said. "There is a gap in the run. The run goes eighty-nine, ninety, ninety-one, ninety-two, ninety-four. There is nothing for ninety-three. My grandfather said it was under the water and never came up."

Hesketh sat back. I watched him do the arithmetic that men like him cannot help doing, which is the arithmetic of how much a thing will cost to find out.

"Then I must proceed on what I have," he said. "And what I have is forty years of payment and no obligation proved. I will put it to the vestry that the payment cease at Michaelmas, and that the overseers be directed to inquire into the woman's settlement, and that if no settlement is proved, the usual course be taken."

The usual course. I had heard the phrase before, in another room, in another county, said by a man in a good coat. The usual course for a person with no settlement is removal to the place they came from, and if the place cannot be found, the place they came from is wherever the justices say it is, and if the justices will not say, it is the house of industry, and the house of industry is not a place a woman of seventy-one comes out of.

"Mr. Hesketh," I said. "Before you put it."

"Mrs. Rooke, you have no standing in this vestry."

"I have no standing," I agreed. "I have a question, and a question does not need standing, it only needs a mouth. Will you put it as the usual course, or will you put it as removal."

There was a small movement in the room, the kind that happens when a word has been chosen carefully in front of people who know what the careful word is hiding.

"Removal is the usual course," Hesketh said.

"It is one of them," I said. "The other is maintenance. This parish has maintained her for forty years. If a parish maintains a person for forty years and then stops, the person does not fly away. The person is still in the cottage, and the cottage is still in the turning, and the bread is still wanted on the Monday. You are not choosing between a lawful course and an unlawful one. You are choosing between paying and not paying, and calling it law because the calling of it is free."

"That is a speech," Hesketh said. "I asked for figures."

"You have my figures. They are yours. I have only put them in the order they happened."

***

The vote was four to three, with the vicar abstaining, and with Hesketh not voting at all, because he was not a ratepayer of the parish and a man who does not pay the rate is not in the meeting when a thing is put to it. The three against were old Ruddock and the two shopkeepers. The four for were the farmers, all four of them, and there was no fifth, because there was no fifth to be had, and Hesketh had the purest position in the room and the least to lose.

***

I went out by the church instead of the vestry door, because the vestry door opened into the yard where the men were standing about, and I did not want to be looked at while I got my coat on.

He was at the gate.

The shoulders of his coat had gone dark with rain, and he had his hat in both hands with the water running off the brim, and he was a heavy man in a coat that had been good when it was bought for somebody else.

"Mrs. Rooke."

"Bell. I am at the back of the hill." He said it as a man says a thing he has practised. "I was at the back of the room."

"You were at the back of the room."

"I am not on the vestry. My landlord pays the rate, and a man who does not pay the rate stands at the back and holds his hat, and when they put a thing to the meeting I am not in it."

"My father stood in that same place before me, with that same hat. He knew about the money. He knew it the way a man knows the weight of his own pocket. He told me the once, and he said, there is a woman in the turning and the parish keeps her and it is not in the book, and if you are ever asked in that room you do not know."

"Why did he tell you, then?"

"Because a man has to tell somebody." He wiped his mouth with his wrist. "The telling and the not-saying were the same evening's work. He did the one so that he could go on doing the other."

"Will you say it?" I said. "In the room. Not to me."

"No."

"Because of the landlord."

"Because to say it in that room is to say that we knew. A man who knew all that time and said nothing is not a witness. He is a party to it, and a party is the one thing a vestry knows how to vote about. They cannot vote away a woman in a cottage. They can vote away a man who admits he knew."

"Then they have you where they want you."

"They have had me there since my father died. I have not minded it. I mind it today." He looked at the gate and not at me. "You said a thing in there about paying and not paying and calling it law."

"I said a great many things."

"You said the calling of it is free." He nodded, the way a man nods at a sum he has just been shown. "I have been in that room every year of my life and nobody ever put it in the order it happened. I will think about it. It is all I can offer."

"That is more than the room gave me."

"The room gave you nothing. The room is not built to give. It is built to hold what is already there."

He put his hat on and went down the lane with his shoulders up, and the rain took him at the bottom of the hill. I went back through the gate and stood under the wall of the church.

Afterwards, in the wet yard, Isaac Thwaite came out with the minute book under his coat and stood beside me as if he were looking at the weather.

"The chest is in the vestry," he said, quietly, to the weather. "The lock is old. It opens with the key that is in the cupboard, and the cupboard is not locked, and I am the clerk, and a clerk who is asked by a person with a legitimate interest to see a document of the parish is not doing anything wrong."

"Am I a person with a legitimate interest?"

"You are the person who found out what was in the fifteenth line," he said. "In this parish that is a legitimacy of its own, and it is not one the vestry can vote away." He turned and looked at me, and he had his grandfather's stoop and none of his grandfather's comfort. "Come at six. The church will be open for the evening reading and nobody counts who comes in. I will leave the chest key on the ledge behind the door, and I will not be here, and if you are asked, you found the chest open, which is true, because I will have opened it."

"That is a lie with a true bottom to it."

"Most of the useful ones are," Isaac said, and went back into the rain.

---

## Three · The Bundle With the Ribbon

The church at six was cold in the way that stone is cold, which is not the cold of weather but the cold of a thing that has never once been warm and does not intend to start. The evening reading was three old women and a boy at the back, and the vicar read from the Psalms in a voice that had been doing it for thirty years, and nobody looked at me, and I went along the wall to the vestry door with my hand on the stone the whole way, because the floor was uneven and because a hand on stone is a hand that is doing something.

The key was on the ledge behind the door. The chest was under the window, and it was older than the room, and the lock was the kind that a man with a knife could open in a minute and a man with a key could open in an hour, because the wards were worn and the key had to be persuaded.

I persuaded it. The lid came up and the smell came out, which is the smell of every parish chest in England: dust, and mouse, and the particular sweetness of paper that has been damp and dried and damped again, which is not a bad smell and is not a good one and is the smell of a thing that has been kept past the point of use.

The chest was in three layers. The bottom was the big books, the registers, lying flat and strapped. The middle was the account books, standing on their tails. The top was loose papers, folded and docketed, tied in bundles with tape that had perished, and one bundle that had a ribbon on it.

***

The lid had no stay and wanted to come down, so I propped it with the brass candlestick off the table, laid on its side with the dish of it under the lid.

The loose papers I took out in handsful and laid on the table in the order they came, because a chest is filled from the top down and the last thing put in is the first thing a person meets.

The first was a roll. It had been rolled tight and tied with tape, and the tape had perished and came away in my fingers, and when I opened it the paper went flat with a small crackling, the way a thing does that has been rolled a long time and has decided to be flat again.

It was a register of apprentices. The heading was printed and the entries were written, and the year at the top was 1792, and the stamps were in the margin where the stamp office had put them, and there were more names in it than I had looked for, which is the thing about a parish: it is always bigger in the book than it is from the road.

I read down the names. A blacksmith took a boy. A wheelwright took a boy. A man described as a grocer took a boy, and the boy's father made his mark at the foot. In the last entry the master's name had been written and then struck through with a single line, and the same hand had written another name beside it, and there was no note to say why, and the parish had gone on stamping the page.

I put the roll down and took up the next thing.

It was a stack of removal orders, or the part of them that a parish keeps, which is the counterfoil: the narrow strip left behind when the order goes out into the world. They had been kept in a fold of brown paper, and every one of them had the name of the person to be removed and the parish they were to be removed to and the day of the order, written in the blanks in a hurrying hand.

Not one of them had been served.

I know how a served order comes back. It comes back with the constable's note on the back, and the day of delivery, and sometimes the mark of the person delivered. These had nothing on the back. They were flat and they were clean, and they had been put away in a fold of paper by somebody who made them out and then thought better of it, and had done that so often that the fold of paper had become a place where things went.

I held one up to the window. The light came through and showed the watermark, and the watermark was the same in all of them, which meant they had been bought in a batch by a parish that expected to use them.

Then the tin box.

It was the kind of box tea comes in, with a lid that sat inside the rim, and it had been used for something else for so long that it smelled of metal and not of tea. Inside it was a seal.

The seal was brass with a wooden handle, and the handle had split along the grain and been bound with wire by somebody who would not pay a man to turn a new one. The face of it was worn at the edge, where a seal wears, from being pressed down more times than the man who cut it could have thought about.

I carried it to the table and read the letters. They were cut the wrong way round, because that is how a seal is cut, and reading them was like reading a thing in a glass.

There was a standish on the vestry table with ink in it that had not quite dried, the vicar being a man who wrote his notices in the vestry and left the lid off. I took a scrap from the litter on the shelf, a torn corner of an account with figures on the back, and I breathed on the face of the seal the way you do, and pressed it into the ink, and then pressed it down on the blank side of the scrap and held it with the flat of my hand.

Then I lifted it.

ELDNIT.

It came out the wrong way round. The letters stood in a row with the T at the wrong end, and the print sat a little crooked, because the hand that held it was mine.

I looked at that for a while.

This is the mark the parish put on everything it ever did. On the orders that were never served. On the apprentices' papers. On the certificates that let a man walk from one parish to another without being moved on. A seal is the parish's word, cut backwards by a man in a workshop in another town, and every paper that came out of this chest with the mark on it came out the way it came out on my scrap: the same letters, in the wrong order, facing the other way.

I put the seal back in the box and shut the lid, and I folded the scrap and put it in my pocket, and my fingers were inky, and I wiped them on the handkerchief I keep for that and for nothing else.

The bundle with the ribbon was still where it had been, at the side of the loose papers, and I had put my hand on everything else in that chest before I put it on that one, which is not a thing I decided to do.

The ribbon was blue and it had been tied by a person who tied ribbons for a living, in a bow with the ends cut even.

I took the bundle out and put it on the table and sat down and untied the bow, which came apart in my hands the way old ribbon does, in one movement and then a small collapse.

There were eleven papers in it. I laid them out in the order they had been in the bundle, because the order a person keeps papers in is the order they think in, and I have never yet been wrong about a bundle.

The first was a copy of the removal order.

*To the Churchwardens and Overseers of the Poor of the parish of Tindle in the county aforesaid, and to the constable of the same.*

*Whereas complaint hath been made unto us, two of His Majesty's Justices of the Peace, that one Tace, a single woman, hath come to inhabit in the said parish of Tindle, not having gained a legal settlement there, nor produced a certificate, we do therefore require you forthwith to remove and convey the said Tace from the said parish of Tindle unto the parish of her legal settlement, if the same can be known, or otherwise unto the place of her last legal settlement, and deliver her to the churchwardens and overseers there.*

*Given under our hands the twelfth day of March in the thirty-third year of the reign of our Sovereign Lord George the Third.*

Two names at the bottom. One was a justice, *Thos. Ellwood*. The other was written in a hand that was younger and faster and not a justice's hand at all, and it said *E. Callard, for the clerk of the peace*.

I sat and looked at that line for a long while.

A removal order is signed by two justices. That is the whole of its authority: two men who hold the King's commission, acting together, upon complaint. There is no order in England that one man can make and another man can write, because if the writing were enough, every clerk in the kingdom would be a magistrate.

*For the clerk of the peace.* That is not a signature. That is a note of transmission. It says: I am not the justice, I am the hand that carried the paper, and I have written here because the second justice did not come.

The second paper was the examination.

*The said Tace saith that she was born in the parish of Nethercote in the county aforesaid, and that she never was bound apprentice, and that she never was hired for a year, and that she hath no child living.*

No child living.

I put my hand flat on the paper and let it lie there, and the paper was cold, and the cold was the cold of a thing that had been told once and written down and never asked again.

The third paper was the register entry: the copy of the page, no, not a copy. It was the page. It had been cut out of the register with a knife along the gutter, and the cut was old and the paper had curled at the edge, and the page was the page of 1793 with a line near the bottom where a name had been taken out, not inked over, not crossed, but taken out with a blade and then sanded, so that the surface was furred and pale, and the name beneath it was gone into the thickness of the paper.

And on the back of the page, in a hand different again, small and dry and neat: *It is entirely lawful.*

I had seen those words before. I had seen them in the autumn of the year before, in a copy of the order book, and I had spent a winter proving that the words were true, and had proved it, and had found that the truth of them was the whole of the harm.

The fourth paper was the appeal.

It was a single skin, folded in three, and the fold was cracked, and I opened it slowly because a cracked fold will break if you are in a hurry.

*And the said churchwardens and overseers having appealed against the said order to the next General Quarter Sessions of the Peace to be holden for the said county, and having entered into a recognizance in the sum of twenty pounds with sureties for the prosecution of the said appeal, the execution of the said order is hereby suspended until the said appeal shall be heard and determined.*

The appellants were named in the same breath as the men the order had been written to, and that is the part of this paper that nobody believes when I tell them. A removal order is a weapon, and a parish that has asked for a weapon does not usually appeal against being handed one. But this order named no parish at the far end. It said *the parish of her legal settlement, if the same can be known*, and nobody had known, or nobody had written down that they knew, and so there was no parish at the far end to be aggrieved and none to appeal. The only party who could appeal was the parish that had obtained the order. Tindle had appealed against Tindle.

Twenty pounds. Two sureties. The recognizance would be at the sessions, but the names were written at the foot of the suspension as a note, and there were two, and one was smudged, and the other was clear.

*E. Callard.*

Under it, in the same young fast hand, a line that had been added later, because the ink was a different colour and the pen had been cut fresh:

*The appellant does not appear.*

***

I read that line four times.

The appellant does not appear. That is the clerk's note at the sessions, written when a case is called and nobody stands up. It means the appeal was entered and the twenty pounds were put down and the sureties signed, and then on the day, nobody came.

And when nobody comes, the sessions strike the appeal out, and when the appeal is struck out, the suspension falls, and when the suspension falls, the order stands.

Except that the order had not been executed. For forty years the order had stood and had not been executed, and the parish had paid eight pounds a year, and the paying had been entered as *the turning*, and there had never been a second attempt to remove her.

I sat back in the chair and the chair creaked and one of the old women at the back of the church coughed, and I looked at the eleven papers laid out in the order they had been kept in.

***

Behind me the air moved and the draught went up the back of my neck.

I went along the row of them the way a woman goes along a hedge, taking each one up at its corners and turning it over and setting it down again, and I did it with the tips of my fingers, because a paper that has lain in a chest for forty years will part at the fold if you take it up by the middle.

The ribbon lay where it had dropped. I put it under the tin box so that it would not go on the floor.

The ones I had read I went past. The rest I turned over and read the backs of, because a paper says what it is on the back before it says anything on the front, and a docket is written by the person who put it away and not by the person who made it.

The next had been folded down and then folded back the other way, so that the creases crossed, and the crossing had rubbed through, and the hole had been mended on the dorse with a small piece of thin paper and paste, and the paste had gone brown and had come up at the corners.

Another had been through water. There was a tide line across the foot of it in a long brown curve and the ink of the last lines had run into the tide line and dried there in a smudge, and the sheet had cockled along the edge and had never gone flat again, and the writing on that part of it sat in a hollow.

Another had a seal on it, red wax, pressed off centre, with the print of a thumb in the soft of it where the hand had held it down too long, and the wax had gone grey with dust and the dust was down in the letters.

Another had been wormed. The holes ran in a line across the middle of the sheet, small and round and dark at the rim, and they had taken the middle out of a word, and there was no telling what the word had been, and the line would not say what it meant without it.

Another had a corner gone where a mouse had cut it for a nest, and the bite was scalloped and the paper below it was stained.

The ink was brown on the old ones and black on the ones written after, and one had been written in an ink that had eaten into the sheet and come through on the other side, so that the dorse carried the writing backwards and faint, the way a blot carries the hand that made it.

"Read me that," I said, and held one up over my shoulder without turning round.

He came as far as the end of the table and took it out of my hand and carried it to the window and held it up with the light on it.

"*The twelfth day of March*," Edward said. "It is the dorse of the order. Somebody has written the date out again on the back, and not in the hand that wrote the front."

"Is it the same date."

"It is the same date."

He brought it back and put it down in its place in the row, and the place was the place it had been in when I took it up, and he went back to the door and stood there with his hands behind him, and he did not ask me what I was looking for, which is the only thing about him I have ever been able to put a name to.

***

"Say it," Edward said from the door.

I did not turn round. I had heard him come in, because he had come in the way he came into rooms, which was without any noise and then all at once, like a man stepping out of a fog.

"The appeal was never tried," I said. "It was entered, and the sureties were found, and the suspension was granted, and then nobody appeared, and the sessions struck it out."

"Then the order stands."

"The order stands and has always stood. For forty years this parish has had a lawful order in its chest to remove that woman, and has not done it, and has paid for her instead." I turned round then. "Edward, that is not a fraud. That is the opposite of a fraud. Somebody in this parish was given a power and put it down and never picked it up again. And the somebody was a girl of about nineteen, because no clerk's clerk writes *for the clerk of the peace* in a hand like that and signs his own name to it."

Edward came to the table and stood looking down at the eleven papers.

"E. Callard," he said.

"You know the name."

"I know a name like it," he said slowly. "There was a Callard in the office at Ely when I was first articled. A copying clerk. He was old and he had a cough and he copied deeds for a shilling a skin, and he had a thing he used to say when the young men complained about the work." He put his finger on the paper, not touching it, an inch above. "He used to say, *my mother was a clerk before me and she had better handwriting than any of you.* We thought it was a joke. We were nineteen. Everything is a joke when you are nineteen and in a warm office."

"It was not a joke."

"No," Edward said. "It was not a joke, and the man is dead, and his mother is not, because his mother wrote this in 1793 and a girl of nineteen wrote it, and a girl of nineteen is sixty now, if she lives, and a woman who has kept one house for forty years has usually lived."

He straightened up.

"There is a way to know," he said. "The recognizance is in the sessions rolls. The rolls will say who stood surety, and the surety's place of abode is written in the margin, because a surety has to be found, and a man cannot be found who has no abode. It is the one paper in England that always tells you where a person lives."

"And the rolls are at Ely."

"The rolls are at Ely," he said, "and I have not been back there since I was twenty-two years old, and I have a great many reasons for not going back, and one of them is standing in front of me holding a piece of a parish register with a hole in it."

I folded the papers back into their order and tied the ribbon, and my hands were steady, which is a thing I have learned to notice about myself and not to trust.

"Then we will go to Ely," I said. "And on the way you will tell me about your reasons, and I will not ask you any of them, and you will tell me anyway, because you have been carrying them since before I met you and a man who carries a thing for that long will put it down in front of the first person who does not reach for it."

---

## Four · The Rolls at Ely

We went on the Monday, and it took two days, and the second day was the longest because the first day had been mostly silence and the second day had to be mostly talking, or the silence would have set, the way a jelly sets, and then we would have had to live in it.

Edward began it at the turnpike, of all places, with the gate going up and the man taking his sixpence and the horse standing while it was taken.

"My father bought my articles," he said. "You know that."

"I know that."

"He bought them because he had bought everything else. He bought the house, and he bought the pew, and he bought the place at the assizes where the clerks sit, and he bought the right to be buried inside the church instead of outside it, and he did all of it out of the profits of a trade he never once described to me in a way that made sense." He took the reins up. "And when I was nineteen I went to him and I asked him what he wanted me to do with my life. That was the question. I had rehearsed it. I had it in the plainest words I could find, because I had learned by then that plain words were the only ones my father could not turn into a compliment."

"And what did he say?"

"He said, *I have already arranged it.*"

The gate came down behind us. The road ran straight for a mile, the way the Ely roads do, and the flat country went away on both sides in fields that had nothing in them yet but water.

"And you never asked again," I said.

"I never asked again. And I have been in rooms for twenty-six years where men asked me things, and I answered them, and every answer I gave was a thing my father had arranged. It is a particular kind of hell, and it is a quiet one, and it does not look like hell from the outside, it looks like a man with good handwriting who is always at his desk."

"Then why are we going to Ely?"

"Because the rolls are the one place in England where a thing is written down and cannot be arranged afterwards," he said. "A roll is a skin sewn to the last skin and turned on a rod. You cannot take a skin out of a roll without the seam showing. My father could buy anything in that town except a seam."

***

The clerk of the peace's office at Ely was in a stone building at the back of the shire hall, and it smelled of the same dust as the Tindle chest, and the man in it was young and had the relieved face of a person who has been left in charge during the vacation.

"General Quarter Sessions, 1794," Edward said. "The Easter sessions if you have them, and the Michaelmas if you do not."

"May I ask the purpose, sir?"

"You may ask," Edward said, "and I will tell you that it is a question of a recognizance, and that I am a clerk of the audit office at Collow, and that if you wish to write to Collow to confirm it you may do so at your leisure and I will wait, but I would rather you fetched the roll while you wrote."

The young man fetched the roll.

***

While he was gone the inner door opened and an old man came out with a pot of paste in his hand.

He was thin the way old clerks go thin, all wrists and collar, and he wore his spectacles pushed up on his forehead, and he carried the paste out from his body as though it were something he had been asked to hold and did not trust.

He stopped at the end of the table and looked at Edward, and then he set the pot down and wiped his fingers on a cloth that was stiff with paste.

"You are Rooke's son."

Edward turned his head. "I am."

"I thought so at the door. You have his walk. He walked from the hip." The old man said it pleasantly, the way a man says a thing about the weather. "I copied for your father. Not the deeds. The other thing. The commission."

"The deputation."

"The deputation, and the commission under it, and the enrolment, and an affidavit that went with the enrolment and was not required by the Act and was required by your father." He put his spectacles down on his nose, looked at Edward through them, and pushed them up again, as though the looking had been enough. "It came in a basket. That is what I remember of it. A man brought it in a basket with a note on the top, and the note said what was wanted, and the basket had the money in it, and I engrossed all of it in this room and I never once had your father's face before me."

Edward said nothing.

"He was a great man in this town," the old man said, and the courtesy in it was exact and complete, and there was something underneath it that I could not have named then and can name now. "A great man. He never came in. He sent."

"The basket."

"The basket." The old man nodded at the press behind him. "We keep them. Not the baskets. The copies. Everything that came in a basket is in the books, and everything in the books is in my hand, and my hand is not the man's hand." He said it without any weight at all, the way a man says the time. "That is the office, sir. We are the hand. Somebody else is the intention."

Edward put his own hand flat on the table.

I have seen him do it all my married life and I had never looked at it before. He puts the hand down with the fingers together and the thumb along the edge of whatever it is, and holds it there while he thinks, and it is the hand of a man who was taught to keep his hands still in a room where other men were losing their tempers.

"Your father's commission," the old man said, "was made out in the name of a gentleman who never came to this office either. That is not unusual. Most of the commissions in the county were made out in the names of gentlemen who never came. The clerk does the work and the gentleman has the authority, and the authority is a thing that can be bought in a basket with a note on the top of it, and nobody in England thinks the worse of it, and I have never been able to decide whether that is because it is lawful or because it is convenient."

"It is lawful," Edward said.

"It is lawful," the old man agreed. "That is the trouble with it."

He went to the press and brought out a deed with a fold in it and laid it on the table and smoothed it with the flat of his fingers.

"Here is your father's hand at the foot of it. I copied this out for the enrolment and I have kept the copy by me, because the hand is a good hand and I am an old man and old men keep things." He put his finger on the signature, and then took it off and tapped the book at his elbow. "And this is the day book. Every man who takes a roll out of this office signs it. I did not make the rule and I have never been able to get anybody to break it."

He pushed the book round and set the pen on it and stood back with his hands folded.

Edward dipped the pen and signed. The nib went over the paper and the ink lay black on it, and he blotted it with the rocker and shut the book and pushed it back, and the old man opened it again at the place and looked at the signature, and then he turned the deed round and laid it beside it.

The signatures lay side by side on the table.

I looked at them. I am not a clerk and I have no eye for hands, and I could not have told anybody which was which, and I did not need to, because the old man was already telling me with his spectacles and his silence.

"Which is which," Edward said.

The old man looked at him over the rims.

"I have been in this office a great many years," he said, "and I would not swear to it."

Edward did not answer him. He put his hand out and drew the day book towards him and looked at his own name in it, and then he squared the deed to the edge of the table, which is how he ends an argument with a paper. Then he took his penknife out and opened it and looked at the point, and the point was good, and he shut it and put it away.

"There is a stone in the north aisle," the old man said, "with your father's name on it and a text, and the cutting is very good. I go past it on my way to the vestry and I have never once stopped." He took up his paste pot. "The roll is coming. It is heavy. Mind the rod at the end, it has a burr on it."

He went back through the inner door and shut it, and the latch fell with a click and then another click, which is the way an old latch falls when the wood has gone soft around it.

It came in on a trolley, because a sessions roll of that size does not come in a man's arms. It was seven skins sewn end to end and rolled on a rod, and the skins were the colour of weak tea and each one was written in a different hand, and the hands got worse as the roll went on, because the clerk wrote the first skin fresh in the morning and the last skin tired at night.

"Michaelmas 1794," the young man said. "The recognizances are at the end, after the convictions."

We turned it. It took the three of us, one at each end and one in the middle, and the rolling made a sound like a cart on gravel.

"Here," Edward said.

The entry was four lines and a note.

*Tindle. Appeal against removal. The appellants not appearing, the recognizance is estreated. Order confirmed.*

And beneath it, in a smaller hand, in the margin, where a clerk writes the thing he is not supposed to write but cannot help writing:

*Surety: Esther Callard, of Barrowfield, spinster. Twenty pounds. Paid into the hands of the sheriff, Michaelmas.*

March of ninety-three to Michaelmas of ninety-four. Eighteen months of suspension out of an appeal that cost nothing but the writing, and every one of those months was a month in which she was not put on the road.

Twenty pounds.

I stood with my hand on the roll and the roll was cold and my hand made a warm place on it, and the warm place faded while I stood there.

"Twenty pounds," I said. "What is twenty pounds to a spinster in Barrowfield in 1794?"

"Everything," Edward said. "It is a year and a half of a copying clerk's wages, and it is more than a dressmaker sees in three years, and it is exactly the sum a woman might have if her father left her a little and she never married and she put it in the bank at five per cent and never touched it."

"And it was estreated."

"It was forfeit to the King, because the appellants did not appear. That is what a recognizance is. It is a promise with money under it, and the money is not the appellant's, it is the surety's, and if the appellant does not come, the surety pays for the not-coming." He looked at me. "She paid for the parish's absence."

I sat down on the stool by the wall, which was not a thing I had been invited to do.

"Say the rest of it," I said.

"The rest of it is that the parish then did not execute the order. They had a confirmed order, and a confirmed order is a live order, and they did not execute it, and they began to pay eight pounds a year, and they have paid it for forty years." He put his finger on the margin. "They let her lose twenty pounds to buy time, and then they used the time to do the thing she had wanted, and they never told her, and they never gave her back a penny of it."

"Because the money went to the King."

"The money went to the King, and could not come back, and a parish that has cost a woman twenty pounds to save a woman's life will not afterwards put its hand in its pocket and say, here is your twenty pounds, we are sorry we did not turn up. It is easier to pay eight pounds a year for forty years than to say that sentence once."

***

For a copy we had to wait.

The young clerk went out with a key ring in his hand and came back without it and went out again, and a door opened and shut somewhere below, and all the while the room smelled of smoke, which came from a stove down the passage that had blacked the wall over the press, and under the smoke the smell of the skins themselves.

The presses went up to the ceiling on both sides of us. Each one had a letter branded into the rail and a row of figures painted under the letter, and the figures had been gone over with the brush and the older painting showed through, and the shelves inside were lettered again on the edge in a smaller brush.

The keeper came up from below with the key on his finger. He was a small man with ink on the cuff of his coat, and he did not ask who we were.

He unlocked the press and took out copy paper and a pen, and the pen was a quill with the barb cut off and the nib shaved to a point, and he laid it beside the roll on the table.

"You will write with that," he said. "Not with yours. A steel pen goes through a skin the way a needle goes through calico, and a hole in a roll is a hole for as long as there is a roll, and there is no mending of it that any man in this county would accept."

"I have not got a steel pen," Edward said.

"You will have a steel pen in your pocket before the year is out, and you will bring it into this room and you will take it out again." The keeper squared the paper with the edge of the table. "You will not lean over the skin. You will not put your sleeve on it. You will not rest your wrist on it to steady the hand, because the grease out of a wrist goes into parchment and comes up in a brown spot after a while, and a brown spot in a roll is a brown spot for good."

Then he turned his face to me.

"And you will not take the pen at all, madam, if you will forgive me. A copy in a woman's hand is a copy I have to go over again, and I am an old man with a great deal to go over."

"What is it you have to go over," Edward said.

"Everything that has ever been taken out of this room," the keeper said, "against the thing it was taken from."

Edward did not answer him. He took the quill up between his finger and thumb and turned it to the window and looked at the point of it, and then he set the roll flat with his hands at the edges and I held the candlestick up on the far side, and the light lay along the skin at a slant so that the writing came up out of it with shadows in the letters.

He wrote with his back straight and his wrist clear of the parchment, and the nib went with a small dry sound, the sound of a pen on a thing that has been dried hard, and when he had finished he sat back and did not blow on it.

The keeper took the copy and the roll and laid them side by side and put his finger under each line and went down the page.

"*Estreated*," he said. "That agrees. And you have put a full stop where the roll has none."

"There is a full stop in the sense of it."

"Take it out," he said. "A stop is a reading. The roll says what the sessions did and the copy says what the sessions did, and there is no room in a copy for what a man supposes they meant."

Edward took his penknife to the stop and scraped it, and the scrape left a place that would not take the ink again.

***

We went out into the close and stood in the sun, because after the roll room the sun was a thing that had to be stood in.

"There is one more thing," Edward said.

"There is always one more thing."

"Esther Callard, of Barrowfield, spinster, 1794. Barrowfield is a hundred and eleven miles from here and it is where you were born and it is where your mother is buried and it is where a woman kept a toll house on the far side of the ford." He let that sit. "You told me once that you passed that house on the way to the market every week of your childhood and that you never once saw anybody in the yard."

"I never saw anybody in the yard. I saw smoke."

"You saw smoke."

"Every week. There was always smoke, and there was never a person, and a house with smoke and no person is a house where somebody is being careful." I looked at him. "And you are going to tell me that the person being careful was a woman of sixty who paid twenty pounds in 1794 to keep a woman in a cottage in a parish a hundred and eleven miles away."

"I am going to tell you that she is the only person alive who was in that room," Edward said. "The justices are dead. The overseers are dead. Your grandfather is dead, and my father is dead, and every clerk who copied those papers is dead. There is one hand left that wrote any part of it, and it wrote *for the clerk of the peace* in a hand that a copying clerk in this office admired in 1809, and it is in a house on the far side of a ford, and it has been there, with smoke, all your life."

I looked at the shire hall and the elms and the flat bright country beyond.

"I have been to Barrowfield once since I married you," I said. "I have written to Judith and I have not gone. I have told myself that it is because of the winter and the roads and the price of the fare, and it is not any of those things, and you know it, and I know you know it."

"I know it."

"And you have not said so."

"No," Edward said. "I have not said so, because you would have thought I was asking you to go, and I was not. I was waiting to see whether you would go by yourself, and now the going has come to you in the shape of a bundle tied with a blue ribbon, and that is not the same as going by yourself, but it is not nothing either." He took his watch out and looked at it and put it back without reading it, which was a thing he did when he wanted a moment. "We can be at Barrowfield on Friday. I have written to the office for the week. I did it on Sunday, before you asked me, because I have been married to you for a year and four months and I have learned that the way to be useful to you is to be ready and to say nothing."

---

## Five · The Seven Households

There is a thing about a parish that nobody writes down, which is that every arrangement in it is held up by seven people who do not know they are seven.

The eight pounds was not paid by the parish. The parish had no eight pounds. The parish had a rate, and the rate was levied on the whole township, and the whole township was forty-one households, and forty-one households do not carry a woman for forty years. They quarrel. They move. They die. A thing carried by forty-one households would have been dropped in the first bad year and nobody would have been able to say who dropped it.

The eight pounds was carried by seven. It was collected with the rate and entered in the overseers' book as *the turning*, which is why Hesketh found it there and why a vestry can put it to a vote; and it was not the rate that paid it. Seven households paid it, into the rate, at the quarter, under a line that named nobody, and they had done it for forty years. I had found that out in the winter of the year before, and I had written it in the margin of my own copy of the rate-book, and I had not done anything about it because there had been nothing to do. It had been a fact about a parish, and facts about parishes are like facts about weather: you may write them down, and the weather goes on.

Now there was something to do, so I went round the seven.

***

Long Bank was first, because Mrs. Gow was first in everything, and because she had been first in 1821 when the seven had been made, and because a person who has been first for thirteen years expects to be consulted before anything happens and is dangerous if she is not.

She was in the yard with her sleeves rolled and her arms in a tub, and she did not stop washing when I came in, and she did not look up, and the not-looking was a courtesy, because a woman with her arms in a tub does not want to be looked at while she decides what to say.

"The vestry voted," she said.

"Six to three."

"Who were the three?"

"Ruddock and the two shopkeepers."

"Ruddock." She turned a sheet over in the water and pressed it down. "Ruddock is sixty-eight and he has been voting against things since he was thirty, and the things have happened anyway, and he has got used to being the man who was against it. He will not do anything. He has not done anything since 1819 and he is not going to start now because a woman from Collow has come into his yard."

"I have not been in his yard."

"You will be." She lifted the sheet and looked at it and put it back. "There are seven of us. You have come to ask whether we will carry it ourselves when the rate stops. That is the question. You may ask it, and I will answer it, but first you will hear what the seven are, because a woman who is going to ask seven people to do a thing should know what she is asking."

She wiped her hands on her apron and sat down on the bench and I sat on the other end of it.

"Long Bank," she said. "Me. Nine acres and a cow and a daughter who will have the nine acres when I am gone, and the daughter has married a man from Wendon who has opinions about money. I am good for a year and I will not swear to two.

"Ruddock's son at the High Field. Forty acres, barley, and a mortgage that his father does not know about. He will say no and he will say it in a way that makes him look prudent.

"The two Ellerbeck sisters at the mill cottage. Spinsters. They have an annuity of thirty pounds between them and they have never in their lives been asked for anything without being told what it was for, and they will give you the money and then they will lie awake.

"Hollis at the pump. He is seventy-six. He will give it and it will be a shilling a week out of a pension, and you will take it and you will feel the weight of it, and you will be right to feel it.

"Thwaite the clerk. Not the young one. The old one is dead. The young one has the office and thirty pounds a year and a grandfather's debts, and he will give you a form instead of money, because a form is what he has.

"The Sowerby estate, which is a farm and a widow and a steward in Hull who has never been in the parish and who will write a letter about it.

"And the seventh is the shop at the cross, which is Nichols, and Nichols will give a pound and tell nobody, and if you thank him in public he will never give again."

She stopped and looked at me.

"That is the seven," she said. "Six pounds and eleven shillings of it is certain if the rate stops and a woman goes round with a hat. It is not eight. It has never been eight. The rate made it eight because the rate took it whether a man liked it or not, and a hat does not."

"Then we will need eight from somewhere."

"Then you will need one pound nine from somewhere, and it will come out of you, because it always does, and I will tell you now what I told you in the winter: a woman who pays for a thing herself has bought the right to decide about it, and the moment you decide about her you are the parish with better manners."

***

The High Field was the second, and it went the way Mrs. Gow said. Ruddock's son was thirty-four and had his father's heavy face without his father's slowness, and he heard me out standing in the barn with a sack on his shoulder, which is a way of not sitting down with a person.

"Eight pounds," he said. "For a woman of seventy-one. And when she dies, another, and another."

"There has never been another. There has been one."

"There has been one because there has been a rate. Take the rate away and there will be a hat, and a hat is a thing that comes round every year, and every year it is a little harder to find the man who gave last time." He put the sack down. "Mrs. Rooke, I am not a hard man. My father says I am, and he says it because I will not do the things he did, and the things he did were done in a year when the wheat was six shillings and no one asked him whether he could afford it. I have a mortgage. I have a mortgage because my father's mortgage was paid off with money borrowed from a man in Ely, and that man wants eleven pounds a year for it, and eleven pounds a year is more than eight, and it will last longer than the woman."

"That is the whole of the argument," I said. "You are not refusing to pay for her. You are refusing to pay for her and the Ely man at once, and you have decided which of the two can be put off."

"I have decided which of the two can be put off," he said, and he had the grace to look away when he said it. "Come back in a year, when the mortgage is smaller, and ask me again, and I will say yes, and I will mean it."

"A woman of seventy-one does not have a year to lend you."

"No," he said. "That is the difficulty with the poor. They are always in a hurry."

***

The Ellerbeck sisters gave one pound nine, which was the sum and no more, because I had asked for the sum and not for a round figure, and a subscription asked for in round numbers is a subscription that grows a second subscription inside it. They asked eleven questions, and every question was a good one, and the last one was the one I had been waiting for.

"Who decided," said the elder, "that she should be fed rather than removed?"

"A girl of nineteen," I said. "In 1793."

"And where is she now?"

"At Barrowfield. Beyond the ford."

The two sisters looked at each other. It was a look of forty years, and it said a great deal in no time at all.

"There was a woman at the ford," the elder said. "When we were girls.
