My father would not take the road past her house after dark, and he never said why, and we were not allowed to ask, and a thing you are not allowed to ask about becomes a story by the time you are twelve. We used to say she was a witch. We were stupid girls."

"What did the other girls say she was?"

"The other girls said she was a woman who had been crossed in love." She smoothed her skirt. "It is what they always say. It saves them having to think about the money."

***

The Sowerby farm stood back from the road behind a hedge that had been laid badly, and the shippon door stood open, and I went in out of the rain into the smell of a cow and wet stone and the warm sour smell of milk that has stood.

The widow was milking.

She had the stool under her and the pail between her knees and her forehead against the flank, and the cow had its tail up and switched it across her back and she did not lift her head, and the milk went into the pail in thin streams with the sound milk makes when it strikes a pail that already has milk in it, which is a soft sound, and then the sound it makes when the pail is deeper, which is a hard one.

"You will be Mrs. Rooke," she said. "Mrs. Gow has told the parish you are going round."

"I am going round."

"I cannot stop. She has been standing a while and if I get up now she will hold her milk tomorrow, and a beast that holds it once will hold it always, and that is a beast spoiled by a visit." She stripped the last out with her thumb and forefinger and set the pail down on the flags and stood, and the cow turned its head and looked at her with great patience.

"What is it you want from this farm," she said.

"What I am wanting from all of them."

"Hull," she said. "Everything off this farm goes to Hull in a book at the quarter, and the steward reads the book, and a tenant who has put her hand to a subscription without asking Hull has put her hand to a thing she can be turned out for."

"Has he ever been here."

"He has never been here. He has never been in this parish. He writes." She wiped her hands down her apron, and the apron was wet at the front and there was milk in the seam of it. "I cannot give you money. I will give you what he does not count."

"What does he not count?"

"The milk. The cheese. A can set on the wall by the gate on the Monday, for whoever goes down the lane with the bread. He counts the coin. He has never once counted the milk, and if he ever does I will tell him the cow gave more than she did, and there is nobody in Hull can prove a cow."

"That is not money."

"It is food," she said. "A woman at the end of a lane wants food. You are going round with a hat because you have settled that she wants money, and money is what a person can add up at the end of it, and you are a clerk's wife and you have a list."

I had the list in my pocket and I did not take it out.

"Do not put the farm on it," she said.

"I will put nothing."

"Put nothing and it is nothing, and the vestry can say what it likes about the estate." She picked the pail up by the hoop. "The vestry cannot read a can. That is the good of a can, and I have thought about it a good deal at night."

She went out into the rain with the pail at her knee and the cow following her to the door and stopping at the door, and I went out after her and turned up the road with the water in my boots and nothing on my list for Sowerby, and a can coming down the lane on the Monday that no book in England would ever have a line for.

***

Hollis gave a shilling a week and would not be talked out of it, and Thwaite gave a form, and the Sowerby steward in Hull wrote a letter that took four days each way and said that the estate would consider the matter at Michaelmas and that in the meantime the tenant was not to commit the estate to any payment, and Nichols gave a pound and asked me not to say so.

***

The shop at the cross went differently from the way I had been told it would go.

It was a low room with a bell on a curl of iron over the door, and the bell went when I came in, and the man behind the counter did not look up, because he had a child at the counter and sugar on the scales.

He was weighing. The scales stood at the end of the counter with the beam at his eye and the brass pan on the left, and he had the sugar in a scoop cut out of a horn and was letting it into the pan in a thin stream and reading the pointer and not the sugar.

The child was a girl with a basin under her arm and a slip of paper in her hand with her mother's writing on it, and she stood on tiptoe, because everybody in a shop looks at the pan.

The pointer went past. He took a pinch of the sugar back out of the pan with his fingers and dropped it in the scoop, and the beam came down and hung, and he waited for it. A beam will settle if you leave it alone and will not settle if you breathe on it.

Then he tipped the sugar into a bag he had made out of a sheet of blue paper, and folded the top over and creased the fold with his thumbnail, and stood it on the counter.

"Tell your mother the sugar has gone up," he said, "and that I am sorry for it."

The girl took the bag and put it in the basin and went out with the bell.

Nichols wiped the counter with the side of his hand and looked at me for the first time.

"Mrs. Rooke."

"Mr. Nichols."

"You have come about the woman in the turning."

"I have."

He did not wait for the asking. He put his hand under the counter and brought out a twist of paper and pushed it across, and the twist was heavy and folded and folded again, and there was a coin inside it.

"It is a sovereign," he said. "It is not out of the shop. It is out of the drawer in the house, and the drawer in the house is mine, and that is all I have to say about it."

He put one finger on the twist. "Do not put my name to it."

I had the list in my pocket and I opened it on the counter, and the pencil Edward sharpens for me was in my other hand.

"Then what shall I put?"

"Nothing."

"A list with a gap in it is worse than no list."

"Put a friend, then." He turned the twist over with one finger. "Put a friend and let it go."

So I wrote it while he looked on, and turned the list round so that he could read it.

*a friend*

He looked at it for a while, the way he had looked at the pan.

"No," he said.

"No?"

"Put the initials." He took the twist up and set it down again, which was a thing his hands did without him. "A friend is what a man writes when he is ashamed of the giving. I am not ashamed of the giving. I am ashamed of the talking about it. Write the initials."

"Say them."

"N. N."

"And nothing else?"

"Nothing else. Anybody who knows this shop will know the initials. Anybody who does not is not entitled to."

So I rubbed out *a friend* with the india rubber and wrote *N. N.* in the gap, and the paper went grey where the rubber had been, and the initials sat in the grey.

He looked at that too, then nodded once and took a bag from the pile under the counter and blew it open and put the twist inside, and folded the top over and creased it with his thumbnail the way he had done for the child, and put it into my hand.

"Do not carry it through the street in your hand," he said. "A woman with a bag is doing her shopping."

The bell went when I opened the door, and the rain had stopped and the street was running with water and the light was going.

I came home at seven with the light going and my boots wet through and a list in my pocket that came to six pounds and eleven shillings if every promise held.

Edward was at the table with the lamp lit and the notebook shut in front of him and a second sheet of paper, ruled, on which he had written the seven names in order of the road.

"Six eleven," he said, before I had taken my coat off.

"Six eleven."

"And the rate is eight."

"The rate is eight, and the rate is also a machine that has been running for forty years, and a machine that has run for forty years does not stop because a vestry votes. It stops because somebody stops turning the handle. Hesketh will stop it at Michaelmas and then the overseers will not enter the eight pounds, and the eight pounds will not be paid, and nobody will have decided anything. That is how a parish kills a person. It does it by not entering a line."

Edward turned the ruled sheet round and pushed it across to me.

"Then we need one pound nine," he said. "And here is what I propose, and you will not like it."

I looked at the sheet. He had written the seven names and beside each the sum, and at the bottom, in his own hand, in the column where a man writes the total: *Rooke, Collow. One pound nine shillings. Standing order, quarterly.*

"Edward."

"It is my money. I earned it at the audit office and it is not my father's and no one arranged it."

"A man who pays for a thing owns it," I said. "You told me that yourself in the first year. You told me that a gift with a name on it is a purchase."

"I did tell you that, and I have thought about it since, and I have decided that it is true of gifts and not true of subscriptions." He put his hand flat on the paper. "A subscription is a thing a person joins. If I put my name at the bottom of a list with seven other names on it, I have not bought the woman. I have joined the seven. And if you would rather I did not, say so, and I will tear it up, and you will go and find one pound nine shillings in a hat, and you will find it, because you always find it, and it will cost you a month."

I sat down.

"Mrs. Gow said something in the yard," I said. "She said that a woman who pays for a thing herself has bought the right to decide about it, and that the moment I decide about her I am the parish with better manners."

"Mrs. Gow is a wise woman who has never in her life had a pound to spare and therefore has a theory about pounds." He looked at me. "You are not deciding about her. You are deciding whether to let a woman go hungry in October while you look for a hat. Those are not the same decision, and if you cannot tell them apart you will be no use to her at all."

I took the pen and put my name under his, and then I crossed out the word *standing* and wrote *quarterly, while she lives*, because a standing order is a promise made to a bank and a quarterly one is a promise made to a person, and I had learned by then that the difference between those two things is the whole of what a parish is.

---

## Six · The Bread on the Stone

The turning was a lane that left the Tindle road at a hawthorn and went down between two hedges to a cottage that had been built before the hedges and had been left behind by them. It was not a bad cottage. That was the thing I had not expected. A person who has spent a winter reading about a woman kept in a turning expects a hovel, because the mind builds a hovel for a woman with no name in a book, and the mind is lazy, and a hovel is the shape laziness takes.

It had a good roof. It had a chimney with smoke in it, thin and blue, the smoke of a small fire that is being kept in rather than a fire that is being enjoyed. It had a window with four panes and three of them whole. It had a stone by the door, and the stone was worn in the middle the way a stone is worn by a foot that has stood on it a great many times, and the wearing was at the front edge, which is where a person stands when they are looking up the lane.

***

I had left the Tindle road at the hawthorn with the bread in a cloth and the cloth knotted at the corners, and the knot sat in the palm of my hand, and I carried it on the left because a person wants the right hand free at a gate.

The hawthorn had been cut and cut again until it was a stump with a head on it, and the head was in leaf, and the gap beside it had been stopped with a hurdle, and the hurdle had been mended with a withe and the withe had gone.

The gate had a hinge gone, so that the hanging end sat down in its socket and the other end had to be lifted, and the lifting of it took both hands, and I put the bread down on the stone at the foot of the gate post while I did it.

The stone was square and had been set there to keep the post out of the wet, and it was green on the top, and the green came off on the cloth where the knot lay, and I rubbed at it with my thumb and rubbed it in worse, and left it.

The lane below the gate had been cut into ruts by carts that had no business in it, and the ruts had water standing in them the length of a cart, and I walked the ridge between them with my boots on the grass down the middle, and the grass was the only dry thing there was.

A dog came along the far side of the hedge while I was at the gate.

It was a dog of no family, the colour of the road, and it put its nose through the gap in the hedge and drew the air in over its teeth the way a dog does when it has a smell it wants to know about, and it did not bark, and it went on along the hedge keeping pace with me, and whenever I turned my head for it, there it was at the same distance, on the other side of the leaves.

It came as far as the end of the hedge and stopped there and sat down with its back to me, having settled that the walk was over without anybody asking it.

The field on the north side ran up to a spinney, and a man was at the hedge at the far end of it with a billhook.

He had been laying a gap. He stopped when I came in sight and stood with the billhook down and both hands on the top of the hedge, and he did not call out and he did not go back to the work, and he kept his face towards me the whole way down the headland, and the keeping of it was not curiosity about me. It was a man putting a field between himself and a thing he knows is going to happen and does not want to be nearer to.

I lifted a hand to him. He did not lift his.

Then the hedge shut and the dog did not come after me, and I was in the lane with the bread back in my left hand, and the warmth of it was coming through the cloth into my palm and it was the warmest thing about me.

***

I stood at the top of the lane for a long while.

I had come with the bread. That was the arrangement I had made with Mrs. Gow, and I had made it badly, because I had made it in the yard with my hands still cold from the pump and had not thought about what it would mean to be the one who carried it.

"The girl takes it Mondays," Mrs. Gow had said. "She leaves it on the stone and does not knock. The knocking was stopped before I came to it. There was a time when the one who took it would come to the door and say thank you, and then there was a time when she would come to the door and not say it, and then there was a time when she did not come to the door, and the bread was on the stone in the morning and gone by the afternoon, and that is the way it has been since before the girl was born."

"Why did she stop coming to the door?"

"How would I know? I have never spoken to her. Nobody in this parish has spoken to her in my lifetime, and I have been at Long Bank since I was nineteen." She had wrung out the sheet then and said, in a different voice, "It is not a hard thing to leave bread on a stone. It is a hard thing to be the one who is seen leaving it."

So I carried the bread down the lane, and I was seen, because there was a boy on the hedge at the top of the field with a stick and a dog, and the boy stopped doing whatever he was doing with the stick when he saw me, and the stopping was a report that would be all over the parish by supper.

The bread was a two-pound loaf and it was still warm through the cloth, and the warmth came into my hands and stayed there, and my hands were the warmest thing about me by the time I reached the stone.

I put the loaf down. I did not knock.

I stood up and looked at the door, and the door was oak and had been painted once, and the paint was the grey of a thing that has been rained on for forty years, and there was a latch and the latch was worn bright, and the bright was the only bright thing in the whole of the lane.

And then the door opened.

Not wide. A hand's width, the way my mother had opened her window a hand's width in the year before she died, and I had a moment of such perfect and idiotic recognition that I heard myself breathe in.

An old woman looked out.

She was small. That was the first thing, and I was ashamed of how much it mattered, because I had built her in my mind out of the papers and the papers were all about a woman who had been moved from parish to parish and had cost money and had been the subject of a lawful order, and a woman like that is a large woman in the mind, a woman with a strong back. She was not large. She was small and straight and she had a white cap that had been washed a great many times and a grey woollen thing over her shoulders, and her face was the face of a person who has spent forty years looking up a lane.

She looked at me. She looked at the bread. Then she looked at me again, and the second look was longer than the first, and it was not a frightened look and it was not a grateful one.

"Who are you," she said.

Her voice was low and it had the Tindle in it, the flat vowel, and it was not the voice of a person who had been silent for forty years. It was the voice of a person who had been saving it.

"Mrs. Rooke," I said. "Of Collow."

"I know what you are called. I asked who you are."

I stood in the lane with the sun on the stone and the loaf between us.

"I do not know how to answer that," I said.

"No," she said. "You would not."

And she shut the door. Not hard. She shut it the way a person shuts a door on a windy day, with her hand flat on it, and I heard the latch go down and then I heard nothing at all, and I stood there long enough for the sun to move off the stone.

***

I went back up the lane and past the boy and his dog, and the boy said, "She spoke to you," in the voice of a person reporting a fire.

"She did."

"Nobody hears her speak."

"Then you have heard her speak now," I said, "and you may tell the parish that she said three sentences and that all three of them were civil, and that if I hear of anybody in this parish saying otherwise I will come back and read their grandmother's washing list out in the market."

The boy considered this and decided it was a joke and laughed, and the dog barked at the laugh, and I walked on with my heart going like a bird in a box.

***

Edward was at the gate of the inn when I got back, and he had the look he had when he had been waiting a long time and had decided not to mention it.

"Well," he said.

"She opened the door."

He took his hat off and held it.

"She asked me who I was, and I said Mrs. Rooke of Collow, and she said she knew what I was called and asked who I was, and I said I did not know how to answer that, and she said no, you would not, and shut the door."

Edward stood with his hat in his hand.

"That is the best thing I have heard in a year," he said.

"It is a disaster."

"It is the opposite of a disaster. A woman who has not spoken to anybody in forty years asked you a question that you could not answer in four words." He put his hat on. "She is not simple, and she is not broken, and she is not grateful, and she is not afraid. She is a person who has been kept in a lane by a parish that never once asked her anything, and the first thing she did when a stranger put bread on her stone was to find out whether the stranger was a person or a message."

"A message."

"From the parish. From the vestry. From Hesketh. She has been a subject for forty years. She wanted to know whether you had come to do something to her." He opened the gate. "You said you did not know who you were. That is not the answer of a message. A message knows exactly who it is."

***

Edward took the horse round to the stable, and I went in under the arch into the yard, and there were women standing in it.

Several of them, in the sun against the wall, with the ordinary things in their hands that give a person a reason to stand in a yard: a basket, a can, a child's coat over an arm.

The parish had known I was going before I had known it myself. A boy on a hedge tells his mother, and his mother tells the woman at the pump, and by the time the bread is off the stone there is a version of the morning going round the parish that has more in it than the morning had.

The oldest of them held out a jar.

"For the stone," she said. "It is only damson. It is not a message."

I took the jar. The cloth over the top was tied with a piece of string that had been used for something else before.

"You went down the lane," she said.

"I went down the lane."

"And the door."

"The door opened."

Nobody moved. A parish learns not to look at itself in a yard.

"Did she take the bread in," said the woman with the can.

"She looked at it."

"She looked at it." The woman said it to the others and not to me. "She has not looked at anything in a long while."

"She looked at me as well. She asked me who I was."

The can went down on the stones, and it was put and not set down, and the noise of it was the loudest thing in the yard.

"She spoke."

"She spoke."

"To you."

"To me, and about me, and she was not confused, and she was not kind, and she was not afraid."

They took it the way a congregation takes a text that has gone on too long. The woman with the child's coat folded it smaller and then shook it out again.

"What did she say about us," she said.

"Nothing."

"Nothing at all."

"She said nothing about anybody. She asked who I was, and I gave her my husband's name, and she told me that a name is not an answer."

Somebody laughed, a short laugh, and put her hand up to her mouth and took it down again.

"That is her," she said. "That is the very sort of thing."

The woman with the coat looked at the trough. "My mother-in-law carried the bread before the girl did. She came home in the cold with her hands blue and said the woman at the end of the lane had thanked her at the door, and my mother-in-law sat down in the scullery and cried and would not say what for." She turned the coat over her arm. "She never went again. She said it was somebody else's turn, and she said it in a voice that meant it was nobody's turn."

"Somebody has gone every market morning since," I said.

"That is not the same as anybody going," the old woman said.

The woman with the can had not taken her eyes off me. "And she asked for nothing."

"She asked for nothing."

"Good," she said, and then, too quickly, "That is to say. It is easier."

"It is easier for the parish."

"It is easier for me," she said. "I am not going to stand in a yard and pretend to be the parish. I have a girl in my kitchen who costs me more than she earns, and a roof that wants doing, and every autumn of my life I have put the price of a jar of damson out of my head, and I would rather she asked for nothing than that she asked me for a thing I would have to refuse." She nodded at the jar in my hands. "There. Now you have it. That is what we are."

The old woman said, "Did she look well?"

The yard went quiet in a way that had nothing to do with the pump.

"She looked like a person," I said.

"That is not an answer."

"It is the only answer I have. She looked like a person who has been in a house for a long while and has kept the house. Her cap was clean. Her fire was in."

"Then she is being looked after."

"She is being fed," I said. "It is not the same, and you know it is not the same, and you came out into this yard to find out whether I had found it out too."

Nobody answered that.

"You are the woman who writes things down," she said.

"I am."

"Then do not write this down."

"Somebody will write it down," I said. "It has gone too far to be kept in a yard. The only question left is whether the person who writes it was standing in the yard when it was said."

"And if you write it, what will it say?"

"It will say that she asked me who I was and I could not answer her, and that the parish has been standing in a yard ever since with jars in its hands, waiting to hear whether it has been cruel."

The pump dripped on the stone.

The woman with the can picked it up. "I will not have it said that I came here to hear the news," she said. "I came because I could not sit in my own house."

They went out through the arch separately, the way people go out of a church, and the woman with the child's coat stopped at the arch and turned.

"Do not tell Mrs. Gow I came," she said.

"Why not?"

"Because she will say I am soft, and she will be right, and I would rather she said I was busy."

Then she went, and the yard was a yard again, with a pump and a trough and a jar of damson in my hands.

I stood there until the jar had warmed through.

***

That night I could not sleep, and at two in the morning I got up and went down to the parlour of the inn and sat in the dark with my mother's coat round my shoulders and the paper in my hand.

I had brought it. I had not meant to bring it, and I had put it in my pocket on the Thursday without deciding to, and it had been in the pocket of the coat for six days, and the fold had worn a little more at the corner, and the hole was the size of the top of my thumb.

*What I want is*

I sat with it and thought about a woman asking me who I was, and about the fact that I had answered with my husband's name, and about the further fact that when I had answered with my husband's name she had not accepted it, and had not been rude about not accepting it, and had simply waited, the way a person waits at a gate for the person inside to come out.

She had waited about four seconds. It had felt like a year.

And in those four seconds I had gone through, in order: my name, my father's name, my mother's name, the parish I was born in, the trade I had learned, the thing I could do that other people could not, and the thing I had done in Barrowfield in the winter of thirty-two that had put a woman's name back into a book. Every one of them was true. Every one of them was a description of a use. Not one of them was an answer to the question she had asked, and she had known it before I did, and she had let me find it out in my own time, which is the most expensive kind of kindness there is.

I put the paper on the table and put the pen beside it and did not take the pen up.

A woman who cannot answer a question in four seconds at a door will not answer it in an inn at two in the morning with a pen in her hand. The pen does not make a person braver. The pen only makes the not-answering permanent.

---

## Seven · The Notebook

He gave it to me on the Sunday, after the bread, in the parlour of the inn, with the window open because it was the first warm day of the year.

"I want you to read it," he said.

"I have read your notebook."

"You have read the outside of it and you have read the last page of it and you have read one entry in the middle that I showed you in the winter because I wanted you to know that I had written it." He put it on the table between us. "I want you to read all of it. From the first page. And I am going to sit here while you do it, which is the hardest part of the request, and if you would rather I went out, I will go out."

"Why now?"

"Because we are going to Barrowfield on Thursday, and a man who goes to Barrowfield with his wife should not have a book in his pocket that she has not read."

I looked at the notebook. It was a small quarto in brown calf, and the calf had been handled so much that the corners had gone round, and there was a stain on the front board in the shape of a thumb and a half.

"Read it to me," I said.

"That is not the same."

"No," I said. "It is better. A man reading his own book aloud cannot skip the parts he is ashamed of without making a noise about the skipping."

He looked at me for a moment. Then he opened it at the first page.

***

The first entry was in pencil and dated the fourth of November 1831.

*She asked Nunn why the toll house has two doors.*

"That is it?"

"That is the first one."

"That is not a question. That is a comment."

"It is a comment in the form of a question, which is what a question is when a person is not sure they are allowed to ask it." He turned a page. "I wrote it down because I had never heard anybody ask a man why his own house had two doors. He had lived in that house for thirty years and nobody had ever asked him, and he answered you for a quarter of an hour. He was delighted. He had been waiting thirty years to be asked."

He read on. The entries were not all the same length. Some were a line. Some were a paragraph. Some were not questions at all but a note of a thing I had said in a room and a note of what the room had done when I said it.

*She asked Bedloe what the register is for. He said it is for the parish. She said, what is the parish for.*

*She asked the bishop's man whether a form may be lawful and wrong. He did not answer. She waited eleven seconds. He answered.*

*She asked Sowerby how much he had been paid for a lie. He told her. She wrote it down. He watched her write it down and did not stop her and when she had finished he said, you are the first person who has ever wanted the number.*

He turned back a page.

*She asked the schoolmaster whether a child who can read the Bible can read a bill. He said the Bible is enough. She asked him to bring a boy who could read a bill. He brought nobody.*

"That is not what happened," I said.

"It is what was asked."

"It is what was asked with the answer taken out of it."

"The answer is on the page after." He turned it and held it round so that I could read, and the page after was in the same pencil and the same hand, and it said, *He brought nobody, and afterwards she asked him what he would need, and he said time and money, and she asked the vestry for the money and the time, and the vestry found the money.*

"Then it is a longer entry than the rest."

"It is a longer business."

I took the book out of his hands and read the entry again, and then the page after it again, and the entry and the page together were like a man described by his coat and then by his face.

"You have made me clever," I said.

"I have made you exact."

"You have made me clever and left out the part where I was frightened."

"You were not frightened. You were angry, and anger looks well on a page, and I did not want you to look well." He held out his hand for the book and I did not give it to him. "I wanted it to be true."

"Then put in the chair."

"What chair?"

"The chair I gave him. When he could not answer, I asked him something else that he could answer, and I asked it slowly, and I asked it in front of the same people, and he sat down on it and answered, and by the time he had finished answering there was not a person in that room who remembered the question before it. That is in the room. It is not in your book."

He looked at the page for a while, and then at me.

"That is the mercy," he said.

"It is the whole morning."

"It is the mercy, and it is not mine to write."

"You were not there."

"I was at the door."

"You were in Collow. You had the trap and the chest and a man coming about a horse."

"I was at the door," he said. "I came in with the coal man and I did not come into the room, because you were working, and a husband who comes into a room to be seen has come in to be seen. So I stood in the passage with my hat in my hands and I had all of it through the door, and I have never told you that until now, and I am telling you now because you have accused my book of leaving out a chair."

"You stood in a passage."

"I stood in a passage for the length of a school morning."

"And you wrote down the question and not the chair."

"I wrote down what was asked," he said. "I have never written down what it cost. If I had begun to write down what it cost I would have filled the book and you would have read a ledger of your own virtues, and you would have shut it and thought less of me for keeping it."

"That is not fair."

"No," he said. "It is exact, which is a different thing, and you taught me the difference, and you taught it to me in a vestry with a clerk taking notes."

I put the book down with my hand flat on the cover, the way I put my hand flat on papers when I am about to say something I have not said before.

"May I write in it," I said.

"No."

"Why not?"

"Because you will make it a kindness, and it was not a kindness. It was a woman giving a man somewhere to stand so that she could go on asking him things, and there is not a person alive who would call that kindness except the man himself, and he has never said so." He took the book and turned the page back. "You may write in the blank at the end. That is the only page in this book that is yours, and I have told you so, and I will not be moved from it."

"You are a very stubborn man."

"I am a man who has been married to a woman who argues for a living, and stubborn is what a man becomes when he stops running."

I laughed, which I had not meant to do, and the laugh came out of me sideways and made him look up.

"There," he said. "That is the only thing you have done today that was not an argument."

"Everything I do is an argument."

"Everything you do is a question with the question mark taken off." He put his thumb in at the page. "May I go on."

"Go on."

He read the next entry, and the next, and after a while they came at me the way rain comes at a window, with no space between, and I stopped trying to answer them and let him read.

*She asked the clerk why the book is kept in the chest and not on the table. He said the chest is dry. She asked him whether the people in the book are dry.*

*She asked the surgeon whether a woman who cannot sign her name can consent to an operation. He said it is not usual. She asked him what is done when it is not usual.*

*She asked the man at the lock how long the water has been let go at that time of day. He said since before him. She asked him who decided it, and he said nobody, and she asked him to say that again, and he said it again, slower, and then he stood in his own doorway for a while with his hand on the beam.*

I sat with that entry.

"That is the entry I remember," I said.

"You should. You made a man stand in his own doorway for the length of a lock filling."

"I did not make him. He made himself."

"You asked him a question he had answered all his life without thinking, and you asked it in a voice that made him think, and then you waited. The waiting is the part that does the work." He turned the page. "You have never asked a question and then filled the silence yourself. It is the only piece of tact you have, and I have sat in rooms while you did it to bishops."

"If you knew all of that," I said, "why did you write the entries the way a man writes an indictment."

"Because that is what it looked like from the door," he said, "and I have never been able to write what a thing was. I can only write what it looked like from where I stood. That is my trade and my limitation, and you have been reading it all morning and reading it as a record of yourself, and it is not that. It is a record of the room, and the room had you in it, and you were the largest thing in it."

He said it and went back to the book, and I sat with my hands in my lap and the fire going down, and neither of us got up to put anything on it.

***

He read on. The lamp had been set on the table while we were attending to other things, and the shade had a brown scorch up one side of it where somebody had turned the flame too high, and the light fell on the page and left our faces in the half dark of the room.

The entry was in pencil, and the spine of the book had gone slack at that place from being opened there.

*She asked the woman at the bakehouse in Cow Lane what becomes of the bread that is not sold by the close of the day. The woman said it goes to the pigs. She asked whether anybody had ever asked her that before. The woman said no. She asked her what she would do with it if it were hers to do with. The woman said she would give it away. Then she said it is not mine, and then she sat down on the flour bin with her hands in her apron.*

I put my finger on the page.

"There is no year on it."

"There is no year on it," Edward said, "and there is a year on every other one in the book."

"You have a year on every other one."

"I have a year on every one I could put a year to without guessing." He took the pencil out of his waistcoat. It was a stub, cut short with a knife, the wood whittled flat on two sides so that it would lie in a pocket. "The year is not the difficulty. The difficulty is that I could find it out. The bakehouse woman is in the rate book at Collow, and the rate book will tell me when she took the shop, and then I would know."

"Then you would have looked it up in a parish book and written it into yours," I said, "and the entry would say that a woman sat down on a flour bin in a year the parish knows about. That is not what happened. What happened is that a woman sat down on a flour bin with her hands in her apron because she had told somebody the truth about a thing that was not hers, and the year has nothing to do with it, and the parish has nothing to do with it, and I will not have you putting the parish into it."

He laid the pencil on the table beside the book and did not take it up again.

"A man keeping a book who leaves the year out is not keeping a book," he said. "He is keeping a story."

"Then keep a story. There is one in this house already, and it has not done either of us any harm."

The lamp smoked and the scorch on the shade went a shade darker, and neither of us got up to turn it down.

"That is the second time today you have accused me of tidying you," he said.

"That is the second time today you have deserved it."

His mouth went at one side. He kept his hand flat on the table and away from the pencil, the way a man keeps his hand off a thing he has decided not to take.

"I will leave it," he said. "Not because you are right. Because a year set beside that would stand beside it the way a sum stands beside a loaf, and the woman did not sit down on a sum."

"Put that in the book."

"No," he said, and turned the page.

***

By the eleventh entry I had stopped hearing the words and was hearing something else, which was the shape of my own life as it looks to a person who has been in the room.

"Edward."

"I am still reading."

"Edward. This is not a book of the questions I have asked. This is a book of the questions I have asked *other people*."

He stopped. He put his finger in the book to keep the place, which was a thing he did not do with books.

"Yes," he said.

"Every one of them. Every single one." I took the book out of his hands and turned the pages back and forward, and the pages went past like a fence going past a cart. "Bedloe. Sowerby. Nunn. The bishop's man. Judith. The clerk's grandson. Mrs. Gow. The woman at the barrow. The man at the lock." I turned another page. "There is not one in here that I asked you."

"There is one," he said.

"Where?"

"At the end."

I turned to the end.

The twenty-seventh entry was dated the twenty-ninth of March in the year before, and the hand was the careful hand he used when he had written something more than once in his head before writing it on the page.

*She asked me to put the book down and come to bed.*

I sat with it.

"That is not a question either," I said.

"It is the only one in the book that has an answer," Edward said, "and I have been carrying it for a year, and I have not written the answer, because the page under it is blank, and the blank is yours."

"Why is the blank mine?"

"Because you have asked twenty-seven questions in three years and every one of them was for somebody else, and a woman who has spent three years asking other people's questions has earned the right to write one of her own." He closed the book, gently, over my hand. "I have kept this book because I wanted to see whether you would ever ask a question that had an answer in it. That is the whole of why I kept it. It was not a record of your work. It was a wager."

"A wager on what."

"On whether a person can be taught to want something." He said it plainly, without softening it, which was the way he said the things that cost him most. "I was not sure it could be taught. I am still not sure. But I have watched you ask twenty-seven questions and I have watched what happens to the people you ask, and I have come to think that the asking is not the thing you do instead of wanting. I think it is the thing you do because you are not allowed to want, and if that is true, then the cure is not more asking."

"What is the cure?"

"Being asked," he said. "Once. By somebody who will wait for the answer."

***

I sat with the notebook in my lap for a long time. The window was open and there was a blackbird in the yard of the inn and the blackbird was doing the thing blackbirds do in April, which is to sing as if the whole of the world has been arranged for its convenience.

"Do you know what is on the paper in my drawer," I said.

"I know you keep a paper in the drawer."

"Do you know what it says?"

"I know what you wrote in the inn at Thorneley in the spring of last year, because I was in the room and you had your back to me and you wrote one line and stopped and folded it." He looked at me. "I did not read it. I have never read it. I have had it in my hands twice, when I moved the tin box, and both times I put it back."

"Why?"

"Because it is the only thing in this house that is yours and not mine."

"And you have never asked me what is on it."

"No," he said. "And I am not going to. And you should know that the not-asking has a reason, and the reason is not the one you think."

"Tell me the reason."

"The reason is that I am afraid of the answer," Edward said. "I am not a man who does not ask because asking would hurt you. I am a man who does not ask because I have a very good idea of what the answer would be and I do not want to hear it said in a room with me in it." He stood up and went to the window and stood with his back to me, and his shoulders were the shoulders of a man of forty-seven and not of forty-five, and I had not noticed the change until that moment. "You have been in this house for a year and four months and you have never once asked me for anything. Not a thing. Not a morning, not a road, not a word. And I have told myself that it is because you are careful, and it is not because you are careful. It is because you have decided that a woman who asks is a woman who owes, and you would rather owe nothing to anybody in the world, including me, including in bed."

He turned round.

"That is what I am afraid of," he said. "That is the whole of it. And now you have it, and you may do what you like with it."

I got up and went to him and put my hand flat on his chest, which was a thing I did, and he put his hand over mine, which was a thing he did, and we stood there for a while in the noise of the blackbird.

"Write in the blank," he said.

"I cannot."

"I know. I am not asking you to do it today. I am telling you that it is there, and that it does not go away, and that a blank in a book is not a debt. A debt comes due. A blank waits." He let go of my hand. "Come to bed. Tomorrow we pack for Barrowfield."

---

## Eight · The Third Letter

The third letter had come in October of the year before, and I had read it twice, and I had put it in the box with the other two, and I had not answered it.

I took the box down from the shelf on the Monday morning and put the three letters on the table in the order they had come.

The first was from Judith, in the summer of thirty-two, and it was four pages and it was about a wedding that had not happened yet.

The second was mine. *Bring it.* Two words, written in a hurry, on a half sheet, and the half sheet had been folded in three and the fold had gone brown.

The third was the one from beyond the bridge.

It was one skin, folded twice, and the wax was a plain wafer and not a seal, and the wafer had cracked in the opening. The hand was old and fine and upright, and the letters leaned, all of them, the same degree to the right, the way a field leans when the wind has been in one quarter for a hundred years.

I put it beside the copy I had made of the order.

The order was in a clerk's hand and the hand was young and fast and the loops were high, and the letters leaned to the right. The letter was in a hand that was old and slow and the loops were low, and the letters leaned to the right.

A hand changes in forty years. It gets slower and smaller and the loops come down and the pen is held further up. What does not change is the lean, because the lean is not a habit, it is the way the wrist is made.

"It is the same hand," Edward said, from the other side of the table.

"It is the same wrist."

"Say the rest of it."

"The rest of it is that I knew this in October and I did nothing about it." I sat down. "I knew it in the order book at Tindle, with my thumb on the paper, and I wrote it in my own hand in the margin, and then I went home and had my supper and I put the letter in a box."

"You had a case."

"I had a case and the case was finished. The name was found, the lawfulness was proved, the woman was being fed, and I had a folded paper in a drawer with half a sentence on it." I looked at him. "There is always a reason. That is the thing about reasons. There is always one, and it is always good, and it is always available at the exact moment when it would be convenient."

Edward sat down opposite me and turned the letter round so that it was the right way up for me and not for him.

"Read it to me," he said. "The whole of it. Aloud."

I read it aloud.

*Madam,*

*I am told that you are the woman who reads. I am told it by a man in Collow who does not know that he is telling me anything, and I have written this letter four times and burnt three, which is a thing I have not done since I was young and had more time than sense.*

*I knew your mother. I want to say that first because it is the only thing in this letter that is entirely true and the rest is business. She was a girl and I was not young when I knew her, and she did a thing for me that I have never been able to do for anybody, and I have thought about her every year since, and I have never once written her name down.*

*I have a thing that was hers. It is not worth money. It is worth something to me and it may be worth something to you, and if it is not, then it may be worth nothing at all and I will have been wrong, which I am used to.*

*I am old and I do not go out. If you come, come before the winter. If you do not come, I will understand it, and I will not write again, and you will not hear from me, and that will be the end of it.*

*E.C.*

I put the letter down.

"She asked me to come before the winter," I said. "In October. She asked me to come before the winter, and the winter is over, and the spring is over, and it is the twenty-second of April."

"Yes."

"And I did not answer."

"No."

I sat with my hands flat on the table.

I want to be exact about what I felt, because it would be easy to say that I felt ashamed, and shame is a large word that covers a great many smaller ones. What I felt was smaller and worse. I felt the way a person feels when they are going through a drawer and find a letter they meant to answer and see the date on it. There is no drama in that feeling. There is only the arithmetic.

Four lines. She had said, if you come, come before the winter. And I had read it twice and put it in a box and gone to Tindle and asked a hundred questions on behalf of a woman I had never spoken to, and not one of them had been *shall I go.*

"Do you know what is the worst of it," I said.

"Tell me."

"She asked me a question. One question, in four lines, and it was not even a hard one. It was *will you come.*" I looked up. "I have spent three years asking people questions they could not answer and I have been angry with every one of them who would not answer me. Bedloe. Sowerby. The bishop's man. Judith, in the winter, when she would not say what she wanted. I have sat in rooms and waited eleven seconds and thought less of a man because he would not give me a straight answer to a straight question."

"And a woman wrote you a straight question and you did not answer it."

"I did not answer it."

"Then you are like the rest of us," Edward said. "That is not a comfort. It is a fact, and it is the only fact in this room that will do you any good, because a woman who has been angry with other people for not answering must one day be angry with herself in the same way or she will spend her life on a step above everybody else."

***

I wrote the answer that morning, at the table, with the window open and the blackbird going.

It took four attempts, which is a thing I have not done since I was young and had more time than sense.

The first began *Madam* and I struck it out, because a woman who says *I knew your mother* is not a madam.

The second began *Dear Mrs. Callard* and I struck that out too, because I did not know that she was a Mrs., and because a name is a thing that has to be given and not assumed, and I of all people should have known that.

The third was four lines and read like a clerk's letter and I burnt it in the grate because it was the kind of letter that answers a question without answering it, and I had received a great many of those and knew exactly how they felt to receive.

The fourth was this:

*I have your letter of the eleventh of October and I have had it since the fourteenth and I did not answer it, and I am not going to give you a reason, because the reasons are all good and none of them is true.*

*I knew your hand before I knew your name. I saw it on a paper in a parish chest in Tindle, on a suspension of a removal order, in the year ninety-three, and it is the same hand and the same lean, and I have known since October that the person beyond the bridge and the person who wrote that suspension are one person, and I did nothing.*

*I am coming on Friday. I will come to the toll house on the far side of the ford and I will knock, and if you do not answer I will come again on the Saturday, and if you do not answer then I will write again and it will not take me four attempts.*

*You say you have a thing that was my mother's. I do not want it. I want to know what she did that you have never been able to do for anybody, and I want to know it in your own words and not in a letter, and I am prepared to sit in a cold room for as long as it takes.*

*M. Rooke*

I read it over twice and did not change a word, which is the only test of a letter that has ever worked for me.

***

The letter had to go onto paper that would travel.

I took a whole skin out of the case and laid it on the table with the window behind it so that the light came through the sheet and showed me where the watermark sat. I set out the ink and the sand and the pen, and I sat down and wrote the letter out fair.

The ink had a skin on it from being left, and I broke it with the corner of a card and lifted it off. The pen is one I keep for copies. The nib has been cut on one side by a hand that was not mine and it throws the ink to the right, so that every downstroke carries a small foot on it, and I had to hold the sheet further over to keep the margin clear of the nib's fault.

I spoiled the first skin with the fold.

I had the letter on it and it was a good letter, and I took it up to fold it before the ink had gone in, and the fold came down through a word and took the half of it that was wetter. I held it up to the window. The word had gone into the crease and there was ink on my thumb, and there was a print of my thumb on the corner of the sheet, the mark of a letter handled too much.

"Edward."

He was at the door with the strap of the trunk in his hand.

"Have you put the day in it."

"I have put the day in it."

"Then we are committed to the day."

"We are committed to the day and to the road and to the horse, and the letter is the smallest of the three." I put the spoiled skin on the fire. "You have been packing since dinner and you have said nothing about any of it."

"I have been packing since dinner and I have been thinking about it since dinner, and those are two different things and only one of them is any use to you." He came in as far as the table. "May I see it."

"No."

He stood with the strap in his hand and did not go and did not ask again, and after a moment he said, "Is it a short letter."

"It is a short letter with a long wait behind it."

He nodded at that and turned and went back to the trunk, and the strap went down on the lid in the other room.

I took out a second skin. This time I waited, and I blotted it with the sand and tipped the sand into the grate and blew along the page, and the ink stayed where it had been put.

Then I folded it. Once, and then again, the way her letter had been folded, two folds and no more, so that the sheet made a square with the writing shut inside it and one clean face for the address. I held it up. The folds lay flat.

Then I opened it out again on the table, because I wanted to read it once more, and because folding is a way of deciding a thing before it is time to decide it.

The fold lines stayed in it and would not go down.

There was a stick of sealing wax in the case, dark red, with the string burnt into one end where it had run over a candle. A stick of wax wants a candle and a steady hand and a table that does not matter, and I had none of the three. I put it back and took out the wafer instead, a thin one the colour of grey paper, and set it on the corner of the sheet where it would go when the time came.

***

I took the sheet down to the bar.

The woman who keeps the inn was at the tap with a cloth over her shoulder, and the bar was wet and she was wiping it in long strokes from the wall to the edge, and she did not stop when I came down.

"There is a letter," I said.

"There is always a letter." She put the cloth down. "Is it to go."

"It is to go to Barrowfield."

"Then it goes in the bag and the bag goes at noon and the bag does not wait for anybody, and if you have come down to ask me to hold the bag you have come to the wrong woman."

"I have not come to ask you to hold the bag."

"Then you have come to ask me something." She put her hand out. "Give it here."

I did not give it to her.

She looked at my hand and then at my face, and then she took her hand back and set it flat on the bar and left it there.

"It is not sealed," she said.

"It is not sealed."

"Then it is not a letter yet. It is a letter when it is sealed, and until it is sealed it is a thing you may still change." She picked the cloth up again. "Do you want it registered."

"No."

"Registered costs, and it goes in the book, and if it is lost the office must answer for it."

"No," I said. "It is not to be registered."

"A letter that is not registered is a letter nobody pays for if it is lost," she said. "It goes into the bag and out of this house, and after that it is a thing in the world with nothing standing behind it."

"Then it may be lost," I said.

She stopped wiping and wiped the same place on the bar again.

"You are going to Barrowfield yourself," she said.

"We follow it."

"Then the letter is not for her," she said. "A letter that goes on ahead of you is a letter that makes you keep your word. You have kept your word already or you would not be packing. So the letter is for you."

"It is for her. She asked me to come before the winter and I did not come, and a woman who has done that does not arrive at a door in the spring with no word in front of her."

"No," the woman said. "She arrives with an excuse, and the excuse is the letter." She put the cloth over her shoulder. "I am not saying it is a bad letter. I am saying what it is for. You want it in her hand before you are on her step, so that when you stand there she has had time to be angry with the paper instead of with you."

"That is a hard thing to say to a woman at a bar."

"It is a hard thing to hear at a bar. I have said harder." She nodded at the sheet. "And you would rather it were lost."

I put my free hand flat on the bar.

"Yes."

"Because then you would come to the door with nothing in front of you, and a woman who comes to a door with nothing in front of her may say anything she likes and blame the post." She took up the cloth again and folded it. "There is a name for that and it is not a kind name."

"There is no name for it. It is only what I would like."

"Then say what you would like and stop calling it the post." She held out her hand again, and this time she held it out flat and did not move it. "The address, then. Write it plain and write it large. A letter that is hard to read is a letter that sits in a bag at a turnpike while a man decides whether it is a B or an R."

"I have written it plain."

"Then write it plainer." She put her finger on the corner of the sheet. "And use a wafer. Wax cracks in the wet and a cracked seal is a letter that has been opened by a stranger, and there is no mending that, and there is no unreading it either."

"I have a wafer."

"Then you have everything." She put her hands on the bar. "Now. Do you want to tell me what is in it."

"No."

"Good," she said. "Because if you told me I would have to remember it, and a woman who remembers what is in other people's letters has to carry it about with her all day, and I have a bar to keep."

The dog came out from under the bench and stood in the passage, and the woman reached down without looking and put her hand on its head.

"Does the bag go at noon," I said.

"The bag goes at noon whether I am here or not. The boy takes it to the turnpike and the coach takes it from the turnpike and after the turnpike it is not my business and it is not yours." She straightened the cloth on her shoulder. "If you want it to go, seal it and bring it down and give it to the boy and do not stand at the bar with it in your hand, because a letter that is stood at a bar with is a letter that does not go."

I went up the stairs with the sheet in my hand and the bar wet behind me and the cloth going from the wall to the edge again.

Then I went in and sat down at the table and read the letter again, and it was still the letter I had written, and the woman at the bar had not put a word in it, and it was worse than it had been, because now it had a purpose that I had not given it.

***

Then I folded it and sealed it and gave it to the boy at the inn, and it went at noon, and it would be at Barrowfield on Thursday if the post went the way the post went, and we would be there on Friday, and so the letter and I would arrive within a day of each other, and one of us would be a day too late or a day too early and I had no way of knowing which.

***

"Do you want me to come?" Edward said, at the door, with his hat in his hand.

"Yes."

"You said in the winter that you did not need me."

"I did not need you in the winter," I said. "I need you now. Those are two different sentences and I have only recently learned that I am allowed to say the second one without it meaning that the first one was a lie."

He put his hat on.

"Then I will come," he said, "and I will not knock on that door, and I will stand at the gate, and if she asks who I am you will say that I am the man who drove you, and I will be content with that, because a man who is content to be the man who drove is a man who has finally learned his trade."

---

## Nine · What the Mill Man Wants

***

The way to the mill goes along the back of the town with the race on the left of it, a straight cut of water some man made with a shovel and then lined, and the wall on the right is brick and too high to look over, and the sound comes over the wall in a long low grind with a knock under it every time a bucket empties into the tail.

There was a cart backed into the gateway when I got there, and a man was putting sacks on it two at a time, and the horse would not stand, and I waited at the wall with the dust coming down on me out of the air.

Flour is in everything at a mill. It was on the stones in a white streak where the sacks had been dragged, and it was in the seam of the man's coat and along his eyebrows, and when he set a sack down the air went white about him for a moment and then cleared.

"Stand back a bit," he said. "He will not go round you."

I went back into the wet grass at the edge of the road.

"You will get it on you there."

"It is on me already."

"Aye," he said. "It gets in."

He took up the last sack and set it on the top of the pile and stood with his hand flat on it, and the carter came down off the shaft, and the horse went out of the gateway with the cart behind it and the wheels sluicing the wet, and the gateway was clear.

"You have come about the money," he said.

"Word goes round."

"Word goes round a mill quicker than a sack goes down a shoot, and the shoot is the quickest thing in it." He wiped his hands down his coat and made it worse. "He will see you. He sees everybody. That is not the same as listening."

"Is he a hard man."

"He is a fair one. He pays on the day and he does not take for the dust, and there is a master not far from here who does both." He picked the shovel up. "He keeps his own book of the flour and checks it against the waggon sheets himself, and a man who does that is not doing it because he likes the writing."

"Why is he doing it?"

"Because it is his," the man said, "and because he is not of this parish and never will be, and a man who pays into a thing he is not of will want to know where it goes."

I stood with the water going by on the other side of the wall.

"Do you know the woman at the end of the turning," I said.

"I know there is one. There has been one at the end of that lane as long as I have carried sacks here, and I have never set eyes on her, and I do not expect to." He put the shovel under the spill by the gate and lifted it. "That is not a thing I am proud of. It is a thing that is true."

"And if the money stops."

He shifted the shovel on his shoulder.

"Then it stops," he said. "There is the stair at the back."

He went into the doorway with the flour going in with him, and I stood in the lane a moment longer with the wheel going over the wall and the dust in the seams of my coat, and then I went under the arch.

***

Hesketh's mill was a four-storey brick building at the end of the Tindle road with a wheel that turned slowly and a yard full of barrels, and the noise of it came over the wall into the lane like weather. I had expected to be kept waiting in the yard. I was not. The girl at the door took my name and came back before I had finished looking at the barrels.

He was in a room at the top with the windows open on the water and a table with no cloth on it and a map of the drainage on the wall, and he was standing at the window with his hands behind him.

"Mrs. Rooke. You have come to be angry with me."

"I have come to ask you a question."

"That is worse," he said, and came and sat down. "Sit down. You will want to be standing, and standing is a way of not being in a room, and I have had twenty men stand in this room and tell me their business and not one of them has ever told me the truth standing up."

I sat down.

"You have the resolution," he said.

"I have a copy. Isaac Thwaite is a good clerk and a bad conspirator."

"Isaac Thwaite is a good clerk and a frightened man, and a frightened man is a great deal more use to the parish than a brave one." He put his hand flat on the table. "The resolution stands. The payment ceases at Michaelmas. I am not going to be moved from it by a woman with a folded paper in her pocket, and I say that with respect, because I have been told what you did at Barrowfield in the winter and I am not a fool and I do not take you for one."

"Then tell me why."

"Because I pay for it," Hesketh said.

He said it without heat, which was the thing that made me listen.

"I am a Dissenter," he said. "I have a meeting house and no vote in this parish and no seat on this vestry and no right to be buried in the churchyard I have paid for. I rent the mill and I pay the rate on it, and the rate on the mill is the largest single sum in the whole of the Tindle books, and I have paid it for eleven years, and in those eleven years I have not once been asked what it is spent on. Do you know why I have not been asked? Because I am not of the parish. I am a man who came here with capital. I am a thing that pays."

"You are not on the list."

"I am not on the list," he said. "And a man who is not on the list begins, after eleven years, to look very hard at what is."

I looked at him. He had the clean hands and the watch chain and the small upright hand, and under all of it he was a man who had been paying for forty years of other people's arrangements and had finally found one he could pull apart with arithmetic.

"You are not doing this because of eight pounds," I said.

"I am doing this because of the principle that the eight pounds represents, and I will tell you what the principle is, and then you may tell me that I am a hypocrite, and I will agree with you, and we will both have had a pleasant morning." He leaned back. "The principle is that this parish has, for forty years, maintained a woman out of the rate without a vote, without a resolution, and without a name. That is not charity. Charity is a thing a man does with his own money and his own name on it. This is a thing done in the dark by men who are all dead, out of money that other men earn, and the darkness is the point. The darkness is what has kept it going. A thing done in the light for forty years would have been voted on, and questioned, and probably stopped, and a thing done in the dark has gone on and on because nobody could see it to stop it."

"And if it stops, she dies."

"Then she dies in the light," Hesketh said, "and the parish will have to look at her while it happens, and it will either pay for her with its eyes open or it will not, and either way it will be a decision instead of a habit." He paused. "You do not like that."

"I do not like it because it is the speech of a man who has never had to be looked at," I said.

"No," he said. "It is the speech of a man who has been looked at every Sunday for eleven years by people who do not count him as a person, and who has decided that being looked at is at least honest." He stood up and went to the window. "Mrs. Rooke, I will make you an offer, and you will not like this either. Bring me the settlement. Prove where she belongs, and I will write to that parish and require them to receive her, and she will go there with a right to relief, and it will be lawful, and it will be permanent, and it will not depend on a hat."

"She has been in Tindle for forty years. She has no people anywhere. She was born in Nethercote and there is nobody left in Nethercote who has ever heard of her."

"Then bring me the order," he said, "and show me it was bad, and I will have no power over her at all, and she will be a woman in a cottage whom nobody may touch, and I will not be able to send her anywhere, and I will also not be able to pay her, because a woman with no order and no settlement is not a charge on the parish and the overseers may not enter her in the book."

"You have thought this through."

"I have thought this through for four months. It is my trade to think things through. I have a man at Ely who does nothing else." He turned round. "There is no answer that gives you both. That is not my cruelty. That is the shape of the law. Any arrangement that makes her safe from removal makes her a stranger, and any arrangement that makes her a charge makes her removable. She has been living in the one gap in the wall for forty years, and the gap is closing, and I did not close it. I only measured it."

***

I sat in the yard afterwards for a while, on a barrel, because the noise of the wheel was steady and a steady noise is a good place to put a bad thought.

He came down after a quarter of an hour and stood in the yard with his coat over his arm.

"May I ask you something," he said.

"You may ask."

"Why do you do it? Not the parish. Not the law. The woman. You have no people in Tindle. You are not of this parish. You are a woman from Collow with a husband and a house and, I am told, a trade that would keep you in comfort if you let it." He put his coat on. "You have spent a winter on a scraped line and a spring on a bundle with a ribbon on it, and you will spend the summer on it too, and at the end of it you will have a woman in a cottage who cannot thank you, because she has not spoken to anybody in forty years. What do you want out of it?"

The wheel went round. A barrel rolled somewhere and a man swore at it.

I opened my mouth to answer, and there was nothing in it.

I have made my living out of the fact that I always have something to say. I have stood in vestries and in court rooms and in a board room in Barrowfield and I have never once in my life been without a sentence. It is not a virtue. It is a thing that happens to a person who has spent their childhood listening at doors: you learn the shape of the answer before you know what the question is, and the shape carries you a long way.

There was no shape in me now. There was a man in a yard asking me a plain question, and behind him a mill wheel going round, and the sun coming through the yard door onto the barrels, and I stood there with my mouth open like a woman who has forgotten the word for a thing she can see.

"I do not know," I said.

Hesketh gave me a look and then looked down at his own hands.

"That is the first honest thing anybody has said to me in this parish," he said. "I am sorry for it. It is a great deal easier to be angry with a person who has an answer."

He walked to the gate and stopped.

"Michaelmas," he said. "If you find something, bring it to me and not to the vestry. The vestry will vote and I will decide, and a vote is a thing that can be taken back and a decision cannot."

***

I walked back along the river and did not go home, and at the bridge I stopped and stood with my hand on the parapet, and the water went under it the way it had gone under it in the autumn, and I said the sentence out loud because a sentence said out loud has to be finished.

"What do you want out of it?"

The water went on.

There is a particular kind of silence that comes after a question you have asked yourself in a place you have been before, and the silence is not empty, it is occupied, and what occupies it is the knowledge that you asked the same question in the same place a year ago and did not answer it then either.

I took the paper out of my pocket and looked at it, and the light was going and the ink was faint and the sentence was the same length it had been in the drawer.

***

A boy came up over the crown of the bridge with a sack on his back and his father's boots round his neck.

He carried the sack with a rope over his shoulder and his hand on the rope, and the boots were tied together by the laces and hung down his chest, and they knocked against him when he walked.

The bridge is narrow. I stood in the middle of it with the paper in my hand, and he stopped at the end of the parapet and set the sack down on the stone, and the sack made a soft sound when it went down, the sound of a thing full of something light.

"Do you want that carried," he said.

"No."

I said it before I had thought about it, which is the way I say no to everything that costs nothing.

He did not pick the sack up.

"It is no trouble," he said. "I am going that way."

"I am not going anywhere."

"Then you are standing still."

He said it without impudence. He said it the way a person says that the water is low.

I put the paper in my pocket. Then I took it out again, because a paper in a pocket is a paper that creases, and I stood with it in my hand and had nowhere to put my eyes.

"You are standing still," he said, "and I am going that way, and it is a long bridge."

"It is a short bridge."

"It is longer than it looks."

I began to walk. He picked the sack up and came after me, and at the end of the bridge he was beside me and not behind me.

"What is in the sack," I said.

"Feathers."

"For what."

"For the beds at the inn. They are wanted in the morning and the man will not come for them, so I am taking them, and I am to stand at the door until the money is paid."

"Who pays you?"

"Nobody pays me. My father is paid. I do the carrying."

The rope slipped on his shoulder and the sack went sideways, and I put my hand out and took the weight of it under the corner and held it until he had the rope right again, and then I took my hand away and put it behind my back.

"Thank you," he said.

"It was falling."

"It was not falling. It was going sideways. It does that." He settled the rope. "You are stronger than you look."

"I am not strong. It was a light sack."

"It is feathers," he said. "Everything about it is light except the carrying."

We went on.

"And the boots," I said.

"They are my father's. The cobbler has had them since the fair and he will not give them up until he is paid, and my father will not pay him until he has the boots, and I am to stand in the shop until the cobbler is tired." He shifted the rope. "It is a good arrangement. It has been going on a long while."

"Who made the arrangement?"

"My father."

"Then it is not a good arrangement."

"It is good for the cobbler," he said. "He has the boots."

I laughed, and the laugh surprised me more than it surprised him, and he looked up and grinned and then stopped grinning, because a boy who has made a woman laugh in the road does not know what to do with the next part.

"You came out of the mill yard," he said.

"I did."

"My uncle says the mill man is going to stop the money."

"Your uncle says a great deal."

"He says it at the table." He shifted the sack. "Is it true."

"It is true."

"Then what will she do."

We walked a little way while I thought about how to answer a boy with a sack of feathers.

"She will be in the light," I said. "That is what the mill man says. She will be in the light and the parish will have to look at her."

"That is not an answer."

"No," I said. "It is what I have."

At the lane end he stopped, and I stopped, because a person stops when the person beside them stops.

"You are sure about the paper," he said.

"I am sure."

"It is not heavy."

"It is not the weight."

He thought about that, standing in the road with the sack on his back. He was a boy who thought about things with his whole face.

"Then it is a thing you want to carry yourself," he said.

"It is a thing I have to carry myself."

"That is different," he said. "That is a good reason. If you had said it was not heavy I would have thought you were being polite."

I put my hand in my pocket and there was nothing in it but a knot of thread, and I took my hand out again.

"I have nothing to give you," I said.

"I did not ask for anything."

"I know. That is the difficulty."

He looked at me, and then at the sack, and then at the road.

"You are a queer article," he said, and said it kindly.

"I am aware."

He took up the sack and went off down the lane, and the boots knocked against him until the sound of them had gone round the corner, and I stood at the lane end with the paper in my hand and could not think what to do with my arms.

I held it against my chest, and then at my side, and then in front of me with my hands under it, the way a woman carries a plate, and a woman came up the road with a basket and looked at me and looked away, and I did not care what she made of it, which is not a thing I have ever been able to say.

***

Then I put it away and went home, and I did not say anything to Edward about the yard, and he did not ask, and we ate, and in the morning we packed for Barrowfield.

---

## Ten · The Road to Barrowfield

The coat was in the chest at the foot of the bed, under the blanket that had gone bald in the middle, and I took it out on the Thursday night and laid it across the bed.

It was my mother's. It was grey-brown and heavy, and the weight of it was the weight of a coat made for a woman who stood at a tub in a cold house and had to be warm while she did it. The lining was torn at the armpit and had been mended there, and the buttons did not match, because she had replaced them as they came off, and a coat whose buttons have been replaced as they came off will tell you the order in which things wore out. The right cuff was thin at the wrist, and the thin place was where the hand goes into the water.

I turned the pockets out over the bed.

A button. A bent pin with the point gone. A key that fitted nothing in this house. A scrap of paper with a list on it in her hand, and the list was not finished, and the last word on it I could not read.

I sat on the bed and turned the pocket out to the seam, and that is when I found the patch.

It was on the lining, a square of another cloth, a piece of something that had been an apron, and it had been sewn on with small stitches that ran from the right of the pocket to the left.

I have sewn all my life. I sew from the left to the right, because I hold the work still and push the needle away from me, and my stitches lie the same way down a seam, and anybody who has ever looked at the inside of a hem in this house could tell my work from hers.

She turned the work. She held the needle still and turned the cloth, and the stitches went the other way, and they were small and even, and they had been put in quickly, because the tear in the pocket had been made by a hand going in for something in a hurry.

I sat on the bed with the coat across my knees and my thumb on the patch, and the candle was not lit yet and the room was going grey.

The door went and Edward came in with the candle and set it on the wash stand.

"You have got the coat out."

"I have got the coat out."

"It will be cold on the road."

"It will."

He stood with his hands behind him and looked at the coat, and then at me, and he said the thing that could be done instead of the thing that could not, which is a habit of his.

"It wants brushing."

"It does not want brushing."

"It has been in a chest."

"It has been in a chest and it will go back in a chest, and I am not going to have it brushed for a town that will not look at it."

He came and stood at the end of the bed and did not put his hand out, which was the right thing, and did not say that it was a good piece of work, which would have been the wrong thing.

"There is a patch in the pocket," I said. "She put it there."

He looked at it for a while.

"Your stitches go the other way," he said.

"Yes."

"Does it matter?"

"It matters to me."

"Then it matters," he said, and took the candle up and held it lower so that I could see the corner of the patch where the thread had gone, and he held it there until my hands were still, and then he set it down.

"You have worn it every winter since," he said.

"Every winter."

"Indoors."

"Indoors."

"And you will wear it into Barrowfield on Friday."

"I will."

"They will ask about it," he said.

"They will."

"And you will say it was your mother's."

"I will say it was my mother's, and the ones who are old enough will look at the lining and remember the apron it came out of, and the ones who are not will look at the buttons and say nothing."

"That is a hard town to walk into in a coat."

"It is the coat I have."

He nodded, and went to the door, and stopped with his hand on the latch.

"Will you want the other as well," he said.

"There is no other."

"Then that is settled. The box goes on the seat and the coat goes on you." He opened the door. "You have been packing that box in your head for a long while. It will be packed by the time the candle is out."
