Then he went, and closed the door with his hand flat on it the way he does when he does not want the latch to speak.

I cut the loose thread at the corner of the patch with the scissors and not with my teeth, and I put my hand into the pocket and left it there.

The pocket came to the height of my hand. It had come to the height of hers.

I folded the coat lengthwise and laid it over the back of the chair, and I went to bed, and it was the last thing I looked at, and in the morning I put it on before anything else.

***

We went on the Friday, at five in the morning, with the sky the colour of a knife and the birds already at it.

I packed a small box. That was the whole of the packing, and it took me an hour, because a woman who is going back to the town she was born in does not pack clothes, she packs evidence.

Into the box went: the copy of the order; the copy of the suspension; the two names from the roll at Ely written out in my own hand; the three slips from the winter of thirty-three, the T and the Tace and the initial with the surname; the third letter; and the paper.

The paper went in last and I did not look at it when I put it in, and I put it between two sheets of the copy so that it would not crease, and the putting of it between two sheets so that it would not crease was a thing I noticed myself doing and did not stop.

"You have packed the paper," Edward said.

"I have packed a box."

"You have packed a box and the paper is in it, and you have put it between two sheets, which is what a person does with a thing they intend to show somebody." He took the box out to the trap. "I am not saying anything about it. I am noting it. Noting is my trade."

***

The road to Barrowfield is a hundred and eleven miles and it takes three days if the weather is kind and the horses are changed at the right inns, and it had been four years since I had gone the whole of it in one journey.

The first day was flat and the second day was flat and then there were hills at the end of the second day, and by the third morning the country had changed into the country I was born in, which is a country of small fields and hedges that are older than the fields and a great deal of sky.

I did not sleep well at the inn on the second night, and Edward did not sleep either, and we lay in the dark of a room over a yard where a man was walking a horse up and down for no reason that either of us could work out.

"Say something," he said.

"What?"

"Anything. A list. You are very good at lists and a list is a thing you can hear in the dark."

"Very well," I said. "Fourteen offices. Market, crane, shambles, coal back, measure, ferry, pump, toll at the turn, lamps, scavenger, bell, bridge, school, almshouse. Thirteen of them produce a fee and one does not, and the one that does not is the almshouse, and the almshouse is the one that keeps people alive, and there is a lesson in that which I have never been able to put into a sentence."

"That is not a list. That is a sermon with numbers in it."

"You asked for a list."

"I asked for something to hear in the dark." He turned over. "Tell me about the house."

"Which house."

"The toll house. You have been avoiding it for three days and we will be at the ford by six tomorrow, and I would rather hear it now than at the gate."

I lay on my back and looked at the ceiling, which I could not see.

"It is on the far side of the ford," I said. "The bridge is the parish boundary, and the toll house is on the far side, so that the toll is paid by people coming in and not by people going out, which is the whole of the politics of Barrowfield in one building. It has a window in the gable that faces the bridge, and the window is where the toll keeper sits, and there is a board with the rates on it nailed to the wall, and the board was repainted every year and the painting was done by the same man for thirty years and he could not spell *carriage*."

"You have looked at that house a great many times."

"I have walked past it every week of my childhood," I said. "It is a quarter of a mile beyond the last gate, and my mother went to the market on Wednesdays, and the road to the market crosses the ford, and there is no other road. So every Wednesday of my life until I was seventeen I passed within four feet of that window, and there was smoke in it, and I never once saw a person in it, and I never once asked who lived there."

"Why not?"

"Because it was on the other side of the bridge," I said, "and I was a child of the parish, and a child of the parish does not ask what is on the other side of the boundary. The boundary is the whole of the lesson. It is taught from the pulpit and it is taught by the toll board and it is taught by the way the road goes, and by the time a child is nine they know without being told that the people beyond the bridge are not their business."

Edward was quiet for a while.

"And now you are going to knock on that door," he said.

"And now I am going to knock on that door."

"In the town where you were a child, at a house you passed a thousand times, to see a woman your mother knew and never spoke of."

"Yes."

"Then I will tell you the thing I have been keeping for the third day," Edward said, "which is that when we get there you will not be a child of the parish. You will be a woman with a box of papers and a husband and a name of your own, and the boundary will mean exactly as much to you as you allow it to mean, and no more." He put his hand out in the dark and found mine. "That is the whole of what I have to say. It has taken me three days to say it and it will take you about four seconds to disagree."

"I am not going to disagree."

"Then we may both sleep," he said, and we did not sleep, and the man in the yard walked the horse up and down until it was light.

***

We came to the ford at six in the evening of the third day.

The water was low for April and the ford was passable and the bridge was where it had always been, humped and narrow, with the parapet worn shiny at the top where two hundred years of hands had gone along it.

I stopped the trap before the bridge.

"Edward."

"I am not going anywhere," he said. "I am sitting here."

I got down and walked onto the bridge and stood in the middle of it with my hands on the parapet, and the water went under me the way it had gone under me when I was seven and when I was seventeen and when I was twenty-eight, and on the far side the road came up out of the ford and turned, and the toll house stood at the turn with its gable window facing me.

There was smoke.

It was thin and it was going up straight in the evening air, and the light was behind the house so that the smoke was lit from the side and looked like a thing being drawn rather than a thing happening.

The window was dark. There was no lamp in it. But there was smoke, and there had been smoke every Wednesday of my childhood, and I stood on the bridge and looked at the smoke and thought about the fact that a fire had been kept in that house for forty years by a woman who had never once been seen in the yard, and that the fire had been kept for the purpose of being alive, and that being alive had cost her twenty pounds in 1794 and a great deal more since.

I went back to the trap.

"Not tonight," I said.

"No," Edward said. "Not tonight. Tomorrow, in the morning, with your boots clean and your letter in your hand and the light behind you instead of in your eyes."

He turned the horse and we went back up the road to the inn at the turnpike, and I did not look round at the toll house, and I was aware of not looking round, and I was aware that being aware of it was the same as looking.

***

At the inn I took the paper out of the box and put it on the table, and Edward looked at it and then at me.

"I am not going to write on it," I said.

"I did not say anything."

"You were thinking it very loudly."

"I was thinking that a woman who carries a half-finished sentence a hundred and eleven miles might be intending to finish it at the other end," he said. "It is not a criticism. It is an observation, and I have already told you that observing is my trade, and you married me anyway."

I took my coat off and hung it on the back of the door and stood in the middle of the room in my shift with my hair down, and the inn was old and the floor was uneven and the candle was on the wash stand and the room was warm for the first time in three days.

"Come here," I said.

He came.

There is a thing that happens when two people have been on a road for three days and have been careful with each other the whole way, which is that the carefulness has to be put down somewhere, and it is usually put down in a room with a door that locks. I put my hands flat on his chest and he put his hands on my back, and the shift was thin and his hands were cold through it and I did not mind, and I kissed him the way a person kisses when they have been thinking about it at the wrong moments for three days, at a turnpike and over a horse and in the middle of a list.

Later, with the candle out and the window open and the smell of the ford coming up from the bottom of the road, he said into my hair, "What are you going to ask her?"

"I do not know."

"You always know."

"I always know what to ask a person who has something I want," I said. "This is different. She has nothing I want and she has asked me to come, and a person who has been asked to come does not arrive with a list of questions. She arrives with her hands empty and waits to be told what the visit is."

"That is not like you at all."

"No," I said. "It is not. I have been on the road for three days thinking about it and I have arrived at the conclusion that the only way to be any use to a woman who has been asked nothing for forty years is to let her ask first."

Edward was quiet for so long that I thought he had gone to sleep.

"That," he said at last, "is the first thing you have ever said that sounds like a person who might one day be happy."

---

## Eleven · Barrowfield Again

The boy at the turnpike inn had one employment, which was the brushing of coats, and he brushed Edward's for a quarter of an hour on the Saturday morning while I stood at the coffee room window and looked down the last mile of road at the town I had not seen from that side in four years.

"You are quiet," Edward said.

"I am counting."

"Count aloud. It will save me asking."

"The chimney," I said. "The roof of the shambles, which has been slated. The pump, which has a lead cistern on it now and a brass cock and a board of subscribers. The saddler's at the corner of Cow Lane, which has a brass plate and a wire blind and is a bank. The chapel at the end of Cow Lane, which is new, and brick, and has a notice board by the door with the times of the services in a very good hand. And a drain in the market place."

"Six," Edward said. "A man could make a sermon of six."

"A man could make a sermon of anything. That is the trouble with sermons."

He came and stood at the window with his coat over his arm, and the boy waited behind him with the brush in his hand, not certain whether the coat was finished with.

"Which of the six do you mind?" he said.

"The drain. It has taken the pump out of the middle of the market place and put it at the side, and a pump at the side is a pump where women stand in a row with their backs to everybody. The pump in the middle was where they stood face to face."

"Then the drain has done more than drain."

"The drain has done a great deal, and nobody will ever put it in a book."

We went down at seven. The smell of the place arrived before the place did, which is the smell of every market town in that county: coal, and dung, and wet rope, and the sweetish reek of the shambles where the blood goes into the gutter and the gutter goes into the river.

I had been born in a house at the back of the shambles, in a room over a yard with a pear tree in it. The pear tree was still there. It had been cut back hard and grew again in a shape nobody would have chosen, and the pears would be small and hard and taken by boys before they were fit for anything.

That was my inheritance from that house, a pear tree and a way of standing in doorways, and I had married a man who wrote both of them down.

***

We left the trap at the George and walked. That was Edward's idea, and he gave it without argument, as a thing already decided.

"A man in a trap is a man passing through," he said. "A man on foot is a man who has come."

"And a woman on foot?"

"A woman on foot is a woman who has come and is not leaving in a hurry. They will have told each other by dinner."

He walked on the outside of me, and at the corner he took his hat off to an old woman carrying a chicken upside down. She looked at him, and then at me, and went on. The chicken and the hat would be in three kitchens before the clock struck.

The market place was half full. It was a Saturday, and the butter was in, and there were four tables under the cross with eggs and greens, and the rest was the ordinary business of a town: a man mending a hurdle, a boy with a barrow of salt, a cart of coal with the horse asleep in the shafts.

Nobody spoke to me. A town you were born in does one particular thing to you: the people who knew you as a girl will look at you for two seconds longer than they look at a stranger and then decide, all at once and without consulting, that they will wait.

***

The shop smelled of tallow and blacking. Judith was behind the counter with a beam scale and a knife, cutting a block of hard soap into squares, and she did not stop when the door went.

"Mrs. Rooke," she said.

"Judith."

"Pratt now. You sent a dish." She put a square on the pan and took a weight from the block and set it beside it, and the beam went down and came up and swung. "It has a blue rim and a chip on the underside. It is on the shelf behind you. It has never had a thing in it."

"Why not?"

"Because it is the only thing in this house that came from you, and I did not want to break it." She wrote the weight on a slate with a piece of chalk and folded the paper over the soap with one hand. "You look well. That is a compliment. I do not give many, and you may take it or leave it."

"I will take it."

"Then sit down. Not there, that is the cat." She swept the cat off the stool with the back of her hand, without looking, and the cat went onto the floor and sat down again a hand's width away and looked at me with the contempt of an animal that has won. "You have come about the toll house."

"How do you know that?"

"Because you have come to Barrowfield in May with a box of papers and a husband, and you have not been to see me since you married, and there is only one thing over that bridge." She put the bag on the shelf and took up the knife. "You will want the list. Everybody who comes back wants the list. Then they want to tell me about the ones they knew best, and I stand here and cut soap, because I am the one who stayed."

"Then give me the list, and I will not tell you anything."

Judith put the knife down. It was the first thing she had done slowly since I came in.

"Marrow the sexton went in the November," she said. "He was digging a grave and sat down in it to rest and that was the end of him, and they buried him in it, which saved a deal of trouble and was the only joke he ever made. Capper the schoolmaster went at Easter, and his sister burnt his books in the yard. The miller's son drowned at the flash in July, and his father has not had the wheel going since. Mrs. Petty is blind and sits at the window and knows everybody by their step. And your mother's stone is up. Your father paid for it and would not have a word of mine about it."

That was the list.

"What word would you have had?"

"Her name."

The beam was still swinging. Judith put her finger on it and stopped it.

"I said that much to him in this shop," she said, "and he said the stone was his to put up and the words on it were his to choose, and I said that a woman is not a word a man chooses, and he said I had no business in it. Then he paid for his candle and went out, and we have been civil ever since."

"Was there a quarrel?"

"There was a sentence each. That is a quarrel in this town. A quarrel here is two sentences and forty years of nodding."

Edward had come in and stood at the door with his hat in his hand.

"Your husband," Judith said.

"Edward Rooke."

"I know. He is the one who stands at gates." She nodded at him and he nodded at her. That was the introduction. "You will want to know what the town says about the woman at the ford."

"I know what the town says. I have known it since I was a child."

"You know the children's version. The children's version is a witch with a box of money. The grown version is that she is a miser, and the grown version is worse, because a miser is a person nobody has to be sorry for." Judith took up the knife again. "She has a boy who fetches for her. He is the son of the woman at the lime kiln, and he is not right in the head, and he is the only person in this town who has been inside that door in years. The town has decided she has something to hide."

"Does she?"

"She does not go out. If the town had made a witch of you at twelve, you would not go out either. That is not a thing to hide. That is a thing to be let alone." She wiped her hands on her apron and stood with both hands on the counter, and her face did not soften. "Now I will say the thing I have been keeping, and you will not like it, and I will say it anyway, because I am the one who stayed."

"Say it."

"Your mother went over that ford every Wednesday of her life, and she went with a full basket." Judith held up one finger before I could speak. "Do not tell me what a market is. A market is a full basket going out and a full one coming back, or a full one out and a light one back, and you can see which it is, because a woman with eggs carries the basket in front of her and a woman with nothing carries it at her side. My mother took note of her for years. She went out with the basket in front and came back with it at her side and empty, every Wednesday, until the year she took ill. My mother asked her once what she did over there. Your mother said, I go to see a woman. My mother said, what woman. Your mother said, a woman who does not want company. That was the end of it, and my mother told me the story when I was a girl and told me not to repeat it."

The shop was quiet. A cart went past outside with a loose iron on it, and the noise went away up the street.

Nobody had told me.

"Ask her what your mother carried over that ford," Judith said. "That is all. Ask her, and if she will not say, come back here and I will give you a cup of tea, and we will talk about the price of soap, and you will go home to Collow with your box, and I will still have the dish."

"Judith."

"Go on. It is Saturday and I have candles to make up, and a woman who is going over that bridge should go before the afternoon, because she sleeps in the afternoon and she will not answer a door after three."

***

The churchyard was on the rise behind the church, and the gate was iron and mended at the hinge with hoop iron, and the yew was older than the wall and cut into a shape that had given up being a shape.

The gate stood open.

Edward stopped at it and put his hat under his arm.

"I will wait here."

"You may come in."

"I will wait here," he said, "and I will take my hat off, and if anybody asks I will say that I am the man who drove you."

The grass had been scythed and the clippings lay in rows and were going yellow, and the smell of them was the smell of every summer of my childhood. The Ashby ground was on the north side, in the shade, which is the cheap side, and there were four stones in it and one of them was new.

It was slate. The letters were cut deep and filled with lead.

*Mary Ann, wife of Josiah Ashby*

That was the top line. Under it there was a space the width of the stone, and under the space, at the bottom, the mason had cut a single line, and the line was a text and not a fact.

*She was a faithful wife and a good mother*

No maiden name. No year of birth. No year of death. No age. She had been born in a house at the back of the shambles and had died in another house in the same street, and the stone said what she had been to a man and to her children, and nothing at all about what she had been before.

I read it four times. Each time the space in the middle of the stone got larger.

"Edward."

He came in with his hat in his hand.

"Read it."

He read it twice. He read it the way he read everything, from the top, without moving his lips, and then he read it again from the top, which is what he did with a document that has something wrong with it.

"There is no date," he said.

"There is no name."

"Mary Ann is a name."

"Mary Ann is a thing her father gave her and her husband kept. It is not the name she was born with and it is not the name she was buried under, because the book has her as Ashby, and Ashby is my father." I put my hand flat on the top of the stone, and the slate was warm, because the sun had come round and the yew had a gap in it. "My father has his trade on his stone and the year he died and the year he was born. He has a life on his stone. She has a marriage."

Edward stood with his hat in his hand and did not say the thing that a man says.

"I have spent three years putting names back into books," I said. "I opened a chest at Tindle and found a line where a name had been taken out with a blade, and I made a noise about it, and I was right to. And there is a stone in my own ground, with my own mother under it, and the name she was born with is not on it, and I have walked past it every year of my life and never once read it."

"You have read it now."

"Yes."

"That is not nothing," he said. "It is very little, and it is not nothing."

I took my hand off the stone and put it on my coat, on the sleeve at the elbow, where the cloth had gone thin. The coat had been hers. I had worn it since I married and had had it turned once, and the turning had put the worn places on the inside, and the elbow was the one place the tailor could not turn.

I put my palm flat on it and held it there.

The yard went the colour of milk. It is not a fog and not a light; it is the air deciding to be thicker than air. A window came into it, and a woman stood at the window with one hand flat on the glass and her back to me, and beyond the glass there was a road and a ford and a house with a gable, and the hand on the glass did not move and did not knock.

Then the milk went out of the air. The yew was a yew. A thrush was at something in the roots. The coat was a coat on my arm, and the warmth in it was the warmth of my own hand.

I did not move.

Edward had not moved either. He had his hat in his hand and his eyes on the stone, and he had seen nothing, because in four years he had never once seen it, and I had stopped explaining it to him, because an explanation makes a thing smaller.

"Tomorrow," I said.

He looked up.

"Not today."

"Tomorrow," I said again, and the word came out of me the way a word comes out when it has been decided somewhere below the place where words are made. "Today I will go back to the inn and sit still, and tomorrow I will go over the ford with my boots clean, and I will knock on that door, and I will take her nothing, because Judith says she will not take a thing from my hand."

"And what will you ask her?"

"Nothing. Not the first day. I have been asking for three years, and every question was a good one, and not one of them ever made a person open a door." I looked at the stone. "She asked me who I was, and I could not answer, and I have had a winter to think about it and I still cannot. A woman who cannot answer that has no business arriving with a list."

Edward put his hat on.

"Then I will stand at the gate."

"You will stand at the gate."

"And if she asks who I am, I will say that I am the man who drove you, and she will look at me for a while, and then she will decide about me, and I will abide her decision."

He went out through the iron gate, and the hinge made a noise like a bird being stood on, and I stayed with my hand on the stone until the slate had taken the warmth out of my palm. I took it away. The slate was cold.

"Tomorrow," I said, to the stone and to nobody, and went down after him.

---

## Twelve · The Toll House

The ford was low for May, and the water went over the stones in a sheet the thickness of a hand, and I took off my boots at the bank and went over with them in my hand, and the cold came up through my ankles and stayed.

Edward went by the bridge. We had arranged it at the inn without either of us saying the word.

"I will take the bridge," he said, "and be at the gate before you, and I will not knock, and if she looks out and sees a man with his hat off, she will know what I am."

"And what are you?"

"The man who drove you." He put his hat on and then took it off again. "The water is cold."

"It is cold."

"You could take the bridge."

"I could."

He looked at the ford and then at me, and did not say the thing he had decided not to say.

"Your mother went over the ford," he said.

"She went over the ford."

"Then I will see you at the gate," he said, and went up onto the bridge with his hat in his hand, like a man going to a funeral, which is what he was.

The bridge is the front door and the ford is the way round the back, and whoever goes over the bridge has agreed to be counted. My mother went over the ford with a basket in front of her, because the ford is free, and a woman with a basket does not want to stand at a window and explain herself.

The water came over my ankles and my skirt got wet to the knee, and I came up the bank into the long grass with my boots in my hand, and there was the toll house, twenty feet away, and I had never been on that side of it in my life.

It was smaller than it looked from the bridge. It was one storey and a loft, the toll window at the height of a seated person's chest, and under the window a slab of oak worn into a dish where the coins had gone down for a hundred years. The bar was up, and its chain was oiled and hung in a coil.

The garden was the size of a tablecloth. It had been dug, and there was a row gone to seed and a heap of last year's stalks, and a path of cinders to the door, and the door was on the garden side, not the road.

I stood at the gate for a while.

There was smoke. There was always smoke. I had looked at that smoke every Wednesday of my childhood from the other side of the water and made a story of it, and the story was about a woman who was cold, and the truth was a woman who kept a fire because the fire was the only other living thing she had.

The paper was in my pocket. I had put it there in the morning without deciding to, and the corner of it was under my thumb, and the fold had worn through in one place.

I did not take it out.

I stood at the gate and looked at the door for as long as it takes to boil a kettle, and then I went up the path and knocked.

Nothing. The knock went into the door and the door did not answer it, and behind the door a clock was going, slow and heavy, with a weight on a chain.

I knocked again.

The door opened inward, a hand's width, and then a foot, and then all the way, and an old woman stood in it.

She was small. I had known she would be small. She was smaller than that, and straight as a rule, and her cap had been washed until the crown went thin, and her hands were the hands of a person who has done fine work and stopped, the knuckles swollen and the fingers still exact.

She looked at me. She looked at my boots, still in my hand and dripping on her cinders, and at my skirt, wet to the knee.

"Who are you," she said.

It was the same question, in the same flat Tindle vowel, at a different door. I had had a winter to think of an answer and had not thought of one, because a person preparing an answer has decided what the question is.

"Mary Ann Ashby's daughter," I said.

"That is your mother. I asked who you are."

"I know. I have spent a year finding out that it is not the same question."

She stood in the door and looked at me for four seconds. I counted them.

"Wipe your feet," she said, and stood back.

I wiped my feet on the iron scraper and came in, and she shut the door behind me with her hand flat on it, and the latch went down.

***

The room was one room and it was warm. That was the thing I had not expected, after the smoke and the closed door and the forty years. It smelled of coal and soap and something cooking slowly, and the fire was built small and close in the grate, the way a fire is built by a person who will not put another piece on until it is needed.

The floor was stone and had been scrubbed. There was a table with a bowl and a knife and a loaf under a cloth, a chair by the fire with a cushion sat on until the stuff of it had gone, a bed behind a curtain, and a shelf with three cups and a teapot and a tin.

On the wall facing the window was the toll board. It was four feet long and painted black, and the rates were in white letters in two columns, repainted so many times that they stood up from the board a little, and the hand that painted them made an S that leaned.

*For every horse, beast, or driving of cattle*
*For every score of sheep*
*For every carriage*
*For every hearse, as for a carriage*
*For a corpse, nothing*

I read the last line twice.

The clock stood by the window, and it was the clock I had been listening to through the door, and the case was mahogany and had a bloom on it from years of polishing, and the face had been repainted by a person who made the numerals too large, so that the hands had to be short.

The cat was on the toll book. It was a tabby with a torn ear and one eye gone the colour of milk, and it lay along the ledger with its chin on the spine, and it did not get up when I came in, or when Esther pushed past it.

"That is my chair," Esther said.

I was still by the door.

"The one by the fire. It is mine. Sit on the other one."

There was another chair, on the far side of the table, with a wooden seat and no cushion, and I sat on it, and the cat opened its one good eye and shut it again.

"You are wet."

"I came over the ford."

"I know how you came. I had my eye on you the whole way." She went to the window and stood at the counter, and the light came through the glass and made a shape on the floor. "You stood at my gate long enough to be rained on, and there is no rain. What were you doing?"

"Deciding."

"Deciding what?"

"Whether to knock."

She turned her head and looked at me along the counter.

"You knocked," she said. "That is the deciding. The rest is a way of making yourself late."

She came back and stood by the table with her hands on the back of her chair.

Then she looked at my coat. Not at me, at the coat, at the sleeve of it, at the worn place at the elbow, and then at the collar, and then at the button that did not match the others.

"That is Mary Ann's coat," she said.

"It is."

"It has been turned."

"Once."

She put one hand out and rubbed the cloth between her finger and her thumb, the way a person rubs cloth in a shop, and then took her hand back.

"Turn round," she said.

I stood up and turned round, and stood with my back to her in the middle of her room, the fire on my left and the clock on my right, while an old woman looked at a coat.

"It hung better on her," she said. "She had shoulders. You have your father's shoulders."

I turned round again.

"You knew my father."

"I knew of him." She sat down in her chair, and the cushion gave under her, and she put her hands in her lap. "Sit down. You are dripping on my floor, standing there like a woman at an auction."

I sat down.

***

The clock struck. It struck the hour and went on striking, and I counted the strokes because there was nothing else to do with my eyes, and it struck one more than the sun was saying.

"Your clock is fast," I said.

"My clock is set."

"It is set a quarter of an hour ahead."

"It is set a quarter of an hour ahead," she said, "because I have not been late for anything since 1794, and I do not intend to begin now."

I looked at her.

"That is a joke," she said. "You may laugh or you may not. Most people do not, and then they sit there wondering whether it was one, and I have to watch their faces while they do it."

"I am not going to laugh."

"No," she said. "You would not."

The kettle began to talk on the hob, and she got up and made the tea without hurrying, and put the pot on the table with a woollen cosy over it and set a cup in front of me, and the cup had a chip out of the rim that had been smoothed by a great many washings.

"There is no sugar," she said. "There has been no sugar in this house since the price went up, and I did not begin again when it came down."

"I do not take it."

"Then you will not miss it." She sat down. "You have come about ninety-three."

"I have come about the twenty pounds."

"The twenty pounds is ninety-three. It is the same errand with a different coat on." She poured the tea. "Ask."

And then, because she had told me to ask, I could not.

***

The bar went down outside. There was the noise of the chain and the bar coming down onto its rest, and a man's voice at a horse, and then a cart wheel on the stones, and Esther got up with the cup in her hand and went to the window and opened the sash.

"Cart," she said, out of the window. "Laden."

The man outside said something I could not hear.

"Laden is laden," she said. "If you have a stone on it, it is laden, and if you have a sack on it, it is a cart, and you may argue with me through the window at Michaelmas when the trust meets and I will still be here."

The man said something else, and there was a coin.

"Put it in the box," she said, "and do not put it on the counter, because I have told you before that a coin on the counter is a coin I have to pick up, and I am sixty."

The coin went into the box with a sound like a small bell. The bar went up. The cart went through, the wheels went away down the road, and Esther shut the sash and came back to her tea.

"You keep the gate," I said.

"I keep the gate."

"You do not go out."

"I do not go out." She drank. "The boy drops the bar and lifts it and takes the coin when there is a coin, and I look out of that window and say the rate, and I write it in the book, and the trust sends a man at quarter day and he counts the box and reads the book, and he has never once found a penny out."

"Who is the boy?"

"Nobody. He is the son of a woman at the kiln and he has no more sense than a hen, and he is worth more than the trust's man, and he does not ask me questions." She set the cup down. "You are not drinking your tea."

I drank my tea. The tea was hot.

"Now," she said. "You have come a hundred and eleven miles to sit at my table and drink my tea and look at my wall. Ask your question, and ask it in the plainest words you have, because I am old and I have stopped doing the other kind."

I put the cup down.

"Did my mother bring you food?" I said.

The cat got up off the book and stepped down onto the floor and went to the fire and sat with its back to us, and Esther looked at the cat and not at me.

"Every Wednesday," she said, "for thirty years."

"Thirty years."

"Thirty years and some. She began when she was a girl and stopped when she was ill, and in all that time she never once came to the door and never once asked to come in, and she put the basket on the step and went away, and I took it in when she was out of sight of the window, and neither of us ever said what it was."

I sat with my hands in my lap.

"Why did she do it?"

"That is not the question you came to ask."

"It is the question I am asking."

"It is a good question," Esther said, "and it is not the one you came for, and I will answer the one you came for tomorrow, and today you may have that one." She turned her head. "She did it because I asked her to."

"You asked her to bring you food."

"I asked her what she wanted."

The fire moved. A piece of coal fell into the ash and the cat turned its head at the noise and turned it back again.

"That is not an answer," I said.

"No," Esther said. "It is the answer to a different question, and you are clever enough to know which one, and I am tired enough not to help you." She pushed her cup away. "You have been in this house a quarter of an hour and asked me a great many questions, and not one of them was the one you came with. Go home. Come tomorrow at the same time, and I will tell you about ninety-three, and you will not like it, and you will write it down, because you are your father's daughter."

I got up.

"Mrs. Callard."

"Callard is my father. I am Esther." She stayed in her chair. "You may call me that. There is nobody left to be shocked."

I put my boots on at the door, and they were still wet, and the leather was cold.

"Tomorrow," she said, "come over the bridge. The ford is a young woman's road."

***

I had my hand on the latch when I turned round to say good day, and the wall was not bare.

It was the wall beside the chimney, and it was not a wall a person looked at, because the fire was in front of it and the chair was turned away from it.

There were papers on it. Four of them, gone the colour of weak tea, each held by a single nail, and the nails had rusted and left a stain down the plaster.

The nearest was a receipt. It was a printed form with the blanks filled in by hand, and the hand was a clerk's, small and upright, and the ink had faded to brown, and the stamp at the corner had been pressed so hard that the paper had split.

*Received of Esther Callard, of Barrowfield, in the county aforesaid, spinster, the sum of twenty pounds, being the recognizance entered into by her for the prosecution of an appeal against an order of removal, and estreated at the General Quarter Sessions of the Peace holden at Ely.*

At the foot of it there was a date in the Michaelmas of 1794, and a signature, and below the signature, in a different hand, the one word *paid*.

I stood with my hand on the latch and read it twice, and the second time I read it the way I read a document, which is from the bottom up, looking for what has been left out.

Nothing had been left out. It was twenty pounds, and it had been taken, and the paper that said so had been nailed to the wall at the height of a woman's eyes, where she would see it every time she turned her head.

"That is the wall," Esther said, behind me. "You have found it. Most people do not, because most people are looking at me."

I did not turn round.

"It has been there since the year it was given to me," she said. "I nailed it up myself. I had a hammer in my hand and it took me an hour, and I have not taken it down, and when I am dead the trust may burn it with the rest."

---

## Thirteen · The Clock

The bridge was a different road in the morning. It was the road I had walked every Wednesday of my childhood with a basket on my arm and my mother a step ahead of me, and the parapet was warm on the flat of the stone where the sun had been at it since six, and there was a man mending the far end of it with a bar and a bucket, and he stopped and touched his cap and let me by.

The bar was down across the road at the toll house. The boy was at it.

He was fifteen or thereabouts and he had a bad eye that turned out and a coat too big for him, and he had his hand on the chain and he was watching the road the way a dog watches a gate, which is to say without any thought in it at all.

"Good morning," I said.

He looked at me and said something that had the shape of a greeting and none of the bones of one, and his hand stayed on the chain.

"I am going to the house."

He nodded, and did not lift the bar.

"Is there a toll for a person on foot?"

He thought about this. He thought about it for long enough that a cart came up behind me and the man in it called out, and the boy went to the bar and dropped it and took a coin and wrote nothing down, and the cart went through, and he came back and put his hand on the chain again.

He lifted the bar.

"Nothing," he said. "For a person."

"Thank you."

"Nobody comes," he said, and put the bar back down after me, and stood with his hand on the chain again.

***

The door was open this time. Not wide. The width of a boot.

I stood on the cinder path and looked at it, and the morning was already warm enough for the flies, and there was a hen somewhere behind the house that had not been there the day before, and the smoke went up straight.

"Come in or go home," Esther said, from inside. "You are letting the heat out."

I came in. She was at the table with the toll book open and a pen in her hand. She did not look up. She wrote a line and blotted it and closed the book.

"Shut the door."

I shut the door.

"The bolt as well. It is not for you. It is for the door."

I put the bolt across.

"Sit in the chair you sat in."

I sat in the chair I had sat in.

"You may take your coat off. Not because I am warm. Because you will be, in an hour, and a woman who sits in a coat in a warm room in May is a woman who is thinking about leaving."

I took my coat off. I hung it on the back of the chair.

"That is better," she said. "Now. You have come over the bridge."

"I have."

"You paid the boy."

"He said there was nothing for a person."

"There is nothing for a person. There is a rate for a horse, a rate for a cart, a rate for a score of sheep, and nothing for a man on his own legs, and that is the only piece of sense in the whole turnpike law." She put the pen down. "The trust has been trying to put a rate on foot passengers for years, and I have been writing to them about it for years, and they have not answered one letter, and I keep writing, and the boy takes the letters down and the man at the quarter day takes them away, and one day there will be a rate on foot passengers and it will be my fault for suggesting it."

"Why did you suggest it?"

"Because a thing that is free is a thing that nobody counts," she said, "and a thing that nobody counts is a thing that can be done away with in a room in Ely by three men who have never been over this ford."

***

She got up and went to the clock.

"The clock is wound on Sundays," she said. "It is Monday. You may watch me do the book instead."

"I would rather watch the clock."

"The clock is not doing anything. The clock was wound yesterday and it will be wound next Sunday and in between it does the only thing it knows." She opened the case with a small key on a string. "Come here. If you are going to look at a thing, look at it properly."

I went and stood beside her.

The case was deep and dark and smelled of oil and old wood, and the weights hung on their lines, one at the top of its fall and one a third of the way down, and the pendulum went across the bottom with a brass bob the size of a fist, and every time it went across it made the noise I had been listening to through the door.

The pendulum went on.

"The striking weight is the one that is low," she said. "It goes down a little every hour, and on the seventh day it is near the floor, and I wind it up, and it starts again. That is the machine. A weight goes down and a wheel goes round and a hand goes round and a bell rings, and none of it knows what time it is."

"It keeps good time."

"It keeps the time I set it to." She shut the case and locked it. "There is a man at Wendon who has a clock that is never wrong by more than a minute in a year, and he is proud of it, and he has been in his house for thirty years and has not been to Wendon in twenty, and I have never understood what he wants the minute for."

"And you want the quarter of an hour."

"I want to know what time it is going to be," she said, "before it is."

***

She made the tea. It was the same pot and the same cosy and the same chipped cup, and this time she put a second cup on the table for herself and sat down opposite me, and the cat came out from under the chair and got onto her lap without being invited and settled there like a hen.

"Now," she said. "You have a list of questions in your head and you have had it since the day you wrote to me, and you may ask them, and I will tell you what I do with questions."

"What do you do with them?"

"I give them back."

"That is not an answer."

"It is the answer to the question you asked." She drank her tea. "Ask."

I had a list. I had had it for a year, written out in my own hand on the back of a copy of a suspension, and I had rehearsed it on the road and in the bed at the inn, and now I was in the room and the list had gone out of my head the way a list goes when the room is not the room you had imagined.

"Were you born in this house?" I said.

"Were you born in yours?"

"Yes."

"Then you know what it is to be born in a house." She put her cup down. "Ask another."

"Why do you keep the gate?"

"Who would keep it?"

"The trust would find a man."

"And what would he do with the house?"

"He would live in it."

"He would live in it," she said, "and he would put his wife in it, and his wife would want the window, and the window is where the book is kept, and the book is where the money is counted. Ask another."

"Are you afraid of the parish?"

"Are you?"

"No."

"Then you have not been in it long enough." She smoothed the cat's ear. "Ask another."

I asked another, and she gave it back, and I asked another, and she gave that one back too, and after a while the words stopped being words and became moves in a game, a woman at a table turning every question round the way a person turns a plate to see the pattern on the bottom.

"Why do you never go out?" I said.

"Why do you?"

"Because somebody would come after me."

She looked at me then, and the look was not unkind and it was not amused.

"That is the first answer you have given me," she said. "You have been asking me questions for an hour and every one of them was a way of not answering one of mine. That is a trade, and it is a good trade, and I have been in it for forty years, and I know the price of it better than you do."

"I did not know I was doing it."

"No," she said. "That is what makes it a trade and not a trick."

I let it alone.

***

The fire was low. The cat got down and went to the hearth and sat with its back to the grate, and I thought about the list on the back of the copy, and about the twenty pounds on the wall behind me, and about the fact that I had been in that room an hour and learned nothing except the way the woman turned a plate.

The tea was cold.

"May I ask you about 1793," I said.

The clock ticked. She did not move her hands from the cup.

"Did your mother not tell you?" she said.

"No."

Nobody spoke at all. The fire made a small noise, a fly came in at the window and went out again, and I began to count the ticks and got to a number and lost it and started again.

Then she said, "Then she had more patience than I gave her credit for."

"Mrs. Callard."

"Esther."

"Esther. I have come a hundred and eleven miles."

"I know how far you have come. I know the road and I know the inns on it and I know what it costs, and I know that you came over the ford barefoot yesterday like a woman bringing a basket, and I know what that was for." She set the cup down. "You came to be told a thing. I am not going to tell it to you today."

"Why not?"

"Because you asked me a question."

"That is not a reason."

"It is the only reason I have," she said, "and it is a better one than you think. A person who is told a thing sits down and listens and goes away with it, and it is a parcel, and parcels get put on shelves. A person who is kept waiting for a thing has to decide, every day, whether she still wants it, and by the fourth day she knows what it is worth to her, and that is a thing no telling can give her."

"I have waited a year."

"You have waited a year for a letter to be answered," Esther said. "That is not the same as waiting for a thing to be given to you by a person who is in the room. Ask me tomorrow. Today I am going to my bed."

"It is eleven in the morning."

"It is eleven in the morning," she said, "and I am sixty, and I lie down in the afternoon, and I have lain down in the afternoon for forty years, and the parish may say what it likes about it."

She got up. The cat got up with her.

"One thing," she said. "You may look at the desk. You have been looking at it since you came in, and a woman who looks at a desk for an hour without asking about it is either a fool or a person who has already decided what is on it."

***

The desk was under the gable window, and it was not a desk. It was a table with a lid, and the lid was open and propped on a stick, and inside there were pigeonholes with papers in them and a row of little drawers with brass rings.

On the top there was a stone bottle of ink with a cork in it, a pen knife with a handle worn to the shape of a thumb, a pounce box, a candle in a stick, and a pen lying across the ink bottle, dry.

I stood in front of it with my hands at my sides.

"May I touch it?"

"You may touch a table," Esther said, from the fire. "It is not a person."

I put my hand on the edge of the lid, on the wood at the corner where a hand would go if it were pulling the lid up, and the wood was smooth and had gone dark with use.

The room went the colour of milk.

There was a girl at the table. She was nineteen or thereabouts, and her sleeve was pinned back at the wrist so that it would not take the ink, and she wrote fast, with her head down and her shoulder up, and the light came through the gable window onto the paper in a white square, and behind her, in the corner where the bed was, there was a man's breathing, slow and wet and going on.

She did not look up. She dipped the pen and wrote and dipped the pen and wrote, and the candle beside her had not been lit because the light was enough, and on the floor by her chair there was a plate with a piece of bread on it and the bread had not been touched.

Then the milk went out of the air, and the room was a room, and the fire was a fire, and there was a fly at the window and the clock was going.

My hand was still on the corner of the lid.

The wood was warm.

"You did something," Esther said.

I took my hand away.

"The room went cold," she said. "It does that. It has done it before, when the trust's man comes, and once when the vicar came, and it did it the day the receipt was given to me, and I have never known what it is, and I have never asked, because a house is allowed one thing that is not explained."

I stood with my back to her and my hand at my side.

"I read things," I said.

"I know what you do. The whole county knows what you do, and half of it does not believe it, and the other half crosses itself." A chair moved. "You read the desk."

"I read the desk."

"And what did it tell you?"

"That a girl wrote at it in daylight with her sleeve pinned back, and that there was a man dying in the corner, and that she did not eat her bread."

Esther was quiet for a moment.

"That is accurate," she said. "It is more accurate than the parish, and the parish has had forty years to get it wrong."

I turned round.

"Whose desk is it?"

"It is mine," she said. "It was my father's and it is mine, and it will be the trust's, and it will be sold with the rest, and whoever buys it will put it in a warm room and put a pot of flowers on it and never know that a man died six feet from it with his mouth open."

***

She went to the bed behind the curtain and drew the curtain back a hand's width, and there was a bed with a quilt on it and a pillow, and she sat down on the edge of it with her hands on her knees like a woman at a fair who has paid to sit.

"Tomorrow," she said. "Not in the morning. In the afternoon, at three, when I have had my sleep and the boy has been and the gate is quiet. You will come over the bridge and you will knock at the door and I will let you in and I will tell you about ninety-three from the beginning, and you will not interrupt me, and you will not ask me why I did any of it until I have finished, and when I have finished you may ask me anything you like and I will answer it."

"Why not now?"

"Because I have not said it aloud since the year it happened," Esther said, "and a thing that has not been said aloud for forty years has to be got ready, the way a room has to be got ready for a guest, and I am too old to be hurried and too proud to be seen getting ready."

She lay back on the quilt. Her boots were on.

"Shut the door after you," she said. "Not the bolt. The boy comes at four."

I put my coat on.

"Esther."

"Go home."

"I will come at three."

"You will come at three," she said, and closed her eyes, and the cat came and got onto the bed and walked up to her shoulder and turned round twice and lay down. The cat slept on. The room was quiet. I let myself out and shut the door.

---

## Fourteen · 1793

She was ready for me. That was the first thing.

The room had been swept and the table cleared, and the toll book was shut and on the shelf, and there was a clean cloth with two cups and a jug of water, and the chair by the fire had been turned to face the other chair, the two of them set against each other like a pair of scales.

The fire was out. The grate had been blackleaded, and the room was cool and smelled of a room that has been opened.

"You are early," she said.

"It is three."

"It is three by my clock, which is a quarter of an hour ahead, so it is a quarter to three, and you are early." She sat down. "Sit."

I sat.

"You have brought your husband."

"He is at the gate."

"He is always at the gate. Does he ever come in?"

"When he is asked."

"Then I will not ask him."

She poured the water. Her hand was steady, and she poured it as a person pours who has poured a great many jugs.

"Now," she said. "You will not interrupt me. I will tell it from the beginning, in the order it happened, and if you interrupt me I will go back to the beginning, and I am sixty, and there is a limit to how many beginnings I have in me."

"I will not interrupt."

"You will," she said. "You are your mother's daughter and your father's too, and you will interrupt, and when you do I will stop, and you will wait, and then I will go on."

She put the jug down and folded her hands on the table.

***

"In ninety-three I was nineteen years old. I was not pretty and I was not clever and I could write a fair hand, which in that house was worth more than either. My father was the clerk of the parish and had been the clerk for thirty years. He had the chest and the books and the key, and a cancer in his stomach, and he was dying in the corner of this room, on that bed, and he took a long time about it."

She said it without any weight on any of the words, the way a woman reads out a bill.

"The parish was removing people that year the way a farmer thins turnips. The wheat came up short and the potatoes rotted in the clamp, and the roads were full of people walking to somewhere else, and every parish on the road was pushing them to the next one, and the next one was pushing them back. A removal order is not a punishment. It is a parish putting a person over a boundary and hoping the next parish will keep her."

"And Tace came in the March."

"She came in the February. She was thirty years old and she came with a child."

"The paper says she had no child living."

"You have interrupted me."

Esther looked at me for a moment. Then she went on.

"The paper was written in the March," Esther said. "The child was buried in the February. Both of those things are true, and I wrote the second one down myself, and I have never been able to say the sentence without stopping in the middle of it."

She looked at the window.

"The child was a girl. She was about two years old and had a cough that would not go, and Tace carried her from Nethercote, which is a long road in a wet year, and the child was dead within the week. The parish buried her in the corner of the yard where they put the ones with no settlement. No stone was set and no fee was asked. I wrote the line in the book in my own hand and wrote it badly, because my hand was shaking, and my father came on it afterwards and said, that is a burial and it will have to be paid for. That was all he said."

The pendulum swung. The cat came in from the garden and sat down in the doorway and began to wash its face.

"Go on," I said.

"I am going on. Do not hurry me." She drank. "The overseer was a man called Sadler, and he was not a bad man. He came to me in the March with the order in his hand and asked me to write the examination, because my father could not sit up. So I sat at that desk with the light coming in and asked her the questions that are in the form, and she answered them, and I wrote them down."

"Do you remember what she said?"

"I remember every word, because I have said it to myself in the dark for forty years. She was born at Nethercote. She was never bound apprentice and never hired for a year. She had no child living. And when I had written that last one she put her hand out and touched the paper at the corner and said, write it again. I said, it is written. She said, write it again, because it is the only true thing on the page."

Esther's finger was on the table, at the corner of an imaginary sheet.

"I wrote it again. It is in the chest at Tindle with the rest, in my hand, twice, once in the form and once under it where there was no line for it."

***

The bar went down outside.

Esther got up and went to the window and put her head out.

"Cart," she said. "Laden."

A man's voice, and then a longer answer.

"You may call it a wagon if you like," she said. "It will still be a cart, and the rate is the rate, and I am at my dinner and the book is shut."

The coin went in. The bar went up. The cart went through, and Esther shut the sash and came back and sat down.

"In the March," she said, "Tindle appealed. Not Nethercote. Nethercote was never a party to anything. Tindle."

"Why?"

"Because Sadler was not a fool. A removal order costs money to execute. You feed the person on the road and pay the constable and hand her over at the other end, and the other end may refuse her, and then you have her on the road again. An appeal costs nothing but the writing, and it buys time, and time is what a parish is always short of in March."

"And the appeal needed a recognizance."

"The appeal needed a recognizance of twenty pounds with sureties, and the parish had no twenty pounds to put on a recognizance, because a parish never has twenty pounds for anything but a bridge." She looked at me. "Do you know what a recognizance is?"

"I know what it is."

"Then say it, so that I know you know."

"It is a bond. A person puts down money and promises to appear, and if they do not appear the money is forfeit."

"It is a promise with a price on it," Esther said, "and the price is not paid by the person who makes the promise. It is paid by whoever put the money down. That is the law of recognizances, and it took me an afternoon to learn and forty years to understand."

She got up again and went to the desk and opened the lid and took a paper out of one of the little drawers, and came back and put it on the table between us.

It was a single skin, folded in three, and the folds had gone brittle, and she opened it with the flat of her hand, easing each crease before she turned it.

I knew it before she had it flat. I had seen its brother in a parish chest at Tindle, tied in a bundle with a blue ribbon, and the hand on it was the same hand that had written to me in October, only younger, and faster, and not yet leaned the way it leaned now.

*And the said churchwardens and overseers having appealed against the said order, and having entered into a recognizance in the sum of twenty pounds with sureties for the prosecution of the said appeal, the execution of the said order is hereby suspended until the said appeal shall be heard and determined.*

"I wrote that," she said. "I wrote it at that desk with my father breathing in the corner, and I wrote it in the form the clerk of the peace uses, and I put my own name at the foot of it where the surety goes, and I put the twenty pounds down."

"Where did the twenty pounds come from?"

"From my mother. She died when I was a girl and left me twenty pounds and a brooch, and the brooch is on my throat and the twenty pounds was in the bank at Ely, and I had never drawn a penny of it, and I drew all of it in one morning and carried it to the sessions in a canvas bag."

"Did you tell your father?"

"He was past telling." She folded the paper along its old folds. "He knew the money was in the bank. He did not know it was gone. I have thought about that more than I have thought about the money."

***

"Now," she said. "This is the part you have come for, and it is short."

She put the paper down.

"The sessions were at Ely at the Easter. The appeal was entered and the recognizance taken and the suspension granted, and Tace stayed in the cottage in the turning, and I went home and sat with my father and waited for the parish to be heard."

"Did they go?"

"Sadler went. He went to Ely on the Tuesday with the order and the recognizance in a leather case and came back on the Thursday, and I met him in the lane and asked when the hearing was, and he said Friday, and that the parish would be represented."

"By whom?"

"By a lawyer from Ely. Sadler had engaged him, and the fee was to come out of the parish books, and the vestry had agreed it." She turned her cup round. "On the Friday the case was called and nobody stood up."

"Nobody."

"Nobody. The lawyer was not there and Sadler was not there and the churchwardens were not there. The clerk of the peace called the case twice and struck it out. When an appeal is struck out the suspension falls, and when the suspension falls the order stands, and when the order stands the recognizance is estreated." She said the last word carefully, the way a person says a word in a foreign language they have learned by heart. "Estreated. It is a good word. It means the money is taken out of the bond and given to the King, and the King does not give it back."

"And you?"

"I was at home with my father. I had it in the June from the man at the turn, who had it from a carrier." She drank. "My father died in the July. He died in the afternoon and I was at the desk, and when I turned round he had stopped breathing, and the clock was striking, and I have never liked that hour since."

The clock stood a quarter of an hour ahead, and the room was quiet.

"I want to be exact about the money," Esther said. "It was not stolen. It was estreated, which is to say it went where the law says it goes when a person does not keep a promise. The person who did not keep the promise was the parish. The person who paid was me. There is no paper in England that says the parish owed me a penny, and there is a paper on that wall that says I paid."

***

"Why did they not come?" I said.

"I have three answers and none of them is comfortable." She held up one finger. "One. They never meant to come. The appeal was to buy the winter, and the suspension was the point, and the hearing would have cost them a fee for a case they did not want to win."

"Two."

"Two. Sadler died in the August, of the same thing that took my father, and the new overseer found a case in the chest with no man to explain it, and did what a new man does with an old paper."

"Three."

"Three." She put her hand flat on the table. "A quashed order would have been the worst thing that could happen to them. Tace would have been a woman with no order against her and no settlement, and a parish may not pay a person it has no obligation to. The overseers may not enter her in the book, and the eight pounds a year would have been unlawful. So they let the order stand, and paid her under it for forty years, and every year they paid they were keeping her exactly where she was."

I sat with my hands folded.

"That is the third answer," she said, "and it is the one I believe, and I have believed it since about the year twelve, when I worked out the arithmetic of it, and I have never said it aloud to anybody, because there is nobody in this county who would want to hear it."

"Then they used your money to buy the thing you were trying to prevent."

"They used my money to buy the winter," Esther said, "and in the winter they decided that the thing I was trying to prevent suited them, and they did it slowly and lawfully and in a book, and after forty years it is a custom of the parish. There is no villain in it anywhere. That is the part nobody can bear. There is a vestry and a book and a girl of nineteen who thought the world would do the right thing with twenty pounds."

The cat finished washing its face and came across the floor and got onto her lap, and she put her hand on it.

"Now you may ask," she said. "I have finished."

***

I sat. The room was cool. The clock ticked and the jug had made a ring on the cloth.

"May I ask about the day you wrote it?"

"You may."

"Did you write the suspension before you asked her?"

She looked at me.

"What?"

"Before you put the twenty pounds down," I said. "Did you write the paper first and then go and tell her, or did you go and tell her first?"

Esther sat with her hand on the cat.

"Why does that matter?"

"Because a paper is a thing a person does alone in a room," I said, "and a promise is a thing a person does in front of somebody. I have spent three years reading papers that men wrote alone, and every one was lawful and every one did harm, and the difference is whether anybody was asked."

She was quiet for long enough that the clock did a whole minute.

"I went to the cottage on the Wednesday afternoon, with the paper in my basket and the pen and the ink, and I sat on the stool by the door because there was one chair and she was in it, and I told her what I had done and what it would cost and what I thought would happen."

"And what did she say?"

"She said, why."

"And what did you say?"

"I said, because you have been here four months and nobody has asked you anything." Esther's hand was still on the cat. "And she said, that is not a reason, that is a kindness, and a kindness is a thing that is done to a person. And I said, then tell me what to do. And she said, no. I said, why not. And she said, because if I tell you what to do you will do it and it will be mine, and if you do it because you think it is right it will be yours, and I have had things done to me all my life and I would like one thing done for me that somebody chose."

I did not say anything.

"So I asked her," Esther said. "I sat on the stool with the paper in my lap and I asked her what she wanted." She looked up. "That is the thing your mother did for me, and the thing I have never been able to do for anybody else, and I have had forty years to think about it and I still cannot do it."

"Why not?"

"Because it is easier to give a person a thing than to ask them what they want," she said, "and easier still to give them a thing and call it what they want. The parish has done that to me for forty years. The trust does it. The vicar comes up that path with a blanket and a prayer and never once asks whether I want either."

She lifted the cat off her lap and put it on the floor and stood up.

"Ask me the last one," she said. "You have been holding it since you came in and it is the only one that matters and you have been too polite to spend it."

"Why did you do it?"

The clock went on. She stood with her hands at her sides and looked at me, sixty and small and straight, and she had been nineteen once in this room with a dying man behind her and a paper in her hand.

"Because I asked her what she wanted," she said.

---

## Fifteen · What She Asked

"Because I asked her what she wanted."

She said it to the table, with her hands flat on the cloth on either side of the cup, and then she stopped, and the stopping was not the end of a sentence. It was the end of a thing she had carried up a stair and set down.

I let the quiet alone. In that house I had learned that a question put into a hurry comes back with the hurry on it, and a question put into a silence comes back changed.

The room was cold. The grate had been blackleaded in the morning and left empty, and the sun had gone off the toll board and up the wall, so that the white letters had lost the light which made them stand out of the black.

"You are cold," she said.

"I am not cold."

"You are sitting with your arms against your sides. That is a woman who is cold and has decided not to say so." She pushed her chair back. "I will make up the fire, and you will not offer to help. You would do it wrong and I would have to sit here and be civil about it."

She went down on her knees at the grate with a noise she did not try to hide, and took two sticks out of the box and laid them across each other with the unthinking exactness of a hand that has done it all its life, and took a spill from the jar and lit it at the candle and put it in under the bark. The flame ran along the underside and took hold and made a small sound, paper being handled in another room.

"Coal after," she said. "Not now. Coal on a young fire is a way of putting it out and calling it thrift."

She stayed on the floor with her hands out to the flame, and the flame stood up between her fingers, and the swollen knuckles threw a shadow on the hearth that was larger than the hand.

"Now," she said. "Ask it in the words you would use if you were writing it down."

"I am not writing today."

"You are always writing. You have a hand in your pocket and it is not a hand that is doing nothing." She got up with one hand on the fender. "Ask."

"What did she say?"

"She said a sentence."

"Say the sentence."

"It took her a long while to come to it," Esther said, "and when she came to it she said it once, low, the way a person says a thing they have already decided not to say twice. I have not said it aloud since."

"Then say it now."

She stood with her back to the fire.

"Not yet," she said. "There is a box on the shelf under the window, behind the tin. Bring it. The lid is warped and it will fight you, and you will not force it, because it is an old box and I have had it longer than I have had the grate."

***

I brought it. It was small and the wood had been planed thin, and the lid was warped so that one corner stood up off the rest. I eased it with the flat of my thumb and it came.

Inside there was a piece of flannel, and inside the flannel there was a cup.

It was horn, turned, and the rim on one side was worn down until the lip of it was thin as a coin. There was a crack in the side and the crack had been mended with silver wire, and the wire had been put in by somebody who knew the work and expected the cup to outlast the repair.

"A cup," I said.

"I can see that you can see that it is a cup."

"Why is it in a box?"

"Because it is not in use." She sat down again. "I lent it to her in the February. She gave it back in the April, washed, and I put it in the box, and I have not drunk out of it since."

"Why not?"

"Because it is a cup, and there are others on the shelf, and a person does not keep a thing for the reason you are thinking."

"May I take it out?"

"You may touch a cup," she said. "It is not a person."

I lifted it out of the flannel. The horn was warm. It held the warmth of something carried against a body for a long while, and then it was warmer than that.

The kitchen went away.

What came in its place was a room with a fire in it and a woman's arm and a child laid back along the arm, and the cup at the small mouth, tipped a little, and the wrist turning to keep the level, and a cloth under the chin, and the hand waiting, and the hand going on waiting, and the firelight on the underside of the jaw, and then the cup coming down and the edge of the cloth wiping the mouth and the chin and the place under it where the milk had run.

Then the kitchen came back. The fire was young and there was no coal on it, and Esther was in her chair with her hands in her lap. My hand was still open.

"It was used to put something into a child's mouth," I said.

"Yes."

She did not ask how.

"Water."

"Water with sugar in it. I had a paper of sugar at the back of the shelf and I put the whole paper in, and she took it in sips and it stayed down, and that was the last of the sugar in this house."

I set the cup down on the table, on the flannel, and I set it down slowly because my hand was not steady and I did not care to have that looked at.

"You have bought none since," I said.

"No."

"Not in forty years."

"It did not seem to me that a house wanted sugar," Esther said, "after that."

The cup sat between us. Neither of us touched it.

Nobody came to the door. The cat came in through the back door, which stood open the width of a hand, and it had something in its mouth that was still moving. Esther got up and took the cat by the scruff with one hand and took the thing out of its mouth with the other, and carried it to the door and put it out in the garden, and shut the door, and wiped her hand on her apron, and came back and sat down. None of it took her longer than it takes to say a sentence.

"It was a young rabbit," she said. "It will do well enough. The cat is a good cat and I do not blame it and I do not let it finish in the house."

She put her hand out and turned the cup round on the flannel so that the mended side faced away from me.

"Now," she said. "The cup is out of the box. Put it back, and then I will tell you about the Wednesday."

***

"You have it in your head that I went down that lane to get a mark on a paper," Esther said. "I had the paper in my lap the whole of that afternoon and I never put it on the table. The pen was in the basket with the ink, and I did not take out either of them."

"What did you do with the paper?"

"Held it. I sat on the stool with my knees up and the paper in my lap under my hands, and I asked her a question, and the question had no paper in it."

Her hands did not move.

"Tell me the lane."

"It had rained all morning. The lane was wet to the ankle and the rooks were at the elm behind the barn, and there was a woman at the well at the top of the turning, and she looked at me and I looked at her and neither of us spoke. The lane was empty. A clerk's daughter going down that lane in the afternoon was something a woman at a well would remember and not something she would ask about."

"The cottage."

"One room and a lean-to. There was no garden and no pump and no fence, and the door stood open, which in that lane meant there was nothing inside worth shutting a door on. She was in the chair by the table. She was not doing anything. She had the look of a person who has finished the work there is and has not the money for the work there is not."

"She did not get up."

"She did not get up. She did not tell me to sit. I sat on the stool by the door because there was one chair and she was in it." Esther turned her cup a little on the cloth. "The stool was low. My knees came up near my chin like a girl at a fair, and the basket was on the floor between us with the pen lying in it, and she looked at the basket once and then she looked at me and did not look at it again."

"Did she ask what was in it?"

"No. She asked why I had come."

"And you said?"

"I said I had come to ask her something. And she said, ask it, then, and do not sit there being sorry for me, because I can smell sorry from the door."

I put my hands together in my lap. The fire had taken the coal by then and the room had begun to be warm at the front and cold at the back, which is how a room is when the fire has been out all day.

"You had been in that office since you could hold a pen," I said. "You had been writing examinations since you were a girl. Did you not know how to begin?"

"I knew how to begin an examination. You put the book on the table and you say, what is your name, and where were you born, and the person answers and you write it down, and at no point in the whole proceeding does anybody have to decide anything. That is the beauty of a form. It asks the questions that have answers."

"And this was not a form."

"This was a woman in a chair with her hands in her lap and no child in the house and the parish wanting her over a boundary, and I had a question with no line under it."

"Say the question."

"I said, what do you want."

"And she said nothing."

"She said nothing. It went on long enough that the fire went down between us and the light went off the wall, and I began to be afraid that I had done a stupid thing and that she was sitting there waiting for me to be done with it. Then I decided to sit in the fear and not fill the room with my own voice, and that was the hardest work I ever did in a room with another person in it."

"How long?"

"Long enough for me to hear a cart go up the lane and come back and go up again."

I have thought since about that room. It had a chair and a table and a stool and a bed in the corner with a quilt on it, and a fire that was going out, and a window the size of a book. The child was in the yard by then, under the north wall where the ground is soft, and there was no stone on her and there would be no stone. Nobody had said her name in that room since the burial. Tace did not say it while I was there. She did not cry, either. She sat with her hands in her lap and her thumbs together and she did not look away from me, and I have thought about that more than I have thought about the money.

I did not say anything.

"Then she said it," Esther said. "She said, a place they cannot move me from."

I did not write it down.

"Say it again."

"No."

"Why not?"

"Because it does not improve," Esther said. "A sentence that is said twice is a sentence somebody is trying to make you agree with."

The clock went on, set ahead by its quarter of an hour, and when it struck it struck at a time that had not arrived, and neither of us moved while it did it.

"That is the answer," Esther said. "It is not a clever sentence. There is nothing in it that a lawyer could use and nothing in it that a vestry could vote on. I have carried it forty years and it has not worn out."

"It is not a thing any person could give her."

"No." She looked at the fire. "And I was nineteen years old, and I had twenty pounds in a bank at Ely, and a father dying in the corner of this room, and I believed that a girl with twenty pounds and a fair hand could build a wall round a woman."

"You told her you would do it."

"I told her I would see what could be done. There is a difference, and I have had forty years to look at the difference, and the difference is that one of them is a promise and the other is a girl going home to think. She did not thank me."

"You went home and thought."

"I went home and did the arithmetic on it," Esther said, "which is not thinking. It is the appearance of thinking, done in figures, and it is a great deal easier, and a person can do it sitting down."

The fire settled. A piece of coal dropped and the cat turned its head at the noise and turned it back.

"It was the most expensive answer that was ever given to me," Esther said, "and I did not know the price of it until the money was gone, and by the time the money was gone the price had gone up."

Neither of us moved.

***

The paper stayed folded in my pocket until the fire had taken the new coal and the room had begun to go the colour that a room goes at the end of an afternoon in May, when the sun is behind the hill and the light comes off the water instead of out of the sky.
