I took it out. I did it without deciding to, the way a hand goes to a pocket.

She looked at it and did not put out her hand.

"What is that?"

"A paper."

"I know a paper when I see one. What is on it?"

"A line and a space."

"Whose hand?"

"Mine."

She waited. The fire came up blue at the edge of the coal and settled to red.

"What I want is," I said.

"And nothing after it."

"Nothing after it."

Esther looked at the paper for a while. Then she held out her hand, and I gave it to her, and she turned it over and looked at the back, which was blank, and gave it back.

"That is the worst thing I have seen in this house," she said, "and there is a receipt on that wall that took my mother's money."

"Esther."

"Put it away. A paper that is taken out and looked at and put away goes soft at the folds, and then one day it tears, and a torn paper is a decision." She got up and went to the window and stood at the counter with her back to me. "Go home. The light is going and the boy is at the bar and I want my tea."

I put the paper away. I put my coat on. She did not turn round. The cup was on the table on its flannel, and the fire was going, and the room smelled of coal and horn and the bread under its cloth.

At the door I turned round.

"Esther. After her. Did you ask anyone else?"

"Ask the town," she said.

"I am asking you."

"Ask the town," she said again, and she said it without heat, the way she said the rate through the window. "There is not a household in Barrowfield that has not had me at the gate with that question in my mouth. I have asked in yards and at back doors and once across a coffin, and they will tell you that the toll woman asks questions and does not wait for the answer, and they are wrong about the last part and I have never corrected them."

"Why not?"

"Because an answer is a thing a person gives you," Esther said, "and a person who repeats it has taken it back."

She was at the window with her back to me, and beyond her the light was going off the water, and the bar was down across the road, and the boy stood at the end of it with his hand on the chain, looking the way he looked at everything, which was without any thought in it at all.

"Every door," she said. "Every door in the town, and not one of them had been asked before."

---

## Sixteen · The Promise

The oven was at its heat. She had it open and the ash was coming out into a bucket, and she was on her knees in front of it with the long rake, and the sweat was on her forehead and she did not wipe it.

"You are early," she said, without turning round.

"It is the morning."

"It is the morning, and the oven is at its heat, and the loaf is ready and the oven will not wait and I will not be hurried." She drew the last of the ash out and set the rake against the wall. "Stand there. Do not talk to me until it is in."

I stood there. The dough was in the trough under a cloth, and it had come up and gone down again the way dough does when it has been left a little long, and she turned it out on the table and knocked it back with the heel of her hand, and the sound of it was a sound out of my childhood.

She shaped it and scored the top with a knife and slid the peel under it and put it in, and then she took a wad of dough and stopped the oven door with it, pressing the edges down with her thumb.

"That is the whole art," she said. "The door."

She wiped her hands. She looked at me for the first time that morning.

"You have brought the book."

"I have brought nothing."

"You have brought the book and the ink and you have left them in the basket in the passage, because you have begun to be careful of me, and it is a little late for that and I do not mind it." She went to the table and sat down. "Bring the basket in. You may put the book on the table. I am not going to take it out of your hands and throw it in the fire, and I would like it understood that I could."

I brought the basket in. The book was heavy. I set it on the table, and she looked at it the way she looked at a cart through the window, which is to say long enough to fix the rate and no longer.

"There is no paper," she said.

"For what?"

"For any of it." She folded her hands. "I have looked for forty years. There is the toll book, which belongs to the trust and has my hand in it and not one word about a woman in a cottage. There is the receipt on the wall, which is a receipt. There is the suspension, which I have, and the order, which the parish has, and neither of them says what was agreed in this town. There is nothing else. You may write down the names. It will do you no good."

"Show me the book."

She got up and took it off the shelf and put it on the table and opened it at the last page with writing on it, and turned it round to face me. The page was ruled. The ink was brown, and the entries stood in columns: the day, the thing, the sum. Her hand at the top of the book was young and her hand at the bottom was old, and no single line of it was different from the line above.

"There," she said. "Find her."

I read down the column. Carts. Horses. A score of sheep. A hearse. A drove of geese. A corpse, nothing. There was no name. Nothing in that column but things that had been driven through a gate.

"There is no woman in it," I said.

"There is no woman in it. There is a woman in the chest at Tindle, and there is a woman on the wall behind me, and there is a woman in a cottage at the end of a lane, and there is nothing in this book." She shut the book. The flat of her hand came down on the cover. "That is what a book is for. It is not a record of what happened. It is a record of what was entered."

"Names are a beginning."

"Names without a paper are a story," Esther said. "You have a box of papers at the inn and you have been building a case out of them for a year, and you have come to the one thing in it that has no paper under it, so you have taken out your book."

I put my hand flat on the cover and left it there.

"Then tell me what holds it," I said.

"Seven people."

***

She told it in the order she had walked it, and she told it the way a person reads out a list of payments, without stopping to say what any of it had cost her.

"The smith, down where the lane ends. He had a horse's hoof between his knees and a file in his hand and he did not put the hoof down, and he said yes before I had the sentence out. He said, put it in the book. I said, there is no book. He said, then put it in your head, and do not come back in the winter to tell me I said it."

"The woman at the kiln. She was setting pots out on a board to dry and her hands were black to the wrist and she did not stop working. She asked whether it would be the same money. I said eight pounds a year. She said yes, and then she said, do not ask me again, because I have a boy who will not be able to explain it when I am gone."

"The shop, where Cow Lane turns. The old woman had the scales on the counter with a paper of tea half weighed, and she made me stand until the beam had settled, and then she did the sum out loud. The house at four shillings a week against the eight pounds a year. She said, the parish saves. I said, the parish saves. She said, then yes, and put the tea back on the scale."

"The wheelwright. He said no."

"He said no."

"He said it was not the business of the town what the parish did with its money, and I said that was true, and I went away. He came to my door on the Sunday after with his hat in his hand and said yes, and he did not say why, and I did not ask him."

"Why did you not ask him?"

"Because a man who has changed his mind has done the hardest part already," Esther said, "and a question puts him back at the beginning."

"Did you choose the doors?"

"I went to the ones I owed."

"Owed what?"

"Small things. A horse shod and not paid for in the quarter. A pot mended. A paper I had written for nothing because the fee would have been an insult. I did not go to the ones who owed me. That would have been a trade, and a trade is a thing a person may refuse."

"That is not a promise either."

"No. It is a beginning."

She turned her cup round on the table. The bread was in the oven and the oven was making a small ticking noise as it cooled at the mouth, and the cat came and sat in front of it at a distance it had worked out for itself.

"The ferryman. He was hauling a boat up the hard with a rope over his shoulder and the water running off the rope. He said she was not of this parish. I said she was in it. He stood with the rope in his hands and thought about it, and then he said yes, and then he said, not a penny more than the eight. I said, not a penny more."

"The farmer at the long field. He was at his gate with a dog, and he said yes before I had asked, and then he asked me in for a cup and I said no. He said, you will want it in writing. I said no. He said, then you are a braver woman than the last one, and I did not ask him what he meant."

"The woman at the end of the row." Esther stopped.

"Go on."

"She had been removed out of this parish when she was a girl. She came back with a certificate and a husband, and the certificate was in a frame over her fire, and she said yes, and then she said, do not tell anybody I said it. I said, why. She said, because a certificate is the only thing I have that nobody can take off me, and a woman who has been moved once does not put her name to anything."

"That is seven."

"That is seven. I walked it in an afternoon and part of an evening, and my boots were wet through by the last door, and I came home and my father was awake and asked me where I had been, and I said, at the doors, and he said, whose, and I said, everybody's, and he said, then you have been about the parish's business, and I said yes, because it was true and because it was not."

I opened the book. I did not write.

"Nobody wrote any of that down."

"Nobody wrote it down."

"Then it is a story."

"It is seven yeses," Esther said, "and a story is a thing that is told by one person. Seven yeses is a thing that is held by seven."

***

"Then why did the parish not go to Ely?" I said.

"They did not go."

"Were they afraid?"

"No. They could add."

She went to the oven and put her hand near the mouth of it. She came back and sat down.

"Put it in columns," she said, "the way your husband would put it, and you will see that there was only one column with nothing in it."

"Show me the columns."

"If they went and won, the order stood and she was moved, and the parish paid the constable and the road and had her off the book, and that was an end of it. That column costs money and settles the matter."

"If they went and lost, the order fell and she stayed, and she was a woman with no order against her and no settlement in the parish, and the parish could not pay her a penny and could not put her out either, and would have had her on the road or in the house. That column costs money and leaves the matter worse than it was."

"And if they did not go, the order stood and the suspension fell and the money went to the King, and nobody had decided anything, and she stayed where she was, and the parish went on paying, and the paying was a thing that had never been voted."

"One column has nothing in it."

"One column has nothing in it," she said, "and that is the one they chose, and they chose it by not writing a letter and not paying a lawyer and not standing up when the case was called."

"That is not a decision."

"No. It is the absence of one, and it takes a great deal of work to arrange, and it is done by men who would be shocked to be told that they had done it." She put her hand flat on the table. "And there is a thing in that column that I did not see for years. An order that stands is a paper that says a woman must be moved. A woman who is not moved is a woman the parish is disobeying its own paper about, every day, lawfully, because nobody makes a parish obey a paper. So they paid her under an order that told them to remove her, and every payment was a small daily disobedience that cost them eight pounds a year, and after forty years the disobedience had become the custom and the paper had become the ornament."

"And the eight pounds was unlawful the whole time."

"From the day the suspension fell. Every year of it."

"And the lawyer's fee?"

"Engaged in the March and not paid in the April, or paid and not used. I never found out which." She turned her cup. "That is the one thing in the whole business I have ever wanted to know out of curiosity, and I have wanted it for forty years, and there is nobody left to ask."

"And nobody was ever charged with anything."

"Nobody was ever charged with anything," Esther said, "because there was nothing to charge. There was a book with a line in it that was not entered, and a vestry that did not meet, and a woman in a cottage who went on being in the cottage. You cannot put a parish in the dock for not writing a line."

The oven was ticking. She got up and took the wad of dough out of the door with the peel and drew the loaf out and knocked on the bottom of it with her knuckle, and it sounded hollow, and she put it on the rack.

"That is done," she said.

***

The boy tapped the glass. Esther opened the sash.

"Coffin," he said.

"On what?"

"Cart."

"Then lift the bar and stand at the side of the road and take your hat off," she said, and shut the sash, and stood at the counter with her hand on the sill.

The cart came on. It came down the road at a walking pace, and the coffin was on it under a cloth, and the man walking at the horse's head had his hat in his hand. The boy stood at the side of the road with his hat off and his coat hanging off one shoulder, and he stayed there until the cart had gone by. The bar was up. The wheels came over the stones by the window and went on. The sound of them went away up the road toward the town. The bar came down.

She did not move. Her hand stayed on the sill.

"Nothing?" I said.

"Nothing."

"The board says a hearse is charged as a carriage."

"That is a hearse. A cart with a coffin on it is a cart, and the trust has never made me take from the dead, and if it ever tells me to I will put the money in the box myself and write it in the book as a gift." She turned round. "Who was it?"

"You did not ask the boy."

"I did not ask him."

She went to the table and sat down and put her hands in her lap.

"The woman at the end of the row," she said. "The seventh door."

"The certificate."

"The certificate." She looked at the rack and the loaf on it. "There are six."

I did not answer.

The room was quiet. The fire was low. The grate had gone down to a red that did not need coal, and the cat had moved off from the oven and was sitting in the middle of the floor with its back to both of us, and the clock was going, and the loaf was cooling on the rack. It smelled of the whole morning.

"Six is not a promise," I said. "Six is a habit that six people are keeping."

"It is a promise that six people are keeping," Esther said. "A habit is what it is called when the last one stops."

"Then it can stop."

"It can stop. It has not stopped." She put her hand out and turned her cup a little. "Do not tell me that six is fewer than seven. I can count. I have been counting since the coffin went past the window, and I will go on counting, and there is nothing in the arithmetic that a lawyer could not do in an afternoon."

I sat with my hands on the book. I did not open it.

"Then what did the twenty pounds buy?"

She thought about it. She did not answer at once, and when she answered she answered as a person answers a question about a bill.

"Not the cottage. The cottage was the parish's and it was never mine to buy, and I never thought it was, whatever the town says." She put her finger on the table and moved it a little, as if she were ruling a line. "It bought a case that had been entered and not heard. That is all it bought. A thing that had begun and had not been finished, and while a thing has not been finished there is still a place in it for somebody to stand up."

"That is not a great deal for twenty pounds."

"No. It is a poor return." She took her finger off the table. "I bought a not-yet-finished, and I have lived in it for forty years, and it is a thin roof, and it has kept the rain off, and I would not advise it to anybody."

---

## Seventeen · The Magistrate

"You will let me speak," Edward said.

"I will let you speak."

"And you will not correct me in front of him when I am describing the procedure, because a justice will explain procedure to a man once and to a woman twice, and then he will ask her whether she has understood, and if you correct him in the middle of it he will decide that you are difficult and he will be polite to you for the rest of the hour and do nothing."

"That is a very cold speech."

"It is a Tuesday speech." He put the leather case on his knee and squared it with the edge of the seat. "I have been a clerk since before you were born, and I know what a room does to a woman who is clever in it. It stops listening and starts watching. You may be as clever as you like after he has said yes."

"And before?"

"Before, you are a wife with a paper, and I am a clerk with a case, and we are the easiest thing in the world to refuse."

The chaise went up the last of the hill. The morning was warm. The town came up on the left with its chimneys and its smell, and the driver took the turn at the top too fast, and Edward put his hand against the strap and did not say anything about it.

"A deposition," I said. "Say it as you would say it to him."

"A statement on oath, taken down in writing by a person who has authority to take it, in the presence of the person who makes it, and signed by that person, and returned to the sessions."

"And if it is not signed?"

"Then it is a conversation, and a conversation is worth what the man who had it says it is worth." He turned the case over. "The difficulty is not the statement. The statement is easy. Anybody can say anything. The difficulty is the taking, and the taking has more than one door in it, and he will open them all and show you that they are locked, and he will do it kindly, and that is the part you must not mistake for a refusal."

"What is it, then?"

"It is a man showing you the shape of the room," Edward said. "If he were refusing you he would not show you anything."

***

The house was at the top of the town on the London side, brick with a white door and a sweep of gravel that had been raked that morning, and a brass bell pull worn bright in the middle by hands.

The maid took Edward's card and put us in a room at the front with a clock in it and a bookcase and a map of the county on the wall, and the fire was laid but not lit, because it was May.

Edward looked at the map. I counted the room.

There was a clock with a seconds hand that jumped, and a bookcase with the law in it in calf, and a table with a newspaper folded and put down, and a chair by the window with the seat worn in one place, and a case of documents on the side table with a brass label on it, and a bell on the table by the door. Nothing was out of place. A room arranged by a person who wanted to know where everything was.

"Mrs. Rooke."

He came in with his hand out. He was a heavy man who had been a thin one, and he had his father's name and not his father's face.

"Mr. Ellwood."

"I am sorry to have kept you. The bench is sitting and a man came up from the mill about a rate, and a mill rate takes longer than a murder." He put us in chairs. The chair was hard. He sat down behind the table. "You have come about the Tindle payment."

"You know of it."

"Mr. Hesketh has written to the bench. I have his letter and I have read it twice." He put his hand flat on the table. "I will tell you what I think of the letter before you tell me anything, so that you know what room you are in. He asks the bench to do nothing. He does not ask for a hearing and he does not ask for an order and he does not ask for advice. He asks that the bench be aware. A letter that asks for nothing is a letter that is arranging something, and I do not care for it."

"Then you are against him."

"I am against a letter that asks for nothing," he said. "That is not the same thing, and you will do me the courtesy of not making it the same thing."

He put out his hand.

Edward gave him the case. He took out the order first. The paper was thin. He read it the way a man reads a thing he has read before, from the top, without moving his lips. He did not look up. Then he turned it over and read the back, and put it down and squared it with the edge of the table.

"This is my father's hand."

"It is."

"And this is not a justice's hand."

"No."

He did not say anything for a moment. He put his finger on the note at the foot of the order and left it there. The room was still.

"I was a boy when this was written," he said. "I remember my father coming home from the sessions at Ely in a bad temper about a removal, and I remember him saying that the second justice had not come and that the thing had been done by a clerk, and that it would stand because nobody would ever look at it." He took his finger off the paper. He let that sit. "He was right about the last part for forty years. He was wrong about it this year, and he is not here to be told."

I kept my hands still.

"Will you take her statement?"

"That is the question, and I am going to answer it in the order that it comes." He sat back. "I will open the doors one at a time, Mrs. Rooke, and you will not care for the shape of any of them."

***

The clerk came to the door with a form and a question about a recognizance, and Ellwood got up and went out into the passage to settle it. The door was open. I was alone in the room with the table and the press and the book.

The book was on the table at his right hand. It was a quarto with a worn corner and a leather cover gone soft at the hinge, and on the front of it, in gold that had been rubbed away at the high points, there was a word I did not need to read. There were forms under it, and a pen, and a box of wafers.

I put my hand on the cover.

The room went white at the edges and then there was another room, with a window and a table and a man standing at the table with his hand flat on the book, and a second hand laid over the top of his, and a thumb pressing down, and a clerk's finger coming in under the hand to hold the place on the page, and the man's shoulders squared and his coat pulled straight, and the book shut, and the clerk writing.

Then the room came back, and the book was a book, and my hand was on it, and the gold letters were under my palm.

The book was still warm. He came in.

"Mrs. Rooke."

"Mr. Ellwood."

"You have your hand on the oath book."

"I have."

"It is not a thing that is usually touched," he said, and sat down and drew it toward him a little, and put it square with the edge of the table, and left it there.

***

"The doors," he said. "The first is whether she can come before me, and she cannot. I will not have a woman who has not crossed her own threshold in forty years brought into a court at Ely in a chair. It would be a spectacle, and a spectacle is not evidence, and the first man who smiled would be a man I would have to put out of the room, and the day would be remembered for that and not for her."

That was the first door.

"The second door."

"The second is whether I may come to her. I may. A justice may take a deposition at a house where the witness cannot travel, and it is done, and it is done lawfully, and I have done it myself, in a case of a broken back." He put his hand out. "It is done on a certificate."

"From a physician."

"From a physician or a surgeon who has seen her and will put his name to the fact that she cannot be moved without danger."

"She cannot be moved without danger."

"She cannot be moved without a decision," Ellwood said. "Those are not the same, and the certificate has to say the second one and not the first. A woman of her age who has not gone out of a door in forty years is not a sick woman. She is a woman who has made an arrangement with her own life, and a surgeon who signs a certificate about an arrangement is a surgeon who has signed something he cannot support."

"Then he will sign it and you will take it."

"The surgeon at Barrowfield is her cousin."

"He is."

"Then he will sign it, because he is an honest man, and Mr. Hesketh will stand up at Michaelmas and say that the certificate comes from her own blood, and the bench will look at the name on the certificate and the name in the parish book and will agree with him, and the paper will be worth less than the breath it took to ask for it." He turned his hand over. "I am not saying it would be false. I am saying it would be true, and that it would not stand, and both would be in the room at the same time."

That was the second door.

"The third door."

"The third door is the one you have not asked about." He drew the order toward him again. "You want her statement. A statement is not a letter. It is taken down, and it is sworn, and it is signed or marked, and it is returned to the sessions and entered, and once it is entered it is not hers any more. It is the court's."

"I know what a deposition is."

"Then you know that it cannot be made without this." He put his finger on the order. "She will have to speak to the order. She will have to say what was done in that office and who did it and on what day, and the moment she says it, it is written, and the writing goes into a book, and the book has this order in it, and the order has one justice's name at the foot of it and a clerk's note where the second should be."

I said nothing.

"She is the last hand alive that wrote any part of it," Ellwood said. "If she speaks, she speaks into the record, and the record will say that the clerk of Tindle put his hand to a thing a clerk may not put his hand to. That is what will be read. Not the dying man in the corner. Not the twenty pounds. A note at the foot of an order in a hand that was not a justice's."

"Her father was dying in the room."

"That will be in the record if she says it," he said, "and a record has no place for a dying father. It has a place for a signature."

That was the third door.

I had nothing to say.

***

He got up and went to the window and stood with his hands behind him, and the light came round the edge of the curtain and lay on the floor in a long square.

"I am not refusing you," he said. "I want that understood, because you will go out of this house and tell your husband that the magistrate refused you, and that is not what happened."

"Then what happened?"

"I have told you what the thing is," he said. "Bring me a certificate from a surgeon who is not her blood, and bring her to the door of her own house to be sworn, and I will come out to her with the book and take her statement in the road if that is what she will allow, and I will return it to the sessions myself. That is not a refusal. That is a list."

"The surgeon at Barrowfield is the only surgeon at Barrowfield."

"Then there is a difficulty," he said, "and difficulties are what the law is made of, and I am sorry for it, and I am not able to be sorry in a way that helps you."

I stood up. Edward stood up.

"Mr. Ellwood."

"Mrs. Rooke."

"One thing, and then I will go. You have been kind, and I would rather you had been rude, because a rude man can be answered." I put my hand on the back of the chair. "You have told me again and again what the law cannot do. Tell me once what it can."

He gave me a look over the top of his spectacles.

"It can put a thing in a book," he said.

"And that is all?"

"That is a great deal. That is what a court is." He came back to the table. "And it is why I have taken an hour with you, and it is why I am going to say the last thing, which you will not like and which you will remember."

"Say it."

"A deposition is entered, Mrs. Rooke. It is not filed in a box with her name on it and given back to her when she asks. It is copied into the records of the sessions and bound with the rest and kept at Ely with the convictions and the recognizances and the examinations, and any man who asks for it may have it, and any man who has it may read it, and there is no part of it that is private and no page of it that can be taken out afterward." He put his hand flat on the table. "You are asking me to take the one thing that woman has never said aloud in forty years and write it into a book that anybody may read. Long after she is dead a man who never met her, who was not born when she was young, will take that book down and read what she said about her father and about that order, and he will not be kind, because books are not kind. They are only kept."

The clock went on. The hand jumped. It jumped again.

I went out with my hand on Edward's arm, and the door shut behind us, and I did not say anything until we were at the gate.

***

"Well," Edward said.

"Well."

We walked down the hill toward the inn. The street was loud. The town was at its dinner and the street smelled of cabbage and hot iron, and a boy went past with a can and looked at us the way boys look at people coming out of a house like that.

"He did not refuse you," Edward said.

"He gave me a list."

"He gave you the law, which is a list with the prices on it." He shifted the case under his arm. "I will tell you what I think, and you will not like it either. The certificate is all of it. If the surgeon signs, the deposition can be taken, and if the deposition is taken, Hesketh has a paper in the record at Michaelmas and he has to answer it. If the surgeon does not sign, we have what we came with, which is a receipt, an order, a suspension, and a woman's memory."

"And the book."

"And the book," he said. "That is the price, and it is not a price the law charges. It is a price the record charges, and the record does not care whether anybody pays it."

We came to the inn yard. The boy who held the horses was asleep against the wall with his mouth open, and Edward woke him with a word and gave him a penny, and stood with his hand on the chaise door and did not get in.

"You are going back to the toll house," he said.

"In the morning."

"You will not be back before dark."

"No."

"Then write it out tonight," he said. "Not the law. What it would say. She will not hear a list, and she will read a paper." He opened the door. "And tell her the last thing he said. Not the first ones. The last one."

"Why the last one?"

"Because the first ones are the law and she has been living inside the law for forty years and will hear them the way she hears a rate," Edward said. "The last one is about her, and she has not been told a thing about herself in forty years, and she will hear it."

---

## Eighteen · She Will Not Sign

It rained in the night and the ford was up, and I went over the bridge with my skirt in one hand and my boots in the other, and the rain came off the parapet and got me anyway.

The bar was down and the boy was at it, wedging a hurdle against the chain to hold it against the wind.

"Bad," he said.

"Very bad."

***

She had the door open before I knocked. The fire was up and the kettle was on the hob. The room was warm.

"You are wet through."

"I am wet through."

"Take that coat off and put it on the chair and not near the fire, because wet wool near a fire is a smell I can do without." She was at the shelf with her back to me. "There is a towel behind the door."

I dried my hair and my hands, and she put a cup in front of me.

"You have seen the magistrate."

"I have seen him."

"And he has refused you."

"He has not refused me." I put both hands round the cup. "He has given me a list."

"Then read me the list."

***

I read it the way I would read a schedule, and she took it the way she took a rate.

"The surgeon must certify that you cannot be moved without danger. It must come from a surgeon who has seen you, and he must sign his name to the fact."

"Which surgeon?"

"Mr. Ellwood says there is only one at Barrowfield."

"He is my cousin. He was a boy when I was a woman grown, and he has been in this house once in forty years and stood in that corner with his hat in his hands." She turned her cup a quarter of a turn. "Go on."

"With the certificate, a justice may come to the house and take your statement on oath, in your own words, and write it down, and read it back, and you sign it or you make your mark."

"And then?"

"It goes to the sessions at Michaelmas and it is entered."

"Entered where?"

"In the records of the sessions at Ely."

"Kept how?"

"Bound with the rest."

"And read by whom?"

I did not answer at once, and she waited, and the rain went on.

"By any man who asks for it," I said.

She set her cup down in the middle of its ring and did not take her hand away from it.

"Any man."

"Any man who asks. There is no part of it that is private and no page that can be taken out afterward. Mr. Ellwood said it twice, and the second time so that I would carry it home."

"That was good of him," Esther said. "Most men would have let you find it out afterward."

***

I took the draft out of the case and put it on the table. It was in my own hand, and the ink was the ink I had used at the inn before it was light.

"I have written out what it would say. Not to be signed. To be read. So that you can see all of what he would take down, and not the part of it I choose to tell you."

She read it and left her hands where they were.

"You have written it."

"From the papers and from what you have told me."

She put on her spectacles, which she did not wear for me, and read the draft the way she read everything, from the top. She read it twice.

Then she put it down and took the spectacles off and held them in her hand.

"Where is my father in this?"

"On the second page. He is named as the clerk of the parish, because the endorsement is in the office's name and the office was his. A statement that left him out would be pulled apart in the first minute."

"He is named."

"He is named."

She turned the draft round with one finger and did not look at it again.

"Take it away."

"Esther."

"Take it away, and put it in your case, and do not take it out again in this house."

The room was quiet.

I did not take it away. I sat with my hands on the table. The clock went on.

"Tell me why," I said.

"You know why."

"I know what you will say. I want to hear you say it, because I have come a long way and I am not going to take a refusal off a table without the reason under it."

She put the spectacles down.

"Because it is a book," she said.

"It is a paper. It goes into a book afterward, and the book is at Ely, and nobody in this parish will ever open it."

"You have just told me that any man who asks may have it," Esther said. "You told me that as a kindness, so that I would know what I was buying. Do not now tell me that nobody will read it. That is the second price and you cannot take it off because I have said no to the first."

"The parish will not read it. Hesketh will read it, and the bench will read it, and that is what we want."

"You want it read by men who are deciding a question about money," she said. "I have spent forty years keeping my business out of the hands of men who are deciding a question about money, and I have done it by not going out of that door and not answering that window and not signing anything that was put in front of me. You are asking me to hand it to them in a fair copy."

"It is the only thing that will stand at Michaelmas."

"It is the only thing you have thought of."

"I have been at this for a year. I have come a hundred and eleven miles twice, and I am standing in a wet coat in your kitchen, and you tell me that the one lawful thing I can do for you is the one thing you will not do."

"The one lawful thing," she said. "Yes."

"What is unlawful about it? It is a statement. It is sworn. It is true."

"There is nothing in it that is not true," Esther said, "and it will still put my father's name into a book as a man who did a thing a clerk may not do."

***

She went to the desk and stood in front of it with her hands at her sides and did not open the lid.

"He was the clerk of this parish for the whole of his life. He kept the chest and the books and the key, and he was never once out of order in anything he did, and when he could not sit up he had me sit at that desk and write for him, and he listened to every line from that bed."

"I know."

"You do not know. You have read the books. The books are what he did. The books are not what it cost him." She put her hand flat on the lid. "In the March of ninety-three the overseer came with an order that had one justice's name at the foot of it and a blank where the other should be, because the second justice did not come to the petty sessions. It was good enough to move a woman out of the parish by Thursday, and not good enough to stand if anybody looked at it."

"And you wrote the note."

"In the office's hand, which was my father's hand, because I had learned to write by copying it and by that year could not tell the two apart. I wrote, for the clerk of the peace, at the foot of it, and put the office's name under it, in the afternoon, with the light going and my father asleep in the corner and the overseer waiting at the door with his hat in his hand."

"Why?"

"Because an order with a note of transmission on it does not get looked at. It gets filed. And a thing that is filed is not argued about until somebody has a reason, and by the time somebody had a reason the woman would have been through the winter."

She turned round.

"That is the unlawful thing. That is the only one. I have kept a gate and written a book and never taken a penny that was not mine, and I put the office's name to a note the office had no right to put, so that a woman's name would not go into a book as a person to be moved out of this parish. And now you bring me a fair copy that says, in my own words, on oath, that the clerk of Tindle wrote that note. You have written it the way a clerk writes, with the dates and the office and the hand, and if I sign it my father goes into the record as a man who did an unlawful thing in his own name, and he is not here to say that he did not do it, and I did."

"He would not deny it."

"He would not deny it," she said. "He would take it. That is what he was. And I am not going to give it to him."

***

The pen was on the table where I had put it down, and I picked it up and held it out to her.

"Then sign it and put his name in the margin. Say it was you. Say the hand was yours and the office knew nothing. Let the record have the truth and have it whole."

She looked at the pen.

"And what does the record do with that?" she said. "A girl of nineteen writing for the clerk of the peace in his own office. The record has no place for a girl of nineteen. It has a place for a clerk, and the clerk was my father, and the parish will read it and say, that is how the Callards did their business, and the daughter confessed it and the father was not there to answer."

She took the pen out of my hand and looked at it and put it down.

"Put it away," she said.

I picked it up. The pen was dry. The nib was furred with old ink, and the horn handle was worn into the shape of a thumb that was not mine.

The room went out.

There was a candle and a corner of a table and a paper with a seal on it, and a hand coming in from the right with the pen in it, and the hand stopping above the paper, and staying, and the candlelight on the back of the hand and on the hairs of the wrist, and behind it, in the corner where the bed was, a man breathing slow and wet and not waking, and then the hand came down and wrote, and went on writing, and did not stop again.

Then the room was a room. I did not move. The rain was at the window and the fire was at my back and the pen was in my hand and it was warm.

"You have read it," Esther said.

"I have read it."

"Then you know what it was."

"I know what it looked like."

"You know what it cost," she said, "and you have come here with a fair copy of it in your case and asked me to sign my name under it, and you did not do it out of cruelty, and that is the worst of it, because a cruel woman could be refused and sent home."

***

I sat down. My hands were cold.

"Then what am I to do? You have told me what you will not do. Tell me what there is to do."

"I do not know."

"You have been in this house for forty years and every year of it has been a decision about that eight pounds. Then you know how it is held."

"I have learned how this parish is held. It is held by six people at their doors, and one of them is dead, and I have not been out of this house to ask another, and I am not going to be able to."

"Then it is lost."

"It is not lost until Michaelmas."

"It is lost at Michaelmas and you know it. Hesketh will take the eight pounds out of the book and the overseers will not enter it, and she will be a woman in a cottage with no rate and no order and no name." I put my hand flat on the table. "That is what a custom is worth when a man with a schedule comes. It cannot be sued on and it cannot be sworn to and it cannot be entered, and the only thing here that can be entered is a statement, and you will not make one."

"No."

"Then you are choosing his name over her life."

The rain went on. She did not get up and she did not turn away.

"That is a good sentence," she said. "It is the best one you have used in this house, and I will tell you what is wrong with it. I am not choosing his name over her life. I am refusing to spend a dead man's name on a paper that I do not believe will save her."

"You do not believe it."

"I believe it will be read. I do not believe it will be enough." She put her hands together. "Hesketh will say the order is good on its face, and has stood for forty years, and has been acted on by the parish and by the bench, and that a statement from an old woman in a toll house will not overturn it. He will say that, and he will be right, and the paper will be in the book for nothing, and my father's name will be in it for ever."

"You do not know that."

"No," she said. "Neither do you. That is the difference between us. You are certain and I am not, and I am the one who would have to put her hand to it."

The fire was low.

"Mr. Ellwood said something else."

"Say it."

"He said a deposition is not a letter. A letter is read by the person it is addressed to. A book is read by whoever asks for it, in another age, by a man who never met her."

"And?"

"And I said that was the price and we would pay it."

"You said that."

"I said it in his room and I believed it." I looked at the pen in my hand. "I have been paying prices all my life out of other people's pockets and calling it arithmetic."

"That is the truest thing you have said today," Esther said, "and it is not enough to make me sign."

"Now I will ask you something," she said, "and you will not like it, and you may refuse it."

"Ask."

"What do you want?"

I sat with my hands on the table.

"That is not the question before us."

"It is the only question that has been before us since you came over that ford with your boots in your hand." She put her hand flat on the table. "You have a paper in your pocket with a space after the word is. You have carried it a year and you have not filled it. You have come a hundred and eleven miles to ask an old woman what she wants, and you have your answer, and you do not know what to do with it because it cannot be entered." She put her hand on the back of her chair. "I have filled mine. Mine is on that wall with a nail through it, and it has been there forty years, and it is the only thing in this house I have ever been able to get anybody to read."

I did not answer her.

"I thought not," she said.

"That is not fair."

"No," she said. "It is accurate."

***

I put the draft back in the case and put the case under my arm and put my wet coat on, and it was cold and it smelled of the rain, and she stood by the table and did not help.

At the door I turned round with my hand on the latch.

"Esther. If I find another way. If there is a way that does not put a paper in front of you."

"There is not."

"There might be."

"There might be," she said, "and you will look for it, because that is what you are. And if it is a paper I will say no."

"Then what am I to bring you?"

She came to the door and stood in front of me, small and straight, with her hands at her sides, and the rain was coming off the eaves behind me.

"Nothing," she said. "Come yourself. That is all I have ever asked anybody, and it took me forty years to learn how to ask it, and you are the first person I have asked who has a pen in her pocket."

I put my hand on the latch.

"Esther."

"If you want to save me," she said, "do not use the pen."

---

## Nineteen · The Night

The chaise was bespoken for the morning and my box was strapped, and I walked down to the toll house in the afternoon to say that I was going home.

The day had gone warm and still. The may was nearly over in the hedge, the dust lay in the road where the carts had been, and I came over the bridge because the ford is a young woman's road, as she had told me, and I had stopped being one somewhere in the past year without marking the day.

The boy lifted the bar before I asked. He had begun to do that in the last week, and it was a courtesy, and it was also a way of not having to say anything.

"She is up," he said. "She has not had anything since the morning."

The door stood open. The room behind it was warm, and the kettle was on the hob, and the fire was built small and close, and the table had the pot on it and the cosy beside it and a cup turned ready.

"You are going home," she said.

"I am going home in the morning."

"Then you will take a cup before you go, because the woman at that inn puts the leaves in the pot and then forgets what she has done with it." She had the pot in her right hand already. "Sit down. You are standing in the door like a man selling something."

I sat down.

She tipped the pot, and the tea came out over the cup and over the saucer and over the cloth in a flat brown line, and it went on coming.

"Esther."

The pot stayed where it was.

"Esther. The pot."

She looked down at her hand the way a person looks at a door that has been locked from the other side. Her fingers were still round the handle. The tea went over the edge of the table and came down to the flagstones in a thin line that broke into drops, and the drops were the only sound in the room.

I took the pot out of her hand the way a person takes a thing out of a child's hand. I did not pull. I set it on the hearth and took her by the elbow.

Her mouth moved. What came out of it had no letters in it. It was a sound with a shape, and the shape went up at the end, and then it stopped, and she tried again and the second one was worse than the first.

"Sit down. Sit down in the chair."

She sat down. She did it herself, with her left hand flat on the table, and she sat the way a person sits when sitting is a decision, and her right hand lay in her lap with the fingers opening and shutting, and she did not look at it again.

Her face had gone slack. There is no way to say that which is not worse than the thing itself.

I went down on my knees. The flags were wet.

"Can you say my name?"

Nothing.

"Can you say anything at all?"

Her left hand came up off the table and went down again.

"Then we are going to the bed, and you are going to help me, because I cannot carry you and I am not going to drag you across your own floor."

She got her left arm up over my shoulder before I had finished the sentence. That was the last thing she did of her own accord for the rest of her life except one, and I did not know it at the time.

She was lighter than I expected and heavier than I could manage. We went along the table with her hand taking the edge of it, and the curtain was in the way and I pushed it aside with my shoulder, and the smell of her at that distance was coal and soap and bread.

I sat her on the edge of the bed and took her boots off and put them side by side under it with the toes out. I did it without deciding. It is what a person does who expects the boots to be wanted in the morning.

Then the bolster behind her shoulder so that she was not flat, and the sheet up to her waist, and her hands on top of it.

Her left hand took hold of my wrist. It was not a grip. It was the weight of a hand laid down. Then it let go.

I went to the door and called him, and he came up the cinders at a run, which he had never done.

"The surgeon. At Barrowfield. Do you know the house?"

"The one with the green door."

"Go. Say a woman has had a stroke and cannot speak. Say I am with her and I am not leaving. If he is not at home, find where he has gone and stand in front of him until he comes."

"Is she dying?"

"Go."

***

Then the afternoon went.

I wiped the tea off the table and the cloth and the flags, and wrung the cloth out at the door and hung it on the nail, and stood in the middle of the room. My hands were empty.

The cat came out from behind the curtain. It got up on the bed and sat by her hip and did not lie down.

The clock stood by the window with its face repainted and its numerals too large, and the striking weight hung on its chain near the floor. She wound it every week. It was near the floor and she had not wound it, and I thought that she had been busy, and I did not touch it, because it was not mine to touch.

That was the first thing, and I did not know it was a thing.

The light came round the room and got onto the toll board and stood on the white letters and went off them. Then there were wheels.

The boy had the door, and he came in without knocking. He stood in the doorway and looked at the room before he looked at me, which is what a man does who has been in a great many rooms on the same errand.

"Mrs. Rooke. You are the lady who wanted a certificate."

"I am the lady who sent for you."

"Those are not always the same errand." He put the bag on the table and the hat beside it. "Where is she?"

He did not hurry. He did not dawdle. He knelt by the bed, which cost him something in the knees, and asked me for the candle and I held it.

"Mrs. Callard. Squeeze my hand."

Nothing.

"The other one."

The left hand closed on his fingers, and it closed hard, and he looked at it for a while.

"Look at me." She looked at him. "Look at Mrs. Rooke." She looked at me, and her eyes came round and stayed, and that was the thing I held on to for the rest of the night.

He rolled her eyelid up with his thumb and looked, and let it down, and did the other side the same way. Then he stood up and wiped his hands on his own handkerchief, and jerked his head at the room, and we went and stood by the table with the curtain drawn back a little.

"It is the brain," he said.

"Which part of it?"

"Nobody can tell you which part of it. There is a vessel in it that has given way, or a vessel that has closed, and the difference is a difference for the anatomists, and she is not a subject and I am not one. I do not know, and no man in this county knows, whatever he may tell you at the door."

"Will she speak?"

"I have seen them speak again, and I have seen them not." He turned his hat round on the table. "It is early. Ask me on Thursday."

"Will she know me?"

"She knows you now. Her eyes are following you and the hand is good on the left side, and that is more than I hoped for when the boy came to my door." He put the hat down. "Keep the room warm and the air off her. Wet her lips, and not with water poured into her mouth, because it will go the wrong way and there is no sense in drowning a woman in her own bed. Turn her before it is light. If she lies on her back the whole night through, the lung will fill, and that is a way of dying I would not leave a dog to."

"Will you bleed her?"

"No. I have seen it done and I have not seen it do good."

He put his coat on. At the door he stopped with his hand on the latch and his hat not yet on his head.

"You will want a certificate for the bench."

"She would not have signed the statement."

"That is not what I asked."

"No."

He waited, and the waiting went on long enough that the cat got down off the bed and crossed the floor between us.

"I would have signed it," he said. "I would have signed it in the March and I would have signed it this morning, and it would have been true, and I would have thought no better of myself for it. She is my cousin and I have been in this house once in forty years and I stood in that corner with my hat in my hands."

"I do not think anything of it."

"You will." He put his hat on. "If she tries to speak and cannot, do not tell her it will come back. Tell her that you have understood her. A woman who cannot make herself understood will use up what she has left trying."

Then the door was shut and the light was going. I put the bolt across. She asked it of every caller, and I did it for her.

***

The candle was on the chair beside the bed, and I trimmed the wick with my fingers and the black came off on my thumb, and the flame stood up and sank and stood up again.

The horn cup was in the box on the shelf under the window, behind the tin, and the lid fought me and I eased it with the flat of my thumb. The rim was worn thin as a coin on one side and mended with silver wire, and it was the only cup in the house that would go to a mouth that could not sit up. I put water in it and held it to her lips and tipped it a little, and she took what she took, and it ran at the side of her mouth and I caught it with the cloth.

Her hand lay on the sheet. The palm was up.

I put my hand over it. It was warm and dry and the nails were clean and the knuckles were swollen the way they had been swollen since before I knew her, and the fingers were still exact.

I could have had all of it.

That is the thing I have to write down plainly, because there is no way to make it smaller. I had only to hold that hand and let the room go, and I would have known whether she was afraid, and whether there was anybody living she wanted sent for, and what her father said to her when he woke and found the note written, and whether she had ever in forty years been sorry. It would have come in the time it takes to draw a breath, and she could not have stopped me, and she would never have known.

I sat there with my hand over hers. I did not do it.

Not because it is forbidden. I have done forbidden things and slept. But a reading is a statement taken without a signature. That was her argument, and she had taught it to me across that table, and I had brought her a fair copy and asked her to put her name under it, and she had said no, with her hand flat on the wood.

She could not say no now. That was the difference, and it was the only difference, and it was the one that mattered.

So I let go of her hand and I asked her with my voice, one question at a time, and waited for the blink.

"Esther. Blink, and I will take it for yes. Do not blink, and I will take it for no. If I have it wrong, blink at the next one and I will know that I had it wrong."

She blinked.

"Do you want the clergyman sent for?"

Nothing. Her eyes stayed on me. They did not close.

"Do you want the clergyman at Tindle sent for tonight?"

Nothing. I waited long enough for the clock to be the loudest thing in the room.

"Do you want me to leave it alone?"

She blinked.

"Then it is left alone." I put my hand on the sheet beside hers and left it there. "Is the candle in your eyes?"

She blinked.

I moved it to the other side of the chair.

"Does it hurt?"

Nothing. I waited. A piece of coal went down in the grate, and I asked her again in different words, because the first asking of a thing is not always the asking that gets through.

"Does it hurt in your head?"

Nothing.

"In your arm?"

Nothing.

"In your side, or your chest?"

Nothing.

I sat back. I had known her a short while, and I knew what her face did when she was deciding not to answer a question, and it was not doing that. It was doing nothing at all, which is a different thing, and it was the answer.

"Is the candle still in your eyes?"

Nothing.

That was the one that told me the count was good. I had moved the candle and she had not blinked, and she was not blinking at everything to be rid of me.

"Then you are not in pain," I said. "That is a great deal, and I am going to hold on to it, and so are you."

The clock struck somewhere in the dark and I did not count the strokes.

"Are you afraid?"

Nothing.

"Are you afraid of being left?"

She blinked.

"Then I will not leave you." I said it the way a person says a thing they have decided and do not intend to argue about. "The chaise can go back to the inn and the inn can keep it. I am going to sit here, and in the morning there will be a man here who will tell us both that he does not know, and we will get through that as well."

Her eyes stayed on me. The left hand came up an inch off the sheet and went down.

"Was that a yes?"

She blinked.

"Then we are agreed, and I will not ask you anything else tonight, because you have answered enough for a woman who cannot speak, and I am not going to sit here and go through you like a rate."

There was a question she had asked me in that room, and I had told her that it was not the question before us. It does not go into a blink. I did not put it to her. I sat on the floor with my back against the wall and her hand in mine, and the question sat in the room with us and was not said.

The night went by in breaths.

Her breath went in and stayed too long and came out, and then there was a pause, and the pause was longer than the breath, and then the next one came. I got so that I knew the length of the pause before it happened, and when it went past the length I put my hand on her chest and waited, and it came, and I took my hand away, and after a while I put it back.

The fire went out. I do not know the hour. The window went from black to grey, and somewhere behind the house a hen began, and then the rest of them.

Her hand moved in mine.

It was her forefinger. It went from the heel of my palm to the root of my finger, slow, and pressed at the end of it, and stopped.

I did not ask her. I have had a long time to decide and I have not decided, and I am not going to put a meaning on it now for the comfort of having one. It was not a word. It was made on purpose, by a woman who had made marks on paper for forty years and had never once made one that was read by somebody who did not want something from her.

"I am here," I said.

The light came up the field and into the room and lay along the bed. The clock did not strike. It had run down in the night with the weight near the floor, and I sat on the boards with my back against the wall and her hand in mine, and the room was warm and the fire was dead and the cat had gone.

---

## Twenty · The Burial

She died in the morning with the light on the bed.

I was sitting on the boards with her hand in mine, and the hand was warm, and then it was not warm. That was the difference. There was no sound to it and no tightening. The breath went in and stayed.

I sat with her until the room was light. Then I put my thumb on her eyelid and it went down and stayed down, and I did the other one the same way, and I said her name. Then I got up. There was a day to get through and it had begun without me.

The boy was at the bar with the chain in his hand and his mouth open.

"Your mother," I said. "Go to the kiln and tell your mother to come, and tell her what has happened, and then go to the clergyman and tell him the same. Go now."

He went without a word and without his coat. I stood in the doorway until he was out of sight. Then I went back in and shut the door on the room and stood with my back against it.

I did not know what to do with my hands. That was the trouble. In a house where somebody is dying there is always water to be boiled and a cloth to be found, and then it is over, and the house is full of nothing to do.

***

She came within the hour with a bag and a clean sheet folded over her arm, and she was a broad woman with her sleeves rolled and her forearms black to the wrist with the clay, and she stopped in the door and looked at the bed and then at me.

"You are the lady from the bench."

"I am the lady who was here."

"Was she your kin?"

"No."

"Then sit down on that chair and let me do this, and if you want to be useful you will hold the basin and not look away, because she would know." She put the bag on the table. "What was her name?"

"Callard."

"With a C?"

"With a C."

She said it over to herself, the way a person says a word they are going to have to write, and then she took her apron off and rolled her sleeves again and went to the bed.

We washed her. The water in the basin went grey and then it went the colour of weak tea, and I emptied it at the door and brought more from the kettle, and the woman's hands were quick and unceremonious and exact, and she talked while she did it, not to me, in a low run of talk about the weather and the price of soap and a sister at Nethercote.

"Turn her," she said. "Not that way. Toward me."

Then the clean sheet under her, and the jaw bound with a strip of linen tied at the crown of her head, and the hands crossed on the sheet. The woman stood back.

She took the coins out of her pocket.

"No," I said.

"It is the custom."

"I know it is the custom."

"The parish pays for it."

"No."

She looked at me for a moment with the coins in her palm, and then she put them back in her pocket and shut her eyes with her thumb and her finger, one side and then the other, and stood back again.

The room was quiet. The fire was out. The clock had stopped, and the table still had the cup on it with the chip in the rim, and the pot, and the cosy. Nobody had cleared it. I cleared it.

"That is as good," she said. "It will not stay as long, that is all." She wiped her hands on the towel. "You may call me the woman at the kiln. That is what she called me, and I have got used to it, and my name is on no paper in this town that I have not signed myself."

***

The overseer came before dinner, with the book under his arm and his hat already off at the gate.

He was a thin man. He was not unkind. He did the thing he had come to do in the order it is done in, which is the mercy of a man with a form.

"Mrs. Rooke. You have had the trouble of this."

"I have."

"Was she of the church?"

"She kept the gate."

"That is not an answer."

"She was baptised in this parish. Her father was the clerk of it for the whole of his life, and her name is in the register at Tindle, in his hand."

"Then she is ours," he said, and it was the truest thing anybody said that day, and he did not know it.

He opened the book. The pages were ruled in columns, with the headings written across the top in a fair hand, and he inked his pen and wiped it.

"Her name."

"Esther Callard."

He wrote it, and spelled it right without asking me, because it was in the rate-book already and had been for forty years.

"Her abode."

"The toll house, at the bridge."

"Her years."

"I do not know them."

He looked up.

"You do not know them."

"I do not."

"It is in the book," he said, and turned back through the register to the baptisms, and ran his finger down a page and stopped at an entry in a hand I knew. "Here she is. Her father wrote her in himself."

He read the year off the top of it, and then he turned forward to the head of the book where the year was written, and did the subtraction with the nib of his pen moving on the paper, and wrote the answer in the column for her years. He did not look up.

He did it in less time than it takes to read out a rate.

He filled the day of the burial. The last column was for the clergyman, and that was the last of her.

"She kept out of books for forty years," I said.

He looked at me over the book.

"I do not know what that means," he said, "and I am not certain that it is my business. She is entered, and she is entered properly, and there is nothing in the column that a man could take exception to." He blotted the page. "That is what a register is for, Mrs. Rooke. It is not a monument. It is a list of what the parish has done, and the parish has done this, and now it is done."

He shut the book. He put his hat on and took it off again.

"The toll book and the keys belong to the trust. The trust's man will come for them, and for the box."

"On Saturday."

"On Saturday." He turned at the door. "Her effects."

"I will see to her effects."

He wrote that down as well, in a book of his own that he took out of his coat, and then he went out to the road and stood with his hat in his hand for a moment before he put it on.

***

In the afternoon I went up to the churchyard.

He was in the ground to his knees by then, and the earth came up on the spade and went over his shoulder onto the heap beside him, and the heap was the shape of the grave turned inside out. There was a rope laid along the edge with a stick driven at each end of it, and the rope held the line of the plot while he dug, and he worked inside it and never put a boot on it.

The clay was wet lower down and came off the blade in a slab with a suck to it, and he knocked the spade against the side of the grave to clear it, and the sound of it went out over the wall and up the road.

"Are you the one that was with her," he said, and did not stop.

"I was."

"Then give me the name and I will have it right on the board."

I gave it to him. He had a piece of paper folded small in his hand and a pencil behind his ear, and he wrote on the paper and held it up against the sky to read his own writing.

"That is the board," he said. "It stands at the head until the stone comes. It is a board and a pencil, and it is what the parish has."

He went on digging.

"I have cut a good many of these," he said, "and I do not know her face. I have been at this ground a long while and I could not pick her out of a row of women at the bridge."

"She kept the gate. She sat at the window of the toll house and took the money through it, and the bar was down across the road in front of her."

He stopped with the spade standing in the earth.

"Then I have paid her at the bar," he said, "and I never knew it."

He went on. When the grave was deep enough he came out of it by the step he had cut and by the rope, and he went to the porch and took a book off the sill, a small one with a pencil pushed through the cover, ruled by himself with a ruler and a pen, in columns. There was a stone bottle of ink on the sill with a rag in the neck.

He set the book on the flat stone by the door and took the rag out and laid the pen across the mouth of the bottle and wrote.

He asked me the day and I told him, and the day went into its column. He asked me where she had lived and I said the toll house at the bridge, and that went into a column too, though the book had none for it and he put it at the end of the line. Then the name, in the spelling I had given him, and then the row, and then the plot against the rope. When he had finished he went back along the line with the nib to see that he had missed nothing, and there was not one of his columns that he had left short.

He wrote her down and the columns took her the way they had taken everybody else, and there was nothing in his hand that hesitated.

He blew on the page and shut the book.

"It has to go down," he said. "A grave with nothing written against it is one nobody can find again. Then somebody digs it."

"That is a good reason."

"It is the only one I have got," he said, and put the book back on the sill and the rag in the neck of the bottle, and went to the heap with the spade.

***

They buried her on the Thursday.

The coffin was deal, painted black, with a plate on it. Her name on the plate was spelled right, because I had written it out for the man on a piece of paper and made him read it back to me.

They put it on a cart. The church is up the road, and a cart is what the parish has.

The bar was down across the road, and the boy was at it.

He lifted it early. He stood at the side of the road with his hat off, and he stayed there while the cart went through, and there was no coin, and there was nobody in the window to take one.

"She never took from the dead," he said.

"No."

"She told me that when I was small. She said, a cart with a coffin on it is a cart, and the trust has never made me take from the dead, and if it tells me to I will put the money in the box myself and write it in the book as a gift." He put his hat on. "I have thought about that a good deal."

Along the road there were people at their doors.

They stood apart from one another, each at his own gate or his own step, and every one of them had his hat off, and not one of them came down to the road. There was a smith with his apron still on and a shop woman with a cloth in her hand and a man at a long field gate with a dog at his heel, and I did not know any of their names, and they did not speak, and the cart went by them at a walking pace and they stood until it had gone.

The woman at the end of the row was not at her door. She had gone up that road herself in the month before, under a cloth, and the bar had been up for her too.

Edward came up from the inn in the morning with his hat under his arm, and he stood at the wall at the back and did not come forward until it was over.

The clergyman met us at the gate of the churchyard.

He was a young man. He had his book open already, with his thumb in it, and he asked me before the service whether she had been a communicant.

"I do not know."

He looked at me.

"You were with her at the end."

"I was with her at the end. That is not the same as knowing whether she took the bread."

"No," he said. "It is not." And he wrote something in the margin of his book with a pencil, and then he read the office.

He read it well. He read it as a thing that is true whether or not anybody in the ground was listening, and he called her our sister, and he said the part about the resurrection and the life, and the earth went in and made a sound on the lid that I have not been able to forget.

The sexton filled the grave with the flat of the spade and patted it down and stood back. The clay was heavy. It did not want to go back in. The clergyman shut his book, and it was over in less time than the vestry takes to decide nothing.

I stood at the grave when they had all gone.

Edward was at the gate with the chaise.

"You have not eaten," he said.

"I have not."

"Nor slept."

"I slept in the chair."

He looked up the road. "The trust's man will be at the house on Saturday. I will be there at the same hour."

"You do not have to be."

"I know I do not have to be," he said, and did not move from the gate.

"There is no stone," I said.

"Not yet."

"I am going to see about it now."

"Have you thought what to put on it?"

"No."

He waited, and then he did the thing he was good at, which was to say nothing, and to stay.

The grave had no stone on it yet. There was a mound of clay with the spade marks in it and a board at the head with a paper on it, and the paper had her name on it in pencil and would not last the winter.

***

The mason had his yard behind the town, a shed with a bench and a rack of chisels and half-finished stones leaning against the wall, and the dust of the cutting on everything. He was a small man, with white dust in the seams of his hands.

He put a paper on the bench and took up a piece of chalk.

"What was she?"

"She kept the toll gate at the bridge."

"I have been through that gate," he said. "She charged me for a load of stone and told me the rate had gone up, and it had not."

"And?"

"And I paid it," he said, "and I would pay it again."

"What do you want on it?"

"Her name."

He wrote the name.

"And?"

"The years."

He wrote the years. Then he held the chalk and waited.

"There is room for a line," he said.

"I know there is room for a line."

"Most people have a line."

"I have not got one."

He waited. The chalk went round in his fingers.

"It is a cold stone without a line," he said. "I am not telling you your business, but I have cut a good many of these, and a stone with a name and the years on it and nothing else looks like a stone that somebody was in a hurry about."

"It was not hurry."

"Then what was it?"

I stood in the dust with my hands at my sides.

"I do not know what she wanted on it," I said. "I have been asking people what they wanted for a year, and I asked her, and she gave me an answer, and it was not an answer that goes on a stone. And I am not going to put a line on her that she would have read and said, that is not mine."

The mason looked at the paper.

"Then it is the name and the years," he said.

"Then it is the name and the years."

He wrote it down. He named a price, and I paid him, and he asked whether I wanted it up before the winter, and I said I did.

"You will think of a line after," he said. "They all do."

"Then I will come back and you will cut it."

"They do not come back," he said, and went to the rack for a chisel, and I went out into the road.

I did not come back.

***

The trust's man came on the Saturday and took the toll book and the cash box and the keys and made a list of the furniture on a loose sheet of paper, and he was civil and he was quick, and he did not look at the bed. The house was cold.

I took the clock down myself and set it on the table, and it did not go, and I did not wind it. It went home with me, wrapped in the curtain, and it has not gone since.

The cat was in the chair and would not be moved. I put it in a basket with the lid open and gave it to the boy to carry to his mother at the kiln, and it went without a sound. It did not look back.

There were papers on the wall, gone the colour of weak tea, and the nails had rusted and left a stain down the plaster, and I got the first nail out with the back of the hammer and the plaster came away with it in a little cone. I laid them flat between the leaves of a book and put the book in the case. The receipt was on the top, with the stamp at the corner that had split the paper.

Then I took the bed apart.

The curtain came down off its rod. The tick came off the boards, and the boards came up, and the dust under them had not been moved in a long time, and the box was at the head end, where a hand could reach down in the dark without getting out of the bed.

It was wood, and the lid slid in a groove, and there was a band of iron round it that had been put on by somebody who expected it to outlast the box.

I put my hand on the lid. I did not open it.

It was still hers. It had not been entered anywhere, and I carried it out to the chaise with the sheet over it so that the trust's man would not see it, and I put it under the seat, and I got in after it and put my hand on it, and the chaise went up the road and over the bridge and away.

The gate stood open behind us. The bar was up. There was nobody at the window.

---

## Twenty-One · The Box

We came home over the long road and stopped at an inn and went on in the morning, and the box rode under the seat with my foot against it the whole way.

The house smelled of nothing, which is what a house smells of when nobody has been in it for a while.

Edward carried the box in and set it on the kitchen table and did not ask about it, and then he went out to see about the horse, and I stood in the kitchen with my coat still on and my hands at my sides.

The drawer stuck, as it always did in wet weather. I opened it with the flat of my hand and looked at the tin box and the paper under it, and shut it again.

I did not open the wooden box that night. I have opened a good many things in my life that I had no business opening, and the one thing that was mine to open I left on the table between the salt and the bread.

It was the evening of the next day when Edward said it, with his back to me at the dresser, speaking to the plates.

"You have not opened it."

"No."

"It has been on that table since we came in."

"It has."

He put the plates down and turned round.

"A box is not a letter," he said. "It does not improve by being left."

"I know what a box is."

"Then open it, and I will sit here. If it is money you will not have to tell me, and if it is not money you will."

I pulled the box to me and he sat down at the table and put his hands on his knees, the way he sat when he had decided to be of no use to anybody and to stay until he was.

The lid slid in its groove. I had thought it would fight me, because everything in that house had fought me, and it came open with one push of my thumb.

There was flannel on the top, a square of it cut to the size of the box, and I sat with it in my hands for a moment and then put it aside.

Under it there was a ring, a book, and a paper folded small.

The ring lay on the folded paper. It was brass and thin and there was no stone in it, and the inside was worn bright in a band where it had turned against a finger for a long time, and it was so light that it seemed to weigh nothing at all. It went on my little finger and turned easily, and I took it off.

"A guard ring," I said.

Edward put out his hand and I gave it to him and he turned it over.

"It is brass."

"It is brass. Women who serve in shops wear them. A woman behind a counter with a ring on her hand is a married woman, and a married woman is not asked the question that a girl is asked." I put my hands together on the table. "She wore it at the gate for forty years. It answered for her. It answered before anybody had to ask, and it answered without a word being written down."

He put it on the cloth.

"That is a paper you can wear," he said.

"That is a paper you can wear."

He looked at it a while longer and pushed it back across the table to me with one finger, the way he pushed a document he had finished with.

The paper had been under the ring. It was folded small and it was thin, and I knew what it was before I had it open, because I had spent a year with the other one in my case. It was the receipt. It was a copy, in the office hand, made on thin paper with a hard pen, and the stamp at the corner had been pressed hard enough to split the paper, and the split ran the same way as the split on the one that had been nailed to the wall.

The date at the foot of it was the Michaelmas of 1794. There was the sum, and there was the word *paid* under the signature in a different hand, and there was nothing on the copy that was not on the original, down to the shape of the split.

She had copied it before she nailed it up. She had sat at that desk with the original in front of her and made a copy of it, in her own hand, which was her father's hand, and then she had taken a hammer and put the original on the wall at the height of a woman's eyes, and left the copy in the box.

I put it down. Then I sat back.

"Why would she copy it?" Edward said.

"Because the one on the wall was for looking at."

"And the copy?"

"The copy was for keeping," I said. "A paper on a wall belongs to the house. A paper in a box belongs to the person who put it there."

He thought about that, and then he nodded once, which was as much as he ever gave to a thing he agreed with and had not thought of himself.

The book lay under it.

***

It was a copybook. It had a marbled cover and the corners were rubbed soft, and there was a label on the front that had been soaked off and left a pale square with the gum still on it.

I lifted it out. The kitchen went out.

It was the same room and it was not the same light.
