There was a table and a lamp turned down low and a hand on the page, and the hand had a brass ring on the little finger, and the forefinger went down the column and stopped and went on and stopped again, the way a person counts a flock at a gate. Beyond the window the bar was down across the road. Then the hand came to the bottom of the page and turned it and went back to the top and began again.

Then the kitchen was a kitchen and the book was in my hands and it was warm, and Edward was saying my name.

"What did you get?"

"The gate was shut," I said. "She read it at night, after the bar was down."

He waited.

"That is all," I said. "It is not a great deal."

"It is more than nothing."

The flyleaf had the copy lines in it. Somebody had ruled them and somebody had copied them out in a careful hand and gone over them again, and they were the lines that are set for children.

*Industry is the parent of fortune*

*A place for everything and everything in its place*

Under the line about places, in a different ink and a much older hand, somebody had written:

*She was wrong about that one.*

I sat with the book open and laughed, once, and it came out badly, and Edward looked up and said nothing.

I turned the page.

The pages were ruled for a child's hand and the writing on them was not a child's. It was the office hand, small and upright, kept small so that more would go on a line, and it went down the page in a column with a space between the entries. The entries were names.

*Mary Ann Ashby*

*Tace*

*the smith at the end of the lane*

*Thomas Gedge*

*Sarah Collen*

*the ferryman at the hard*

*William Pratt*

*Hannah Pratt*

*the shop at the corner of Cow Lane*

Some of the lines had a small cross beside them and some had a dot and some had nothing at all, and I did not know what either of the marks meant. I thought at first that the crosses were the dead, and then I found a cross beside a woman I knew to be living, and I stopped trying to make the marks say anything.

Some of the entries were names. Some of them were not. Where she had a name she wrote the name. Where she had not got one, she wrote the door.

It was not in the order the doors had been knocked at. Nobody's mother is at the top of a book of forty years unless somebody has put her there. She had put her there.

The paper had gone yellow. The top corner had gone brown, the ink on the first pages had gone the colour of weak tea, and the top right corner of every page had worn round with turning.

I put my finger on the top line and left it there.

"Edward."

"What is it?"

"My mother is in this book."

He came round the table. He stood at my shoulder and read it, and his breath moved the page at the corner, and he did not say anything for long enough that I turned round to look at him.

"Mary Ann Ashby," he said.

"Mary Ann Ashby."

"Your mother brought her a basket every Wednesday for thirty years."

"Yes."

"And her name is at the top of the book."

"Her name is at the top of the book." I took my finger off the paper. "She was a girl when she began going over that ford with the basket. She came to the door and put it on the step and went away, and then one Wednesday she did not go away, because the door was open and the woman inside it asked her a question."

"Which question?"

"The one the book is for."

He stood at my shoulder.

"That is not a list of promises," he said.

"No."

"That is a list of people she asked."

"That is a list of people she asked." I turned the page. "And there is not a word in it about what any of them said."

***

I read it all evening. It took the rest of the evening, and it was forty years of names in a child's copybook, one to a line, with a space between the entries so that nothing would run together.

There were names I knew from the town and names that were strange to me, and there were doors instead of names, and there were pages where the ink changed and the hand got older and the lines got further apart. The smith was there, and the wheelwright, and the farmer at the long field, and the woman at the kiln, and the woman at the end of the row who had gone up the road under a cloth in the month before Esther did. The boy was in it, near the end, on a line of his own that said *the boy at the bar*.

Edward sat with me for the first pages and then he got up and made the tea and put a cup at my elbow and went and sat by the fire with the newspaper and did not read it.

I did not read the book the way a person reads. I read it the way a clerk reads, from the top of each page, looking for what had been left out, and what had been left out was everything except the names. No date, no place, no word about money, no note of what anybody had answered. A column of people and nothing else in the world.

Most of the names in that book are in the churchyard now. I had spoken to some of them in the past year. I had not known, when I spoke to them, that a woman at a toll gate had once stood at their door with the same question in her mouth.

Under the last name there was a line ruled, and it was empty.

Beside it, in the same hand and in ink that was darker than anything else in the book, were words.

*Ask her*

I sat with my finger under the words.

The fire went down. Edward turned a page of the newspaper and turned it back again, which is what he did when he was not reading and wanted to look as though he were.

"Whose name is missing?" he said.

"Nobody's."

"Then why is the line empty?"

"Because she had not got an answer to put on it." I shut the book. "She asked me in her kitchen with her hand flat on the table. She said, what do you want, and I said it was not the question before us, and she said that she thought not, and then she went to the drawer for the pen, and I thought she was going to write something down about the parish."

"And she wrote that."

"She went to the book that night and ruled a line and left it for me. She wrote a note to herself, because she had learned not to trust that there would be another afternoon."

Edward put the paper down.

"It means you."

"It means me."

He did not say anything for a while.

Then he said, "You are not going to fill it in now."

"No."

"Why not?"

"Because I do not know what to put." I put my hand on the cover. "And because a line filled in at a kitchen table in the middle of the night is a line I would have to live with, and I have lived with one of those already."

He got up and put a piece of coal on the fire and stood with his back to it.

"Then what is the book for?" he said. "If it is a list of names, and no answers, and one line that nobody can fill, what is it for?"

I sat with that.

"It is a list of doors," I said. He waited for the rest, and it did not come.

***

Edward went to the fire and stood with his back to it, and I set the things out in a row on the table: the square of flannel, the ring, the receipt, and the copybook laid flat with the cover up.

I took the ring again and rolled it along the board. Brass runs further than it has any business running. It went the length of the table and came down on its side against the salt, and the bright band on the inside of it caught the lamp and let it go again. The dark had gone into the creases where the ring had been cut, so that the band and the rest of the metal were two different colours, and I turned it slowly to find the place where the two met.

The receipt I held up to the lamp. The paper is thin enough that the light comes through it in a patch, and the stamp at the corner had been pressed so hard that it had gone clean through the sheet and left a brown ring on the back of it, and the split ran out of the ring the same way on both sides.

"That is a hard press," Edward said.

"It is a press that was done twice," I said. "Once on the wall and once on this. She leaned on it."

I put it down and took the copybook up by the spine and let the leaves hang.

The block of them was thicker than the cover had promised. Every leaf had been turned at the top corner with a damp thumb, and the damp had cockled them, and the cockling would not go down under my hand. On the first pages the ink has gone into the paper and faded to brown. Lower down it stands up on the surface, black, and it has gone through to the back of the leaf, so that a page held up to the lamp carries her writing twice, once from either side, and the two do not agree.

The hand goes with the ink. At the front the letters are round and tall with air between them, and the crossing of a t is a stroke put down by somebody who was not yet in a hurry. At the back they are small and the loops have gone, and she has been over some of them twice to make them dark, and where she has done that the nib has left a small blot and the blot has dried shiny.

"What are you doing," Edward said.

"Looking at it."

"You have read it."

"Reading is not looking," I said. "I read it for the names. I have not once looked at what the names were put down with."

He came and laid his hand on the cover without lifting it.

"It is a child's book," he said.

"It is a child's book that was ruled for a child's hand and then kept by a woman who had been taught in it, and every page in it is a page she wrote on when she was past being taught." I let the leaves go and laid it down. "She went on writing in the hand her father set her. It got smaller and it never stopped being his."

He took his hand off the cover.

"You have known the hand on the receipt since October," he said.

"I have known it since October."

He went back to the fire, and I sat with the three things in a row on the table and did not put any of them away.

***

I put the ring back in the box, and the receipt under it, and then I went to the drawer.

The drawer still stuck. I opened it the same way and took the paper out from under the tin and unfolded it on the table, and the fold had worn through at the corner in the shape of a thumb, and the line was where it had always been, and so was the space after it.

I did not write anything. I put the paper in the book at the empty line, folded, and shut the book on it, and the line was still empty with my paper lying against it.

"Edward."

He turned round.

"Bespeak the chaise."

"For when?"

"For the morning."

He did not move.

"You have a paper," he said. "You have had a paper all year, and you have been looking for a way to make it stand, and now you have a book with no paper in it at all."

"I have a list of doors."

"That is not a case."

"No. It is better than a case." I put the book in the box and the lid on the box and held it down with the flat of my hand. "A case is a thing a man may answer with a letter. A door is a thing a man has to come to."

He looked at the box.

"There are a great many names in that book," he said.

"Then I will be a long time."

"And Michaelmas?"

"Michaelmas is a day in the church's year. It is not a wall."

He stood with his back to the fire and his hands behind him, and then he went and took his coat off the hook and his hat off the peg and put them on, and at the door he stopped.

"You will not take the pen."

"I will not take the pen."

He went out to the yard to see about the chaise, and I sat at the table with the box in front of me and the lid shut. The house was quiet. The clock stood on the dresser with its hands where they had stopped, and I had not wound it, and I did not mean to wind it until I had been to Tindle and come back.

The chaise came early. I carried the box out myself and put it under the seat and got in after it and put my hand on it, and the wheel went round in the yard and the horse leaned into the collar, and the house went behind us. Then the road began.

It is a hundred and eleven miles to that gate. I know the road, and I know the inns on it, and I know what it costs. It is the gate I do not know.

The last line in the book is still empty. The line will keep.

---

## Twenty-Two · The Names

The chaise went east out of Collow and the hedges came down to the height of the wheel and the sky got large, and Edward rode up on the box beside the driver with his collar turned up, because there was no room inside with the box on the floor and my feet against the box.

At the first halt the book was open on my knees before the horses had their noses in the water.

It was a house with a bench along the wall and a girl who brought ale out to the yard, and the sun came over the roof and lay along the bench in a long yellow bar, and I put the copybook on my lap and my hand flat on the marbled cover and did not go into it.

I had gone into it once, in the kitchen, with the lamp turned down. That reading gave me one thing worth having, which was that she read it at night after the bar was down, and it cost me an evening to get it.

This was the ordinary reading. Anybody may do it. I turned the cover back and put my thumb on the flyleaf and began at the top of the first page.

***

The first page had a short column of names on it and nothing else. No place, no mark of time, no word about money, no note of what anybody had answered. The hand was the office hand and it was young, and the ink had gone the colour of weak tea, and there was a space between the entries of about the width of a thumb, so that nothing would run together.

Beside some of the names there was a small cross. Beside some there was a dot. Beside some there was nothing at all.

I had tried, in the kitchen, to make the crosses mean the dead. That theory lasted until I found a cross beside a woman who had given me a piece of seed cake in the spring. After that I stopped trying to make the marks say anything and read the page as a page.

The places came later. On the page where the ink thickened, she had begun to put the door after the name, and after that she put the door every time, and the column widened to take it.

*Hollis, the pump*

*Ellerbeck, the mill cottage*

*Nichols, the shop at the cross*

A door may be written down without anybody's leave. A name may not, and she had learned that early, and the learning of it is in the way the pen presses on the first letters and eases off at the end.

The times came later still, and the times were the part I sat over longest.

She had not written a single year in that book. Not one, in forty years, in a book ruled for a child and kept by a woman who had been raised in a clerk's office and could have written the year in her sleep. What she wrote instead was this.

*the year the mill burned*

*the year of the flood*

*the year the great frost*

*the year the road was turned*

*the year the old squire was buried*

*the year the chapel was put up*

I sat on that bench with the sun on my knees and worked it out, and the working out took me the length of a pipe and another mug of ale, and when I had it I put the book down and looked at the yard.

A year is a number, and a number in a book is a claim on a record. A number says, this happened at such a time, and any clerk may check it and any lawyer may stand it beside another number and draw a conclusion. She had no right to a number. The book was hers and the ink was hers, but the years were the parish's, and she had been the clerk's daughter long enough to know what happens to a person who writes the parish's numbers in her own book.

So she wrote them as things that had happened to everybody, and she wrote them so that only somebody who had been there could say when they were.

I could date some of them. The mill that stands at Tindle now is brick and the one before it was timber, and the brick one is not old. The chapel I could date because I had read the meeting house's trust deed at Ely and the deed carried the year before the flood in the top corner.

The frost I could not date. The road I could not date. Those will stay in the book as a phrase on a yellow page, and when the last person in Tindle who remembers them is under the grass there will be nothing behind the phrase at all.

Edward came round the corner of the house with bread in his hand and his hat pushed back.

"You have not moved."

"I have moved a great deal. I have been in ninety-three and in the year of the flood and back again."

He sat down on the end of the bench and put the bread between us and did not take any of it, which meant he was waiting.

"There is not a year in it," I said. "Not one. She wrote the mill burning and the frost and the road being turned, and she never once wrote a figure."

He thought about that with his hands on his knees.

"Because she had no business to."

"Because she had no business to."

"That is a hard discipline for a woman with nobody to check her." He broke the bread. "Most people would have written the year. Most people write the year because the year is the easy part and it makes the rest look like a record."

"She was not making a record." I turned the page. "A record is kept for somebody else to read. She was making a list for herself, and she made it so that nobody else could use it."

He ate, and looked out at the road, and said, "Then how did you read it?"

"Because I have been in this parish for a year," I said, "and I have been reading its books for longer, and I am the last person left who can put a year to a flood."

***

We came into the flat country in the afternoon, and the road ran along the top of a bank with water on both sides of it, and there were willows with their heads cut and a windmill turning without any sail on it, and the light came off the water and into the carriage and lay on the page.

That was when I understood the shape of the book, and it was not the shape I had carried out of Collow.

She had never gone to a door.

I had it in my head that she had walked the parish with a book under her arm, the way I had walked it in the winter with a list. She had not. She had kept a gate. A gate is a place where the whole parish comes to you, one cart at a time, and stands still and waits and cannot go on until the coin is taken and the bar is up.

She had asked them there. Every name in that book had come to her window and stopped, and she had leaned out with the chain across the road and asked them the question while their horse stood in the shafts.

That is why there was no order to it. A book of doors knocked at would run by the roads, lane by lane, and this one did not run by anything. It ran by whoever had come.

I turned back to the beginning and read it again with that in my hand, and it read differently, and it read worse.

*Ann Gow, Long Bank, the year the mill burned, would not say*

I sat with my finger under it.

Mrs. Gow had been first in everything since she was a girl and had arranged the bread, and had told me in the yard, with her arms in a tub, that it is not a hard thing to leave bread on a stone but it is a hard thing to be the one who is seen leaving it.

She had said no. She had stood at that gate with a cart and said no, and then she had gone home and become the woman who leaves the bread, and she had never once said why, and she was not going to be asked.

"Hollis," Edward said. He had the book now, and his finger was going down the column. "Here. The pump. The year of the great frost. And then a letter and a line under it."

"What letter?"

"An N." He turned the book round. "It is an N and a full stop, and it is the only punctuation in the book."

"No," I said. "It is a no."

He looked at it again.

"He gave a coin a week out of a pension," I said, "and would not be talked out of it, and I took it and I have been ashamed of taking it since. He had said no to her first. He was an old man on a pension and he had said no at that gate before I was born, and then he said yes with a coin in his hand, and it took him forty years to get the second answer out."

Edward put his finger on the next line.

"The sisters."

*Ellerbeck, the mill cottage, the year the mill burned, the elder answered*

"The elder answered," he read. "Not, they answered."

"The younger one sat in the parlour and let her sister do it, and when I told them about the girl at the ford the elder said, we were stupid girls, and the younger said nothing at all." I took the book back. "She was asked at that gate when she was young and she let her sister answer for her, and she has been letting her sister answer ever since, and I have sat in their parlour and been glad of the elder and never once looked at the younger."

"You are looking at her now."

"I am looking at a line of ink."

The next page had the estate on it, in a different ink, in a place where the column had room.

*Sowerby, the Hall, the year the old squire died, will consider it*

I laughed, and it came out of me in the carriage and Edward looked up.

"Read it."

"Will consider it." I held the page against the light. "The steward in Hull wrote to me in the spring and said the estate would consider the matter at Michaelmas and that the tenant was not to commit the estate to any payment. He thought he was writing it himself. He was copying out a sentence that had been given at that gate before his father was born."

"Does it say what happened?"

"It says what always happens. A thing that is being considered is not a refusal, and a thing that is not refused does not have to be paid for."

***

The last pages were in the older hand and the lines were further apart and the ink was darker, and there were gaps in the column where a name had been left out and a door written instead, and there was one door I knew.

*the boy at the bar*

There was a cross beside it, and then, further down, in a place where the column had been ruled again, the same words a second time with nothing beside them at all. She had asked him, and she had asked him again. She had asked him when he was a child small enough to stand on the step to be seen, and again when he was tall enough to lift the bar, and she wrote him down both times and neither time wrote an answer.

I shut the book on my thumb and sat with it.

The flat country went by, and a church tower, and sheep washed on a bank, and Edward took his hat off and put it on his knee and said nothing for a long while.

"There is one more," he said at last. "You have not read the top of it."

"I have read the top of it."

"You read it in the kitchen."

"I read the name in the kitchen. I did not read what she wrote after it."

He waited.

"There is nothing after it," I said. "That is what she wrote. Would not say."

***

We came into Tindle in the evening and took rooms at the inn, and Edward went down to see about the horses, and I sat at the table under the window with the lamp lit and the book open and the shutters not quite closed.

*Mary Ann Ashby, the ford, would not say*

The hand that wrote it was young, and the pen had come off the paper hard at the end of the word.

My mother went over that ford with a basket every Wednesday for years. She put the loaf on the step and went away, and one Wednesday she did not go away, and the woman inside the door asked her a question, and my mother stood there, and then she went home.

She never told me. She never told anybody. I have known all my life that my mother did not answer questions, and I had put it down to her temper, and to the chapel, and to a woman's business being her own. It was none of those. She had been asked the one question that a person may not refuse politely, by a woman in a doorway with nothing to give, and she had stood in front of it and said nothing at all, and had gone back the next Wednesday with another loaf and gone on doing it for years.

The basket was the answer. It was the only answer she had, and it was not a thing that could be written down in a book, so the book says, would not say.

Esther wrote that down and then she put the name at the top of the book, above the smith and the ferryman and the wheelwright and every soul in the parish she had asked, and she left it there for forty years.

A refusal is the one thing that does not wear out. You may wear out a man's yes by asking again. A woman's silence is a wall, and Esther Callard spent forty years going back to the same wall.

I turned to the last leaf.

The lines on it were ruled and the ruling was new, and all of them were empty except the one at the top, and under the last of them she had written the two words in ink that was darker than anything else in the book.

*Ask her*

I had thought, in the kitchen at Collow, that it meant me. I had thought it meant a name to be written on the line, and that the name was mine, and that I had been left a place in a book.

It does not mean that.

She asked this parish for forty years, at a gate, with a chain across the road, and she asked my mother at a door with a basket in her hands, and there is not a name in that book she did not ask. She asked the shop at the cross and the kiln and the bar and the boy, and the boy again. She asked the woman at the end of the row, and the woman at the end of the row is in the churchyard now and her name was asked of her before she went.

She did not ask me.

I sat with the lamp and the noise of the yard coming up through the floor, and I understood why.

I came through her door with a case under my arm and a fair copy in the case and a pen in my pocket. I sat at her table and she asked me a question and I answered it with a sentence about a parish. A woman who has been written down for forty years does not answer a question that is being taken down. She knew that. She had known it since she was a girl at a desk with a dying man behind her, writing what other people said into a form.

So she did not ask me. She wrote a note to herself in the last week of her life, and the note says, ask her, and she meant to do it, and she was waiting for a day when I would come without the case.

There was no such day. She ran out of afternoons.

I put the book face down on the table and sat with my hands on it. The last line was still empty. It had been empty in her kitchen and it was empty at the inn at Tindle with the lamp on it and the road behind me, and it would be empty in the morning.

The pen was in the case, and the case was under the bed, and I did not go down on my knees to it.

The line will keep. That much I had been right about. What I had not known was that it was not a line waiting for a name. It was a question with nobody left to put it.

---

## Twenty-Three · Michaelmas Is Not the Point

The mill yard at Tindle was full of barrels, and the wheel was going round, and the noise came over the wall into the lane and up through the soles of my boots.

The girl at the door took my name and was gone and back before I had finished looking at the barrels, which is a courtesy a man learns in a trade where the corn will not wait.

He was at the table with the ledger open and a ruled sheet beside it and the window behind him open on the water. There was a cup at his elbow with a skin on it, and he had been at the accounts a long while.

"Mrs. Rooke."

"Mr. Hesketh."

"You have come to tell me what you have not got."

I sat down without being asked, because the last time I had stood in that room he had told me what standing was, and I had remembered it.

"Yes," I said. "That is exactly what I have come to tell you."

He put down the pen and turned the ledger square with the edge of the table, which is what a man does when he means to give a conversation his whole attention.

"Then tell me," he said, "and be quick about it, and do not dress it."

***

"There is no settlement. There never was one. She was born at Nethercote and there is nobody left in Nethercote who knows her name."

"Go on."

"The order stands. The appeal was entered at Ely and struck out, and the recognizance was estreated, and the money went where the law sends it. The only person living who could swear to what happened in that office is in the churchyard at Tindle with the clay still loose on her."

"Go on."

"The statement was never signed. I have the draft in my case and it is in my hand and it has no name at the foot of it and it will never have one."

"Go on."

"That is all. I have a receipt for twenty pounds that the King took, a copybook with no dates in it, and a dead woman's word for the rest. Not one of the three is a paper that a bench would act on, and you know it, and you knew it before I came up those stairs."

He sat back.

"I have known it since the spring," he said. "I have known it since the day Thwaite told me the old woman at the gate had been a clerk's daughter. A clerk's daughter is the worst witness in England, Mrs. Rooke, and I say that with respect to your trade. She knows exactly how much a piece of paper is worth, and she will not put her hand to one that is worth nothing, and she is right."

"She is dead."

"That does not improve the paper."

"No."

He looked at the window and then at me.

"Then we are where we were in April, and I am sorry for it, and I am not going to pretend to be sorrier than I am." He put his hand flat on the ledger. "The line goes at Michaelmas. I have the vestry's resolution and the overseers will not enter the sum, and there is nothing in that book that will stop it, and nothing you have brought me will stop it either."

"I know."

"Then why have you come the whole way from Collow to tell me a thing I already know?"

"Because I want something from you, and a man is easier to ask when he has just said no."

He laughed. It was a short one and it stopped itself, and he had not done it in front of me before.

***

"Say what you want."

"You have the sheet there," I said. "The one with the Michaelmas accounts on it. Is the eight pounds on it?"

He turned the sheet round with one finger. There was a column ruled down the left side and a column of sums on the right, and there was a space in the middle of the sheet where a line had been left blank.

"I ruled it in the spring," he said. "I have not entered it. I do not enter a sum I am going to take out again."

"Then leave it blank. At Michaelmas you will strike the line in the ledger and you will write nothing in its place. No reason, no minute, no word to the overseers. The book will show a payment that stopped."

"That is what a stopped payment looks like."

"It is what a payment looks like that was never made by anybody who can be asked about it." I put my hands together on the table. "You have been at pains for a year to say that this parish maintained a woman out of the rate without a vote and without a name. You were right. Then let the book say the same thing you have been saying. There was never a name in that column. There is now no sum in it. What is left is nothing, and nothing is not a decision that anybody can be made to answer for."

He did not move. The wheel went round outside. A man in the yard shouted at another man, and got an answer.

"You are asking me to leave a gap," he said.

"I am asking you to leave the gap that is already there. You did not put her in that book. A dead man did, forty years ago, in a hand nobody can call. You are not striking out a person. You are striking out a line that never had a person in it."

"And in September, when the town asks me what I have done with the woman in the turning?"

"You will say there is no such payment in the book."

"That is true."

"It is true, and it is all you have to say, and it is a great deal better than the truth you have been carrying since April, which is that you stopped a woman's bread and called it arithmetic."

He looked at me with his head a little on one side.

"You are a hard woman to be in a room with."

"I have been told."

***

"There is a difficulty," he said, "and it is not the eight pounds, and it never was."

"I know it is not."

"Then say it, since you have come this far."

"You are not afraid of the vestry. You are afraid of the year after." I put my finger on the sheet. "At Michaelmas you renew the mill. The town sets the rent and the town is the men who sit in that vestry, and a man who has turned an old woman out of a cottage is a man a town can charge for the privilege of keeping him. You do not want the eight pounds. You want a decision that reads like a decision and not like a grudge."

He sat back and put his hands behind his head.

"Michaelmas is not the point," he said. "I have been saying that to myself since February. Michaelmas is a day I stand in a room with men who do not count me as one of them and who take my money and set my rent, and every year they set it a little higher because I have nowhere else to go, and every year I pay it, and the only thing I have ever had in that room is that I am a man who does his business in the light." He brought his hands down. "If I take that line out and put nothing in its place, then in the room in September there is a gap in the parish book with my name at the bottom of the page, and a man may be asked about a gap for the rest of his life."

"Not if there is nothing to ask about."

"There is always something to ask about. That is what a vestry is for."

"Then give them nothing to hold." I turned the ledger an inch toward him. "A reason can be argued with. If you write, the payment ceased because there was no settlement, every man in that room may answer you, and the argument will be had in the room and it will be had again next year. If you write nothing, the only answer is that there is no such line. A man cannot argue with a page that is empty. He can only say that it is empty, and then somebody will ask him what he proposes to do about it, and he will have to propose something, and it will be his proposal and not yours."

He sat with that.

"You have thought about this on the road."

"I have thought about it for a fortnight. I had the road and nothing else to do with it."

"There is one more thing," he said, "and it is the only thing that matters, and you have not said it yet."

"Say it for me."

"The money." He tapped the sheet. "You are not proposing that she go without. You are proposing that somebody else carry her, and that the parish not know, and that I not know. A subscription is a story until it is money in a hand. If it is a story, then in a year the seven get tired, and the woman comes back on the parish, and this time it will not be a line that a dead man wrote. It will be a decision, and it will have a date on it, and I will have to make it in the light, and I will make it the other way. I have made that decision already, in this room, in my own head, and I did not care for the man who made it."

"Then do not build it on a story."

"Show me the money."

I took the list out of my case and put it on the table and turned it round.

He read it from the top, with his finger going down the side.

"Names," he said. "No sums."

"The sums are beside them in the hands of the people who will pay. I would not copy another man's hand."

"And this at the bottom."

"Rooke, of Collow. Quarterly, while she lives."

He put his finger on it and left it there.

"Your husband."

"My husband."

"Why?"

"Because he was asked and he said yes, and because he is not of this parish and has no rate here and no vote here and nothing to get by it. And because he is the part of it that is not a story." I put my hand flat on the table. "You asked me for money in a hand. There is a man in Collow who has put his hand to that sheet in his own writing, and he has been paying it since the spring, and he will go on paying it until she dies, and if you want to see him he will come and stand in this room and say so to your face."

Hesketh sat back and looked at the list for a while.

"Is it a subscription or a purchase?" he said. "I have to know which I am looking at, because they are different things in a book and they are different things in a town."

"His name is at the bottom of a list and there are other names above it and none of them is first. A man with other names above him has joined something. A man whose name is the list has bought it."

"And the woman? Has anybody bought her?"

"No."

"Then it is a subscription," he said, "and I will take it as one, and I will not ask to see it again."

***

"Show me the line," I said.

He turned the ledger round and opened it at the place where the ribbon was, and ran his finger down the page until it stopped, and turned the book so that it lay in front of me.

The column had been ruled by a man who was dead before I was born, and the entries were in several hands, and the one I was looking for was the same in every year of it.

*The turning. Eight pounds.*

*The turning. Eight pounds.*

*The turning. Eight pounds.*

Down the page and over and down the next page, in the office hand of whoever held the book that year, and the last of them in Isaac Thwaite's careful even looping hand, and not one word beside any of them to say who the eight pounds was for. No name. No age. No parish. A line in a column for forty years, entered by men who had entered it because it had been entered the year before.

I put my hand on the page.

The room went out.

There was a table and a stool and a man's coat hanging on the back of a door, and a pen going down a column, and the pen stopped at the line and the man did not stop with it. His hand went on to the next line before his eyes had finished the one he had written. There was a fire behind him and a dog on the hearth and somebody in the next room moving a chair, and the eight pounds went into the book the way a stone goes into a wall, and nobody in the room thought about it at all.

Then the room was a room, and the ledger was under my hand, and it was warm, and Hesketh was sitting back in his chair with his fingers together and his eyes on me and no expression on his face at all.

"You went somewhere," he said.

"I did."

"Where?"

"Into a man entering a line."

He looked at the ledger and then at me, and he did not ask another question about it. That was the greatest courtesy anybody showed me that summer.

"That is forty years of it," he said. "I have read that column more times than I have read my own name, and I have never once been able to make it say anything except that the parish paid."

"It says that nobody looked."

"It says that nobody had to look. There is a difference, and it is the difference between a wicked parish and a careless one, and I have not decided which of the two I am living in."

***

He shut the book.

"At Michaelmas I strike the line. I write nothing in its place and I say nothing to the overseers, and if I am asked I will say that there is no such payment in the book, and that will be the truth, and I will say it in a flat voice and look at the man who asked."

"And if the money fails?"

"Then she is on the parish again, and I will be a man who left a gap and a woman fell into it, and I will have made the decision I have spent a year avoiding, and I will make it the other way." He stood up. "Do not let it fail."

"I will not let it fail."

"You cannot promise that. You are not the one who pays."

"No," I said. "But I am the one who asks, and I have been asking for a year, and I have got better at it."

He went to the window and stood with his back to me and his hands behind him, and the wheel went round below and the light came up off the water and moved on the ceiling.

"Mrs. Rooke."

"Mr. Hesketh."

"You have altered a book for one person."

"I have altered it for a person who is not in the book," I said. "That does not count as altering it."

He turned round.

"No," he said. "It does not. That is the trick, and I would not have found it, and I am not certain that I approve of it, and I am going to do it."

***

I went down the stairs and out into the yard, and the noise of the wheel came at me from the side, and I stood by a barrel with my hand on the rim of it until it was only noise again.

There was nothing in my case. No paper with his name on it, no line of writing from his hand, no note of the day or the hour or what had been said. He had promised me a thing in a room with the door shut and there was nothing to show for it and nothing to sue on, and if he changed his mind in September no man in England could say he had ever said otherwise.

That was the arrangement. It was the only arrangement that would hold, and it held because it could not be produced.

The girl at the door said good day to me. I said good day to her, and went out through the gate and up the lane toward the town with the sun on the water and the smell of the river coming up over the wall.

I had come to that mill with a case full of paper and I went away with the case empty, and it was the first time in a year that I had come out of a room with less than I carried in, and the less was what I had come for.

---

## Twenty-Four · The Turning

I left the case under the bed at the inn with the lock turned and the key in my pocket, and I went out through the yard and up the street with nothing in my hands.

That was deliberate and it cost me something. The case had the receipt in it, and the copybook, and the draft with no name at the foot of it, and I had carried that case into every room I had entered for a year the way a man carries a gun into a field, not because he means to fire it but because a man with a gun is treated differently from a man without one. I walked out of the inn without it and my hands were light and wrong, and I put them in my coat pockets and took them out again, and by the end of the street I had stopped doing that.

The town gave out at the last house and the road went on between hedges with the may still out on the tops and a smell of wet grass and cow parsley, and the sky was low and white and the light came through it flat, without shadows, so that the fields on either side had no depth to them and looked like a thing painted on a board.

The boy was at the same field.

He was on the hedge with his stick and the dog was at the gap, and he was a year older than he had been when I first came down that road and he had the same stick, and when he turned his head and knew me he got down off the hedge and came to the gate.

"You are back," he said.

"I am back."

"Are you going down the lane?"

"I am."

He thought about it, and the dog thought about it, and the boy and the dog stood at the gate with the look of creatures who have a piece of news and are deciding whether it is theirs to give.

"She has been at the window," he said. "All the week. My father says she is watching for the cart."

"What cart?"

"The one that comes for her." He said it without any weight on it, the way a boy says a thing that was said at a table and that he has not thought about. "My father says when the parish stops paying they send a cart and take the furniture for the rate."

"Your father is wrong."

"He is not often wrong."

"He is wrong about this," I said. "There is no cart."

The boy looked at me and then at the lane and then at me again, and I understood that he had been carrying that cart about with him for a week and had not liked it, and that he had brought it to the gate to give it to somebody.

"There is no cart," I said again. "If a cart comes down that lane it will be because I have sent for it, and I have not sent for one, and I am not going to."

"All right," he said, and let the dog out of the gap, and went back up the hedge with his stick over his shoulder.

***

I have been down that lane before, and I have counted it as two visits, and this was the third.

The first time I had a loaf in my hands still warm through the cloth and I put it on the stone and did not knock, and the door opened a hand's width and a woman asked me who I was, and I said Mrs. Rooke of Collow, and she said she knew what I was called and asked who I was, and I could not answer her, and she shut the door on me with her hand flat against it.

The second time was the same afternoon, and I have thought about that a good deal since, because the woman who walked down that lane with the bread and the woman who walked back up it were not the same woman, and the lane had been walked by both of them.

The hawthorn was in leaf and the lane was dry and the hedges had been cut back since the spring, and the cottage at the bottom had the same roof and the same chimney and the same thin blue smoke coming out of it that I had seen in the winter, and the stone by the door had the same hollow worn in its front edge.

The yard had been swept. That was the first thing, and it was swept the way a yard is swept by a person who has the time, with the lines of the besom showing in the grit and the leaves in a heap by the wall and a bucket standing beside the heap. There was a water butt under the eave and a chopping block with the axe standing in it and a bundle of kindling tied with a withy, and the kindling was split fine, and there was a great deal of it, as if somebody had been at the woodpile all winter and had not stopped.

The door was shut.

I stood at the bottom of the yard for a moment. There was nothing in my hands to put on the stone. That had been the arrangement for years and I had broken it, and I stood there with the broken arrangement in full view of the window, and the window was dark and I could not tell whether there was anybody behind it.

***

A hen came out from under the sill and went across the grit with one foot scraping, and stopped and put her head on one side at me, and went on. There were two more at the woodpile standing on one leg in the shade of it, and a line was tied from a nail at the corner of the house to the top of the stack, and on the line there was a cloth dried stiff and standing out from it in the shape of somebody's shoulders, and the bottom corner of it hung dark and heavy and had not finished drying.

The butt was full. There was green on the hoop and the staves had swollen shut, and a skin had come on the water with a leaf in the middle of it, and when the hen passed under it the skin broke and came together again.

The wood was stacked with the cut ends out and the bark on, and the ends had gone grey and split across the middle of them, and there were cobwebs in the gaps that had not been disturbed. Somebody had been at that stack all winter and had stopped, and the stopping showed at the top of it: the last course was laid loose and anyhow, the way a person lays wood they mean to come back to. The block had a dish worn into the middle of it and the dish was full of chips that had gone black with wet.

The stone by the door had been swept. The hollow in the front edge of it had rain in it and the rain had a film on it, and there was nothing on the stone, and there had been something on it every Monday for as long as I had known about it, and the place where the something had sat was a paler place than the rest of the stone and would not darken.

The yard smelled of turf smoke and of wet grit, and the chimney was drawing, and there was not a sound in it but the hen.

Then there was a voice, from the other side of the door. It came through the wood without being raised.

"There is nothing in this house for you."

I put my eyes on the door. The glass to the left of it was old and had run, and it was dark behind it, and I could not have said whether there was a face at it.

"I have not come for anything."

"That is what they say at a door."

"It is true at this one," I said. "I have nothing in my hands. You may look at them."

"I have looked at them."

"Then you know I have not come for anything."

There was a quiet then, long enough for the hen to go under the sill and come out again, and I stood in the middle of a swept yard with the cloth on the line moving a little and nothing else moving at all.

"What have you come for, then?"

"To stand in your yard."

"That is a poor thing to come a long way for."

"I have come further for worse," I said.

***

Then I went up to the door and knocked.

There was a sound inside that I could not place at first, and then I had it, which was the sound of a chair being set down on stone. She had been in the chair at the window and she had got up out of it and set it back where it stood, and she had done that before she came to the door, and she had done it in no hurry at all.

It opened.

Not a hand's width. It came back all the way against the wall with the draught of it moving the hem of my coat, and the woman stood in the middle of the doorway with her hand still on the latch, and she looked at my hands before she looked at my face.

"Come in," she said.

***

The room was one room. It was as clean as a room can be that has a fire in it.

The floor was stone and it had been scrubbed and there was a hollow worn in it in front of the chair, a pale place where the stone had gone down under a foot over a long time, and the hollow was in the shape of a foot that stands and then sits.

The table was against the wall and the wood had gone white with scrubbing and the grain stood up in it. There was a knife on it with the handle worn to the shape of a grip, and a loaf under a cloth, and a salt dish, and a cup, and a jug.

The bed was in the corner with a quilt on it, and the quilt was squares of old cloth stitched edge to edge, and I could read the squares the way I read everything, because a quilt is a record if a person knows how to look at one. There was a blue that had been a coat and a brown that had been a skirt and a red that had been something better than either, and there was a square in the middle that was the colour of the inside of a pocket.

The room smelled of turf and soap and of greens boiled a long time, and under that there was cold stone and the sweetness of the butt water, and it was the cleanest room I had been in since my mother's, and it had been cleaned by a pair of hands for a long time without anybody to clean it for.

The shelf had a lidded tin on it and a comb and a candle in a dish and a bundle of string and a stone bottle with a rag in the neck.

There was a fire of turf and small wood, kept in and not built up, and the ash had been raked back and there was a shovelful of it in a box beside the grate, and the pot on the hob had a cloth under the lid to keep the steam in.

And there was no glass on the wall.

There was a nail where a glass would have hung, at the height of a woman's eyes, and there was nothing on the nail, and the plaster round the nail was the same colour as the plaster everywhere else in the room, so that whatever had been there had not been there for a long time, or had never been there at all.

I did not ask about the nail.

There was no paper in that room. Not a bill, not a label, not a text on the wall, not a scrap in the window. I had been in houses where the walls were papered with the parish's notices and the almanac and a text over the bed, and this room had nothing written in it anywhere, and I stood in the middle of it with my hands at my sides and understood that I had come into the one room in Tindle where nothing had ever been entered.

She moved her chin at the stool by the door.

I sat on it.

It was low. My knees came up near my chin, and I put my hands on them, and I knew before I had settled that this was the stool a young woman had sat on with a paper in her lap and a basket on the floor at her feet.

The chair was oak and it had arms and a back that had been rubbed pale at the top by a hand going over it, and there was a folded blanket on the seat. It stood square to the window, not to the table and not to the fire, and the window looked straight up the lane.

From that chair a person could see the gap at the top of the lane where the road went by, and nothing else in the world, and the gap was a pale door-shaped piece of light with the hedge on either side of it.

She sat down in it. She turned it a quarter with her hand on the arm so that she had the window on her right and me on her left, and she put her hands in her lap.

The glass in the window was old and had run, and the lane through it was bent, and the gap at the top was a bright place with the hedge bent round it. A person in that chair had been looking at that gap for years, and the gap had been empty for most of them, and nobody goes on looking at an empty gap unless the looking has become the work.

She did not ask me anything.

The fire moved. A piece of turf went down and the ash came up and settled.

***

I had thought a great deal about what I would say, on the road and in the inn and on the walk out of the town, and every version of it had begun with the same few words, and now I was in the room and the words were no use to me.

"Mrs. Callard is dead," I said. "Esther. The woman at the gate."

"I know."

"Did somebody tell you?"

"Nobody tells me anything." She looked at the window. "The bread came on the Monday and it has not come since."

"There is a girl who brings it."

"There was a girl who brought it."

The loaf on the table under the cloth was small and round, and it had been baked in a pot on the hearth, and it was not the shape of the loaves that came down that lane on a Monday. There was a meal sack in the corner with the neck rolled down, and it was not full, and it was not empty.

I had my hands on my knees and the stool creaked under me when I shifted, and I stopped shifting.

"The bell went up the road," she said. "On the Thursday."

That was the funeral. The bell had gone up the road for it, and she had been sitting in that chair, and she had worked it out by herself in a room where nobody had told her anything for forty years.

"You did not come up for it," I said.

"No."

"Why not?"

She turned her head and looked at me, and her face was the face I had seen in the lane in the winter, and there was nothing in it that wanted anything from me.

"Because they would have had to stop and speak to me," she said. "And then it would have been a thing that happened at a gate, and not a thing that happened to her."

I sat with that. The fire went on. Somewhere behind the cottage a hen was making the long complaining noise a hen makes in the middle of the day for no reason.

"You have had the trouble of her," she said.

"I have."

"Was it a trouble?"

"Yes."

"Good," she said. "Then it was worth something."

***

I got up after a while, because sitting on a low stool with my knees up had put a cramp in my back and because I did not know what to do with my hands.

The chair was between me and the window. I put my hand on the top of the back of it, on the pale place where a hand went. The room went out.

There was a lane and a gap of light at the top of it and a stone by a door with bread on it, and the light came across the floor and went up the wall and the wall was the same wall, and then the light came again from the other end and the floor had a hollow in it that it had not had before. There was a hand on the sill. There was a winter in it, and a summer, and a winter again, and a man coming down the lane with a cart and turning it round at the bottom and going back up without stopping, and there was nobody at the gap for a long time, and the hand stayed on the sill, and the shadow of the hedge went out across the floor and came back and the hollow in the stone got deeper by a fraction that nobody would ever measure.

Then the room was a room, and the fire was at my back, and the wood under my palm was warm, and the woman was standing at the table with the jug in her hand.

She had been standing there long enough for the jug to be steady.

I took my hand off the chair.

"Sit down," she said, and I sat, and she poured water into the cup and set it on the table at my elbow, and the water was cold and it had come out of the butt and there was a little grit of ash on the surface of it that she did not trouble to take off.

She stood with the jug in her hand and looked at me, and I looked at the cup, and the quiet in that room was not the quiet I had been afraid of. It was a room with both of us in it and nothing being asked.

I had come the length of the road with a case full of paper and had left the case under a bed, and I had come down that lane with nothing in my hands, and I had sat in that room for the length of a long conversation and had not once been asked what I wanted, which is the thing I had been braced for since Collow.

She put the jug down.

"Have you eaten?" she said.

I had the cup in both hands and the warmth of the room in my back and the light from the lane on my face. I had been asked a great deal in that parish and every question had a hook in it. This one had none, and it took me a moment to find the answer.

"No," I said.

She went to the shelf for the loaf.

---

## Twenty-Five · The Cup

She cut the bread on the table with the knife and the knife was dull and she did not sharpen it, and the crust came away in a ragged edge and the crumb was dry and pale, and she put the piece down on the wood in front of me and pushed the salt dish along beside it.

I ate. It was yesterday's bread and it had been kept under a cloth and it was better than anything I had eaten at the inn. The salt was coarse and there was a good deal of it and it made the dry bread into a meal, and I put too much on the next piece and ate it anyway, and she let me do it and said nothing about it.

She ate hers standing at the table with one hand under it to catch the crumbs, and then she sat down in the chair and put her hands in her lap and looked at the window, and we stayed like that for a while with the fire making its small noise and the cup going cold at my elbow.

There is a particular sound a room makes when one person is eating and the other is not, and I have never been able to be comfortable in it, and I ate faster than I wanted to because of it.

"You have come about the money," she said.

"I have come about a great many things. The money is one of them."

"Say the money first. It is the shortest."

So I said it. I told her about the vestry and the resolution and the man with the schedule, and about the eight pounds a year that had been paid out of a parish book in respect of a person in the turning, and about the line that would be struck at Michaelmas, and about the seven households, and about the list with the names on it and the sum at the bottom of it in my husband's hand.

She listened with her hands in her lap and her eyes on the window, and she did not move, and when I had finished she was quiet for long enough that I thought she had not followed it.

Then she said, "I have never seen the money."

"No."

"It came as bread. And coal. And a man to do the roof."

"That is what it came as."

"Then it was never money." She turned her head. "It was a line in a book, and the bread was what the line turned into."

"Yes."

"Then it is the line that is the trouble and not the money, and you have been running about the country for a year about a line." She said it without contempt. It was the flat statement of a person who has had forty years to think about a thing and has arrived at the end of the thinking.

"I have."

"Why?"

***

That was the question. It was the one Hesketh had asked me in the yard with the wheel going round, and it was the one Esther had asked me at her table with her hand flat on the wood, and it had come round again in a room with a swept floor and a fire kept in and no glass on the wall.

"Because you are not in the book," I said.

"That is not a reason. That is a description."

My hands lay on my knees and I did not move them.

"No," I said. "It is not a reason. I have been a year looking for the reason and I have not got one. I have had the whole road from Collow to find one on and I have not found it, and I am not going to invent one at your table to make myself look better than I am."

She looked at me for a while.

"Then say it the way it is."

"I do not know why."

"Good," she said, and looked back at the window.

After a while she got up and took the pot off the hob with the cloth and set it down on the hearthstone, and lifted the lid and stirred what was in it with a stick and put the lid back and the pot back, and all of it was done with the same unhurried exactness, and her hands were the hands of a woman who had done the same small work in the same square of floor for a very long time. There was a burn on the back of her right hand, old and white, the shape of the lip of a pot.

The fire took a piece of turf and the smoke went up and the room smelled of it, and I sat on the stool with the dry bread in my hand and the cup at my elbow and nothing in my case and nothing to say, and it was the easiest I had been in a year.

***

"Mrs. Callard is dead," I said.

"You said."

"She died in the morning. I was with her. It took her a while."

"It takes everybody a while."

"She could not speak at the end. I asked her questions and she blinked for the answers." I put the bread down. "I did not read her."

She turned her head at that, and her face did not change, and she waited.

"I can read a thing that has been handled," I said. "I have done it since I was a girl. A chair, a pen, a latch, a paper. I put my hand on it and I am where it was. I could have taken her hand and had every year of it, and she could not have stopped me, and she would never have known."

"And you did not."

"No."

"Why not?"

"Because a reading is a statement taken without a signature."

She sat with that. The fire went down and she put her foot out and pushed a piece of turf back into it with the toe of her boot, and the ash went up and settled on the hearth.

"Then you are a better clerk than the ones they had," she said. "They never asked me to sign anything either, and they took it all the same."

***

"There is a thing I have to say about her," I said, "and I do not know how to say it, so I will say it badly. She put her mother's money down for you. It was taken off her at Ely, and it went to the King, and there is a receipt for it on the wall of a toll house and a copy of the receipt in a box, and she was still paying for that afternoon when she died."

"I know what she put down."

"You knew?"

"She told me." She looked at the stool, which was the stool I was sitting on, and then at me. "She sat where you are sitting with a paper in her lap and told me what she had done and what it would cost."

"And she asked you why you would not tell her what to do."

"Yes."

"What did you say?"

"I said, because it would be mine. Everything has been mine. I wanted one thing that somebody chose."

"And she asked you what you wanted."

"She asked me what I wanted."

The room was quiet. A cart went up the lane at the top of the field and the sound of it came down the gap and went away.

"Nobody else has," she said.

"She kept your name at the top of a book for forty years," I said. "Above the smith and the ferryman and every soul in the parish. She wrote it there and she never wrote anything after it."

"That is not the same as coming."

"No," I said. "It is not."

She turned her hand over on her knee and looked at the palm of it.

"She never came back," she said. "I have thought about that more than I have thought about the money, and I have decided nothing about it."

***

I had not meant to say the next thing, and I have thought since about whether I would have said it if the bread had been new.

"I have read the burial register at Tindle."

Her hands were on her knees and they did not move, and everything in the room got very still, and the fire, which had been making its noise, went on making it.

"There is a line in it for the February of ninety-three with no name on it."

"There is no name on it," she said, "because nobody asked for one."

I did not say anything.

"The man came with the book. He asked me how old she was and whether she had been baptised, and I said she had not, and he wrote it down. He did not ask me what she was called. He had a line ruled for the name, and he wrote, a child, and shut the book."

"And you did not tell him."

"No."

"Would you have?"

She looked at me.

"No," she said.

I sat with the dry bread in my hand and the salt between us and the fire going.

"We came from Nethercote in the February," she said. "It rained the whole road. The parish put us over the stone at the boundary, and the next parish put us over the next one. There was a man with each of us at every stone. The men were not cruel. They had a paper for each handover, and the paper said where we had come from and where we were going, and nothing about the child except that she was with me."

She stopped and put her hand out to the cup and did not lift it, and then she put the flat of it on the table, the way she had put it down on the wood in the toll house forty years before, and left it there.

"She was on my back under the shawl. She coughed the whole way and the cough got into her chest and stayed there. She was dead before the week was out."

"They walked her to death," she said. "A paper at every stone, and not one of the papers had her name on it."

The fire moved. I put the bread down on the table and did not eat any more of it.

"They laid her in the north corner under the wall, where the ground is soft. There was no stone and no fee. The man read a piece out of the book, and it was the piece they read over everybody, and it did not have her name in it, because there was no name in the book to put in it."

"Who was there?"

"Me. And the man with the book. And a woman from the row who held the shawl."

"Nobody from the parish."

"The parish was there," she said. "The parish had a man with a book."

"Have you been to it?"

She looked at the window.

"It is up the road," she said.

I sat with that. The churchyard was up the road and over the bridge and past the last house, and she had not been up the road in forty years, and the corner under the north wall was a short walk from her own door.

***

I have written a great many things down. I have written down what a woman said in a kitchen and what a man said in a vestry, and I have made a living out of the fact that a thing written on a Tuesday can be read on a Friday by somebody who was not there, and I had never once in my life sat in a room where the only way to keep a thing was to leave it unwritten.

"What was she called?" I said.

She looked at me for a long time. The fire went down between us and the light on the wall went down with it, and the room got small.

Then she said the name.

She said it once, in the flat Tindle voice, and she did not say it again, and she did not explain it, and she did not tell me where it came from or who she had been named for or whether anybody else alive knew it.

I am not going to put it down.

There is a reason and it is not a delicate one. That name was said in a room with the door shut, to me alone, forty years after the last person who might have used it was laid in a corner under a wall. It was not said so that it could be entered. If I put it on a page it becomes a thing that can be read by a man who never met her, in another age, and it becomes the parish's again, and it has taken forty years to get it out of the parish.

So it stays where it was said. I have it, and it will go when I go, and that is all I can do for her.

She put her hand out and turned the cup round on the table so that the chipped side faced away from me, and the chip was old and the rim had been worn thin on one side by a mouth.

"Eat," she said.

"I have eaten."

"Eat the rest of it. It will be dry tomorrow."

I ate the rest of it. She got up and put a piece of turf on the fire and stood over it until the flame took, and the light in the room changed and the corner behind the bed went dark, and she sat down again.

***

I took the ring out of my pocket. It had been in the pocket of my coat for a fortnight and it had worked its way into the corner of the lining and I had to turn the pocket out to get it, and it came out warm, and I held it in my hand for a moment with my thumb in the band where the finger had been.

Then I put it on the table between us.

It was brass and thin and there was no stone in it, and the inside was worn bright in a band where it had turned against a finger for a long time, and it lay on the scrubbed wood with the light on it and it looked like what it was, which was a thing worn to the shape of an answer.

"She wore it at the gate," I said. "A woman behind a counter with a ring on her hand is a married woman, and a married woman is not asked the question that a girl is asked. She wore it so that nobody would have to ask. She copied the receipt before she nailed it up and she put the copy in a box with this and a book, and she left the box to me, and I have carried it from Collow, and I am not carrying it any further."

Tace looked at the ring.

"She had it on her hand when she sat on that stool," she said.

"Yes."

She looked at it a while longer. Her hands stayed in her lap.

I do not know what I had expected. I had carried that ring in a box and then in my pocket for a fortnight, and somewhere on the road I had got it into my head that the thing to do with it was to put it into the hand it had been worn for, and that the hand would close on it, and that something would be finished.

The hand did not move.

"Take it back to Collow," she said. "Put it in the box with the book."

"It was hers."

"It was hers," Tace said. "She is dead, and I have no use for a ring that says I am married."

I folded my hands.

The light from the lane came through the glass and lay across the table and went off the brass and up the wall, and the fire settled, and neither of us said anything for a while, and the ring stayed where I had put it, in the middle of the table, between the salt and the bread.

---

## Twenty-Six · Ask Me

I put the ring in my pocket and stood up.

The table had a faint circle on it where the brass had lain, and the circle stayed after the ring was gone, and neither of us wiped it off. The stool went back against the wall with the sound a stool makes on stone, and I let it go and stayed on my feet.

I have asked a great many people a great many things. I have asked from chairs, and from the edge of a bed with a woman's hand in mine, and from a bench in a yard with a cup going cold at my elbow, and once, in a room at Barrowfield, I asked a man a question with my hand flat on the table between us so that he would have to look at the hand. I had never asked anything on my feet.

Hesketh told me in his office that standing is a way of not being in a room. He said he had men stand in front of him and tell him their business all day, and not one of them had told him the truth. He was right about the standing and wrong about the reason. A person who stands is not trying to be comfortable. I did not want to be comfortable.

I put my right foot into the hollow in front of her chair. The hollow took it the way a shoe takes a foot, and I left it there, and the stone was cold through the sole and stayed cold.

She was in the chair with her hands in her lap and her thumbs together. The light from the lane lay on the floor between us and had begun to go up the wall.

I had rehearsed on the road. I had rehearsed the way a person rehearses who has spent a year in other people's rooms, which is to say I had prepared answers and not a request, and every answer I had prepared was an answer to a question nobody had put to me. I had a defence of the vestry and a defence of the eight pounds and a defence of my own conduct from Barrowfield to that lane, and I had nothing at all ready for the only thing I had come to say, because a request is not a thing you can prepare. It has to be said by the person who wants it, in their own mouth, with their own breath in it.

I did not take the paper out of my pocket. It had one line on it and a space after the word, and the space was mine, and I was not going to fill it with ink in front of her and I was not going to hand it over to be read. What I had was not written down anywhere. I said it in the plain words I had, and I said it standing.

"I have asked for other people my whole life. Ask me. I will answer."

***

She did not move.

The fire took a piece of turf and the flame went up the back of the grate and came down again. The ash lifted and settled. Behind the cottage a hen made the long complaining noise a hen makes in the middle of the afternoon for no reason at all, and a cart went along the road at the top of the lane and the sound of it came down the gap and away, and no other cart came.

Her hands stayed in her lap. Her thumbs were together and the skin over the knuckles was white and thin, and there was the old burn on the back of the right one in the shape of the lip of a pot. She looked at me the way she looked at the window, which is to say without hurry and without giving anything away, and the light went off the floor and onto the wall by the width of a hand.

Then she said, "No."

It was not said hard. She said it the way she said the price of a thing, and she did not look away while she said it, and when she had said it she went on looking at me, and the looking was worse than the word.

"Why?"

"I will not."

"That is not an answer."

"It is the answer I have."

I stood in the hollow with my hands at my sides. There was a small draught coming under the door and it was cold on the back of my neck, and it was June, and the cold was the only thing in that room that was doing anything.

I have been given that answer before. It was in a back kitchen at Barrowfield, and the woman had her hands in a basin and did not take them out, and when I asked her she said, I will not, and I wrote at the foot of the page, would not say, and shut the book and went out to the chaise, and the paper went to a lawyer at Ely and a man read it in a room I was not in. I had not thought about her hands in the basin for a year. I thought about them now, in a room where I was the one who had asked and the one who was not answering.

"You have answered every question I have asked you in this room," I said.

"I have answered the ones about bread."

"The payment stops at Michaelmas. That is not a threat and it is not mine. If you will not be asked by me, be asked by the vestry. Write to them. Say what you want of them and I will carry it."

"The vestry does not ask," she said. "It writes."

"And I am the one who writes."

"You are the one who writes," she said. "That is why I answer nothing."

"Ask me one thing. Anything. Ask me what I did at Barrowfield."

"No."

"Ask me what I have written down."

"No."

"Ask me what I am."

She looked at me for longer over that one. The fire moved and the shadow of the pot went across her apron and back.

"I know what you are," she said.

"Then ask me what I want."

"No."

The fire had gone to a red heart with grey over it. I put my hands behind my back and took hold of one wrist with the other hand and stood there like a woman waiting in a line at a counter.

"Do you want me to go?"

"No."

"Then what am I to do?"

"Stand there."

So I stood there.

The parish would meet at Michaelmas and strike the line out of the book, and a woman of her age in a cottage at the end of a lane would have no bread coming down that lane on a Monday and no coal and no man to do the roof, and the line that had kept her for forty years would be struck through, and after that there would be weather. That was what I had come about. It had gone very small in the room. A refusal is a large thing and it takes up the space that the reason for the journey was in, and I stood in that space with my hands behind my back and could not get at the reason any more.

***

She got up out of the chair.

It took her a moment. She put her hand on the arm and leaned on it and stood, and then stood still with her head down before she went anywhere, and I had not known until that evening that her knees were bad, and I have thought since about how much of her life had been arranged around a thing she never named.

She went to the hearth and took a piece of turf from the box and put it on the fire and pushed it in with the toe of her boot, and the flame came up round it and made the room a shade brighter and the corner behind the bed a shade darker. Then she took the pot off the hob with the cloth and set it down on the hearthstone and lifted the lid and stirred what was in it with the stick and put the lid back and the pot back, and every part of it in the order it was always done, and none of it hurried.

Her back was to me. I talked to her back.

"I have been in this parish a year," I said. "I have asked the smith and the ferryman and the woman at Long Bank and the man at the pump and the sisters at the mill cottage, and every one of them answered me, and I wrote down what they said, and the book with their answers in it is at the inn under a bed with the lock turned."

She wiped the table with the cloth, working round the circle the ring had left, and folded the cloth and put it on the shelf.

"They answered because the book was open," she said.

"The book is not open now."

"No."

"That is the difficulty."

She turned round then. She stood with her back to the fire and her hands at her sides and her chin up a little, and she was a small woman and she filled the room anyway.

"Sit down," she said.

"No."

The word came out of me before I had decided on it, and it came out flat, and it carried her voice and not mine, and I did not take it back.

She did not answer it. She went past me to the window, and the chair stood empty behind her with the blanket folded on the seat and the hollow in front of it where my foot had been, and she stood at the glass with her hands on the sill and looked up the lane at the gap where the road went by. The gap was a pale door-shaped piece of light with the hedge bent round it, and there was nothing in it, and she looked at it the way she had looked at it for forty years while the parish brought her bread and went away again.

Both of us standing in a room with a chair in it, and neither of us in the chair.

***

I went out into the yard.

The light outside was better than the light inside and the air was moving, and I stood by the water butt with my hand on the iron hoop and the hoop was cold and wet and there was green on it. The yard had been swept. The lines of the besom showed in the grit and the leaves were in a heap by the wall and the besom stood against the wall with its head down. The kindling was tied with a withy and split fine, and there was a great deal of it, and the axe stood in the chopping block with the handle worn pale where a hand went.

The hen came round the corner of the cottage and looked at me and went away again.

I put my hands in the butt. The water was colder than the air and colder than the stone, and it came up over my wrists and the cold went up my arms and stopped at the elbow, and I stood like that with my hands in the water and my eyes on the gap at the top of the lane.

Nothing came down it.

I had come down that lane the first time with a loaf in my hands and nowhere to put it but a stone, and I had come down it the second time with nothing in my hands at all, and I had thought that the second coming was the harder one. It was not. The hard thing was to stand in a yard with your hands in cold water after you have asked, and to have nothing to do next.

I dried my hands on my skirt and went back in.

She was in the chair. The light had gone up the wall and was near the top of it and the room had the flat even colour a room has when the sun has left the floor.

There was a road up that lane and a horse at the inn and a case under a bed with the lock turned, and a man at that inn who would have the tea made and would not ask me one question about the day, and it was all of it within an hour of walking. I have left a great many rooms. I am good at it. The thing a person needs for leaving is not courage and it is not a reason; it is a hand on the latch, and my hand knows that latch now, and it did not go to it.
