She did not ask me to stay and she did not ask me to go. She sat in the chair with her hands still and left the door shut, and a shut door is not a refusal and not an invitation, and I stood in the middle of her floor and understood that I was going to have to decide it myself with nobody's help.

I did not decide it. I stayed where I was.

***

"I will ask you for one thing," I said, "and then I will go."

"Ask."

"Say that you have heard me."

The fire made its small noise.

"I have heard you," she said.

"Then say my name."

She did not say it. She sat with her hands together and her eyes on me and she did not say it, and the quiet went on long enough for the fire to need nothing and for the light to leave the top of the wall.

"I know what you are called," she said.

"You said that at the door in the winter."

"I said it then and I say it now."

"Why will you not say it?"

"Because a name said in this room is a name I have to keep."

I stood with that. I have kept a name in my mouth for a year and not written it down, and I had thought that the keeping of it was the hardest thing a person could be asked to do with a name, and she had been keeping one for forty years in a room with nothing written in it, and she was telling me she would not take on another.

"Then I have nothing to put it in," I said.

"No," she said.

The light was off the wall. The fire was the only light in the room and it was low, and she did not build it up, and the corner where the bed was had gone dark, and the nail on the wall with nothing on it had gone dark with it. The room was the size of a room again.

I did not sit down. My legs had begun to ache at the back of the knee, the way they ache after a long time in one place, and the ache was the only part of the evening I could measure. Since Barrowfield I have put questions down in front of people the way a man puts down a sack, and I have stood in their rooms and waited for them to pick it up, and when they had answered I have written it down and gone out and shut the door behind me. I never once stayed to see what the room was like afterwards. A person who answers is left holding the answer.

I had done that to a great many people. It had not been done to me.

The refusal did not go anywhere. It stayed in my hands with nothing to hold, and the room stayed the size it was, and she sat in the chair. That was not what I had braced myself for. She did not send me out. She did not get up and open the door and stand aside. She let me stay in her room with the refusal in my hands, and she did not soften it and she did not explain it, and the fire went down, and the light went out of the gap at the top of the lane, and when it was dark enough to want a candle she did not light one.

I stood in her room in the dark, and she let me.

---

## Twenty-Seven · Because You Would Answer

It rained in the night and the lane was running.

The water came down the ruts in brown threads and stood in the hollows where the carts had been, and the hedge was heavy and put its wet against my shoulder as I went down, and the may was over. The leaves were full and dark and the whole lane smelled of wet stone. The field at the top was empty. The boy was not at the gap. The dog was not at the gap. The grass was laid flat where they stood of an afternoon.

The smoke came out of her chimney and went sideways. The wind was up. The door was shut, and there was water standing in the hollow of the stone by her door so that the hollow held a little pool and the pool shook when I knocked.

She opened it a hand's width and looked at my hands before she looked at my face.

"You came back," she said.

"I came back."

"You are wet."

"It is raining."

She stood back and I went in, and she shut the door with her hand flat on it and the latch went down, and the room was dim and full of smoke.

***

The fire was not going.

The turf was wet through. It sat in the grate and smoked and would not take, and she was on her knees in front of it with the ash raked back and a piece of kindling in her hand, and she had been at it long enough for the smoke to have got into her eyes. She put the kindling in and leaned down and blew on it, and the turf went red at one edge and then grey, and the smoke came up in her face.

I stood by the door with the rain still on my shoulders.

I did not put my hand out to her. A hand put out to that woman would have been refused, and she would have had to be the one who refused it, and I had made her refuse enough in one evening.

She got up. It took her a while and she used the edge of the hearth and then the table, and when she was up she stood with her hand on the table and looked at the fire she had failed to build, and then she sat down in the chair and put her hands in her lap.

"There is dry wood in the box," she said. "Under the turf."

I put the wet pieces to one side and took the dry from the bottom of the box and built the fire up small and close, the way she built it, and it took, and the flame came up blue at the root and yellow at the top, and the smoke went up the chimney instead of into the room.

I sat down on the stool. I did not wait to be told.

She turned her head at that and looked at me for a moment.

"You are sitting," she said.

"I am sitting."

"Yesterday you would not."

"Yesterday I was standing."

She looked back at the fire.

***

"You want the reason," she said.

"I want the reason."

"Because you would answer."

The fire made its noise. The rain went on against the glass in small handfuls, and a drip came off the eave and kept time with nothing.

"That is not a reason," I said. "That is a description of me."

"It is both. Most reasons are."

I sat with my hands on my knees and looked at her, and she looked at the fire, and the smoke went up between us.

"Say it again," I said.

"Because you would answer." She said it without any change in her voice, the second time as flat as the first. "A man came to my door in the winter with a schedule and asked me my age and whether I had a settlement, and I did not answer him, and he wrote down what he had come with and went away. The girl who brought the bread asked me nothing at all, and I gave her a cup of water and she went. The vicar asked me whether I was in want and I said no and he was glad, and he has been glad for forty years. You are the first person who has come down that lane in forty years who would take the answer and do something with it, and that is why I will not ask you."

"That is a reason to be careful with me," I said. "It is not a reason to refuse me."

"It is the same reason."

"It is not."

"It is the same reason," she said, "and you know it is, and you are arguing because arguing is what you do instead of sitting still."

I sat quite still.

***

"A question is a bill," she said.

She said it slowly, the way she said the things she had thought about a long while, and she kept her eyes on the fire.

"You put it down in front of a person. They pay it or they do not pay it. If they do not pay it, nothing has happened and you may ask them again, and I have asked a great many people who did not pay. If they pay it, they have bought something with it. They have bought it with their own mouth, and they cannot sell it back, and it stays bought."

"That is a tidy account of a question," I said. "By it, a doctor may not ask a man where it hurts."

"A doctor is sent for."

"And a woman who has been paid for by seven households for forty years may not ask the next whether she is warm enough."

"The next may see for himself."

"Then nothing may ever be asked," I said, "and the book at the inn with the names in it is a list of injuries, and I have spent a year adding to it."

"Some of them are injuries," she said. "You have written down what people told you at their own doors and carried it away in a case, and you have been right to do it, and it has cost them. Both of those are true. You do not get to have only the one."

"And you have taken one from me," she said.

"From you?"

"In the winter. Your hand on the back of that chair, and your face gone away from me. I stood at this table with the jug in my hand and let you go, and I know what it looks like when a person is somewhere else, and I have known since I was a girl. You had my forty years off a piece of wood and you did not ask me for one of them."

"That is not the same as a question."

"No," she said. "It is worse. And I said nothing then, and I say nothing now."

The rain came off the eave. It hit the stone.

"You have picked that up from the parish," I said. "They talk at your door."

"They talk at my door," she said. "They do not come in and they talk, and I have had forty years of it, and I know what is left in a room when you have gone out of it."

***

"Then tell me about one," I said. "One who answered you."

She was quiet for a while.

"The year the war ended," she said. "That was the year I asked them."

"Asked them."

"All of them. The seven. They came in their turn that year with the bread and the coal and the faggots, and each put his load down at the door, and I asked him the same question, and I asked it at the door and not in the room, because a man with his load still in his hands has not yet decided to stay."

"What question?"

"The one you have been asking me since the winter." She looked at me. "What do you want?"

I sat on the stool with the fire on my face.

"The smith would not say. The ferryman would not say, and he made a joke of it and went off up the lane laughing at his own joke. The woman at Long Bank said she wanted nothing and said it the way a person says a thing they have decided in advance. The sisters at the mill cottage answered each other, the way they do, and neither of them answered me. The man at the pump said he was too old to want anything and stood in the doorway and told me about his pension. The rest gave me nothing, and I stood at that door and took it and was glad of it, because a question that gets no answer costs nothing."

"One of them wanted something," I said.

"All of them wanted something."

"Then how do you know?"

"Because they came. A man who wants nothing does not carry another man's coal down a lane on his shoulder."

I sat with my hands flat on my knees.

"The woman at Long Bank," she said. "She told me she wanted nothing. She has arranged the bread for this house for forty years, and she has never once carried it down the lane herself, and she has never once knocked on that door. It is not that she is a hard woman. It is that a person who leaves bread on a stone has to be seen leaving it, and she would rather arrange it from a warm kitchen than be seen."

"That is not the same as a want."

"It is exactly the same as a want. It is a want that has been given a job to do instead of a name. She wants to be the one who gives. She has wanted it since she was a girl, and she will not say it, and so it goes and does itself in the dark every Monday, and I take the loaf in and put it under the cloth, and we have both been well satisfied with that arrangement for forty years."

"And you have never asked her again."

"No."

"Then your silence has been the most expensive thing in this parish," I said, "and you have charged it to her."

The fire took a piece of the dry wood. The flame went up. The room got brighter. She did not look away from me.

"Yes," she said. "I have charged it to her. I have charged it to all of them. It is the only bill I have ever sent."

"That is cold."

"I did not say it was right. I said it was the price, and I have paid it in the same coin and for the same number of years, and there is nobody in this parish who has paid more of it than I have."

She sat back in the chair.

"And the seventh," I said.

"The seventh came in the March with a sack of flour on his shoulder. Nichols. He kept the shop at the cross." She put her hand out and turned it over, and looked at the palm of it, and put it back in her lap. "He was a heavy man and the sack was heavy and he had carried it down the lane on his shoulder from the road, and he put it down inside the door and stood and got his breath, and I asked him. He took his hat off and held it in both hands. He said, I would be shut of it."

"Shut of the shop."

"Shut of the shop, and the counter, and the ledger, and the door with the bell on it that went every time anybody came in, and his wife's family that came with it. He said he would go to the coast and keep a boat. He said it in my doorway with his hat in his hands and the flour on his coat, and then he looked at me as if he had set something down that he could not pick up again."

"And then?"

"And then he went home and said it at his own table," she said. "I do not know how it went in that house. I know that his wife's brother came down the lane in the April and stood in my yard and told me to keep my questions to myself, and I stood in my door and let him say it, and I did not answer him either, which he took for agreement and was not."

The wood broke and fell into the ash.

"He sold the stock in the March of the year after," she said. "Not for much. The shutters went up and stayed up, and a woman from the row kept a bucket and a candle in there for a while, and then it was a house again with a family in it, and nobody in this parish has said his name in years. A letter came for his wife in the summer and she put it in the fire without opening it. He went, and he did not come back."

I sat with my hands resting on my knees.

"He was the only one of the seven who ever answered me," she said, "and he is the only one who is not here."

"That is not cause and effect."

"No."

"It is a story a person tells to make a rule out of an accident."

"It is a story I have," she said. "I did not make a rule of it. I stopped asking, that is all, and a person who stops asking does not have to explain it to anybody, and I have not explained it to anybody until this morning, and I am explaining it badly."

***

"Then you are telling me," I said, "that the way to care for a person is to want nothing from them."

"No."

"Then what?"

"It is to want it," she said, "and not take it."

"You think I do not ask because I do not care," she said. "I do not ask because I know what it costs, and I have never met a person yet who paid it and stayed the same shape afterwards."

The rain had gone off to a drizzle. The light in the room had come up a little.

"That is a cold house to live in," I said.

"It is the house I have."

"It is a house with one chair in it and no glass on the wall and a nail where the glass was."

"There was never a glass," she said. "There is a nail. I have never had a glass. The nail was there when I came and I have never taken it out, and a person may hang a thing on a nail or leave the nail, and I have left the nail."

I looked at the nail. It was at the height of a woman's eyes and there was nothing on it.

"You have let the parish pay for you for forty years," I said, "and you would not let a person ask you one question. Which of those is the colder house?"

"The parish pays," she said, "and does not ask. That is what a parish is. It is an arrangement for paying without asking, and I have taken what it paid because the alternative was the road, and I have never once pretended it was love. Do not put your question in the same box as their eight pounds. Their eight pounds is a line in a book that costs them nothing to write. Your question would cost you something to ask and me something to answer, and that is the difference, and if you cannot see the difference you are not the woman I have been sitting with."

I sat with that.

"I see it," I said. "I do not accept it."

"No," she said. "You would not."

***

"Then there is something you are not saying," I said.

"There is."

"Say it."

She looked at the window. The drizzle was on the glass. The lane beyond it was bent and green.

"You have been in this room since the winter," she said, "and you have put your hand on my chair and my table and my latch, and you have told me what you can do with a thing that has been handled. And I have let you, because a hand on a chair costs me nothing. But you have been asking me why I will not ask you, and I have given you a reason, and you have argued with it, and the reason is true and it is not all of it."

"Then give me the rest."

"I have taken an answer from you already," she said.

The fire moved. The wood settled. The flame leaned and came back.

"You have taken nothing," I said. "I have been in this room and you have given me bread and water and a stool and you have not once put your hand on me."

"No," she said. "Not in this room."

The rain dripped off the eave. It fell on the stone.

"When?" I said.

She did not answer that. She looked at the window for a while, and then she looked at me, and her face had the same flat quiet on it that it had when she told me about the man with the book, and the quiet was worse than anything she could have said.

"I have read you once," she said.

---

## Twenty-Eight · What She Read

The rain had gone in the night and the morning came up clear, and the door of the cottage stood open.

In a year of coming down that lane I had never once seen that door open. It was open a foot and the light went in through the gap and lay along the stone floor, and the smoke went up straight out of the chimney into a still sky, and there was a blackbird on the roof of the outhouse with its beak open.

I stood in the yard and looked at the open door.

Then I went up and knocked on the doorpost with the flat of my hand, which is a thing a person does at a door that is already open, and she said, from inside, "Come in or go home."

She was at the table with the loaf and the knife. The fire was built up and burning well and the pot was on the hob and the cloth was under the lid, and the room had the flat clean light in it that a room has when the door is open.

I sat on the stool. The ring was in my pocket.

"You did not sleep," she said.

"I slept."

"Then you lay awake after."

"I lay awake after."

She cut the bread and put a piece in front of me and pushed the salt along beside it, and I ate it, and it was this morning's bread and still warm in the middle.

***

"Ask it," she said.

I put the bread down.

"When?"

She wiped her hands on the cloth and put them in her lap, and she looked at the open door and not at me.

"Before the order," she said. "The order is dated ninety-three. We were on the road long before anybody put a date on us, and there is no paper for those years, and I have never been able to make anybody understand that. A parish writes you down when it wants rid of you. It does not write you down while you are going."

"Where was the road?"

"Everywhere. It went from one stone to the next stone and the men changed at the stones. We came to the ford in the afternoon of a wet day and the water was low and the stones were slick."

"Which ford?"

"The one with the toll house on the far side of it," she said. "There is a bridge and there is a gate on the bridge and there is a toll on the gate, and the man with the paper had no money for a toll, because a toll comes out of a man's own pocket and a paper does not. So he put us over the ford."

The room was very quiet. The blackbird went on with the morning.

"I know that ford," I said.

"I know you do."

"You could have said so."

"What would I have said?" She turned her head. "Come down my lane, Mrs. Rooke, I think I pulled you out of a river when you were small. There is no way to say a thing like that at a door."

I sat still.

"Go on," I said.

***

"I had her on my back," Tace said.

She said it plainly. She had her hands together and she said it the way she said the weight of a sack.

"I had her on my back under the shawl and my boots in my hand and the water came up over my knee, and the stones turned under my foot, and there was a child in the water."

"Was I in the water?"

"You were in the water," she said. "You had gone in off the bank and the water had you by the skirt and you had your mouth open and no noise coming out of it. I came off the stones sideways and got you by the arm."

I have a piece of that. It is not a picture, it is a weight: a hand closed round my forearm above the wrist, and the water going out of my ears, and the sky coming back, and a shawl against my face that smelled of wet wool and smoke, and a child coughing against my shoulder, a small cough, the kind that comes from low down and goes on after.

"There was a child coughing," I said.

"She coughed for the rest of that year."

"I remember the coughing. I have remembered it all my life and I did not know what it was."

"She was on my back," Tace said. "You were against her. That is why you remember it."

I put my hand on the table.

"You put me on the bank," I said. "You said, go home. You did not say anything else, and I thought you were angry with me, and I went home and I did not tell my mother, because a woman who is angry with you is a woman you have disobeyed."

"I was not angry."

"I know that now."

"You were in the water," she said, "and I got you out, and I put you on the bank, and I said go home, and you went. That is all that happened at the ford."

"That is not all that happened."

"No," she said. "It is not."

***

"And I had you before I let go," she said.

The fire moved. A piece of wood went down and the ash came up.

"Say what that means."

"It means what it says. It comes through the hand. I do not choose it and I have never been able to stop it, and I have not put my hand on a living soul since that afternoon."

I sat with my arm on the table, the arm she had taken hold of, and the room was the room and the fire was the fire, and it was the plainest thing in the world and it had been waiting for me for a year at the end of a lane.

"What did you have?" I said.

She looked at me.

"A person nobody had ever asked," she said.

She did not say it kindly and she did not say it cruelly. She said it the way she would have said the colour of a coat.

"That is all?"

"That is all. It came once, the way a bell says a thing once, and then it was gone, and I had a child on my back and a man with a paper waiting at the top of the bank, and I went up the stones and I did not look round."

I sat with it.

"A person nobody had ever asked," I said.

"Yes."

I have been asked a great many things in my life. I have been asked my name at doors and my business in rooms and whether I was the woman from Collow, and I have been asked to go away, and no one had ever put it to me in a handful of words that fitted like that.

"And you carried it," I said.

"I carried it."

"For forty years."

"For forty years," she said. "It is not a heavy thing to carry. It is only heavy when the person comes down your lane."

***

I got up off the stool and went to the door and stood in it with my back to her, and the light came over the yard and the leaf heap and the wet stone, and I stood there and did the arithmetic.

The water had me and it did not keep me. Every morning since that afternoon was a morning I had not been owed, and the reason I never knew what to do with them was that I had never been told where they came from. I had spent them asking other people's questions in other people's rooms, and I had been good at it, and I had told myself it was a trade and a use and a living, and the truth was simpler and worse: a person who has been given a life she was not owed does not think she may spend it on her own want. She spends it on other people's. It is the only way she can think of to pay it back.

I have known all my life that it was not to be wasted. I did not know who had decided that. Now I did.

I turned round.

"You took it without asking," I said.

"Yes."

"You have sat in this room for forty years with a thing in your hand that was mine, and you never sent for me and you never wrote and you never asked anybody whether the child at the ford had lived."

"No."

"Why not?"

"Because it was yours," she said. "A thing taken is not mine to give back, and it is not mine to spend, and I have not spent it. I have not said it to anybody until this morning, and I have said it to you now because you asked me a question at my door and I could not think of a way to answer it that did not come through this."

I stood in the middle of her floor.

"You could have told me in the winter."

"Yes."

"Why did you not?"

She looked at the fire.

"Because you would have been kind to me," she said. "And I have been paid for with kindness for forty years, and I did not want it off you as well."

***

I sat down on the stool.

"Then I have one question left," I said, "and it is not about the parish and it is not about the money and it is not about her."

"Ask it."

I have asked that question in a great many rooms. I have asked it with a pen in my hand and a book open and a paper at the bottom of it, and there was always a paper at the bottom of it, and the paper was the reason I was in the room.

This time there was nothing at the bottom of it. That was the difference, and it had taken me a year and a road and a wet lane to learn it.

"What do you want?" I said.

She did not answer.

The fire went on. The blackbird had stopped and the yard was quiet and somewhere out beyond the hedge a cart went along the top road, and the sound of it came down the lane and into the open door and went away, and she sat in the chair with her hands where they always were and did not answer.

I did not fill it. That was the hardest thing I did that morning. I have filled a great many silences in my time, because a silence in a room where I am asking is a silence I have caused and it is my business to end it. I sat on the stool and put my hands under my knees and let it run.

The fire took a piece of wood.

Then she said, "Somebody on the step. Of a morning. Before the fire is lit."

I sat with that.

"Why?"

"Because the first thing I hear in a day is my own hands," she said. "The grate and the lid and the besom. I have heard my own hands every morning for forty years and I know the sound of them better than I know my own face, and I would like, once, to hear somebody else in the yard before I have started."

"Not talking."

"Not talking."

"Just sitting there."

"Just sitting there," she said. "On the stone. Where the hollow is."

***

I got up and went out.

The stone step had the hollow worn in the front edge where a foot went over it, and the hollow held a little water from the night, and I put my hand in it and then I sat down.

The stone was cold through my skirt. The yard was in front of me and the lane beyond it and the gap at the top where the road went by, and the hedge on either side of the gap had been cut and the cut ends were pale where the sun was on them. The blackbird came down onto the leaf heap and worked through it.

I sat there. The sun came up over the outhouse and got onto my knees, and the stone under me began to take the warmth out of me and give nothing back, and I stayed.

The boy came down the lane with the dog.

He stopped at the gate when he came level with the yard, and he stood with his stick over his shoulder and looked at me sitting on the step of that door, and the dog came through the gap and up the yard and put its nose against my knee and I put my hand on its head.

"Is she dead?" the boy said.

"No."

"She has the door open."

"She has the door open."

He thought about that. He was a year older than when I first came down that lane and he had the same stick and the same slow way of deciding.

"My father says when the door is open the house is for sale," he said.

"Your father is wrong about that as well."

"All right," he said, and called the dog, and went on down the lane with the stick over his shoulder.

I sat on the step until the sun had come round onto the wall above my head, and the stone was warm under me where I had been, and I got up and there was a warm place on it the size of a person.

She had not come to the door. She had not looked out. She had let me sit there in her yard for the length of a morning, and she had gone on with whatever she did in that room when nobody was in it. She had not come to the door in order to be seen not coming.

***

I went in.

She was in the chair. The fire had been built up again and the pot was back on the hob.

I took the ring out of my pocket and put it on the table between the salt and the bread, and I turned it with one finger so that the worn side was up, and then I pushed it across the wood toward her.

It went a little way across the wood and stopped.

She looked at it.

"It is not a wedding ring," I said. "It has not been one since the day she died. She wore it at a gate so that nobody would have to ask her a question, and she left it to me in a box, and it is the only thing anybody ever left me that I did not have to ask for. I am not giving it to you to finish anything. There is nothing in this parish that a ring can finish. I am giving it to you because you told me what you wanted, and because a person who has been asked should not be left with her hands empty."

She did not say anything for a moment.

Then she put her hand out and took it.

She held it in her palm and closed her hand on it and sat with her fist on her knee, and the brass showed at the top of the fist where the fingers did not meet. She did not put it on. She turned it over once with her thumb.

"It is thin," she said.

"It is worn thin."

"By a hand," she said.

She got up out of the chair, and it took her the way it took her, and she went to the shelf and took down the tin and lifted the lid and put the ring inside and put the lid back on and set the tin on the shelf.

Then she stood with her hand flat on the shelf for a moment with her back to me.

"Tomorrow," she said.

"Tomorrow."

"Before the fire."

"Before the fire."

She did not turn round and she did not thank me, and I had not asked her to and she would not have known how, and a person who thanks you has put the thing behind them, and she had put a ring in a tin and she was standing at a shelf.

I went out. At the top of the lane I turned round. The door was open and the sun was on the stone step and the hollow in the step held its little water, and there was nobody in the doorway at all.

Nothing had been settled. The line was still in the parish book and Michaelmas was still coming and the eight pounds a year was still going to be struck through at the end of it, and I had come down that lane to change a line and I had not changed it, and I went up the road with my hands empty and no notion at all of what I would do next, and for the first time since Barrowfield I did not mind not knowing.

---

## Twenty-Nine · The Blank

We came home on the last day of June, and the house at Collow had the smell a house has when it has been shut for six weeks and the weather has been warm, which is the smell of other people's furniture.

I opened every window in it. Then I stood in the middle of the parlour with my box in my hands and did not know where to put it down.

That is a thing nobody tells you about coming home from a place where something finished. The house is the same size. The chairs are in the same places. And you stand in the middle of it holding a box with a dead woman's ring in it and you cannot think where a box like that is supposed to live.

Edward took it out of my hands and put it on the shelf by the clock, which was the highest shelf in the room and the one nobody used for anything.

"It can live there," he said.

"It is a box with a stranger's ring in it."

"It is a box with a woman's ring in it, and the woman is not a stranger, and the ring is not the only thing in the box." He took his coat off and hung it up. "You have carried it a hundred and eleven miles. It has earned a shelf."

***

I read the copybook again that night, all of it, from the first page to the blank one.

I had read it on the road and I had read it in the inn at Tindle and I had read the parts of it that mattered with a finger under the line. This time I read it the way I read a parish book, which is to say I read it for the things that were not in it.

There were no answers in it. Not one. Forty years of names, and beside each name a mark, and the marks were not answers, they were notes on the not-answering.

*Would not say.*

*Said she did not know.*

*Said, what business is it of yours.*

*Wept.*

*Asked me the same.*

Forty years of a woman going to a door and asking a question and writing down what the door did. It was the most patient document I had ever held, and it was the most frightening, and it took me until the last page to understand why.

She had never written down her own question.

Not once. Every entry was the name and the mark and the not-answer. Nowhere in forty years had she written the words she said at the door. She had kept the whole of it in her mouth for four decades and had put only the refusals on paper, and the refusals were the thing she could bear to look at, and the question was the thing she could not.

I sat with that for a long while with the lamp going down.

"Why would she not write it," I said.

Edward was at the other end of the table with the notebook in front of him and his pencil in his hand and he had not written anything for an hour.

"Because writing it down would have made it a form," he said. "A form is a thing you can hand to a person and say, fill this in. She did not want a form. She wanted a conversation, and a conversation cannot be kept, so she kept the only part of it that could be."

"The refusals."

"The refusals. And the names. A name is a thing a person will give you when they will not give you anything else." He turned the pencil over. "It is what I have been doing for four years. I have been keeping the questions and not the answers, because the answers were none of my business."

***

The next morning I took a blank book down off the shelf.

It was a good one, bought in Ely, quarto, ruled, with a calf cover and a hundred leaves, and I had bought it in the spring with the intention of beginning a fair copy of the Tindle rate-book, because the rate-book was forty years old and the mice had been at the corners and a parish that has only one copy of its own accounts has no accounts at all.

I put it on the table beside Esther's copybook and I ruled the first page into columns, which is a thing I can do in my sleep: name, place, the mark, the date. Then I sat down and opened the copybook at the first page and took up the pen.

I wrote one line.

*Mary Ann Ashby, the ford.*

I sat with it.

The second column was for the mark, and the mark in the copybook was *would not say*, and I had the words on the end of my pen and I did not write them. I sat there with the pen an inch above the paper until the ink dried on the nib and had to be wiped.

Here is what I had been about to do, and I want to put it down plainly, because it is the last time in this book that I will be stupid in a way I could have avoided.

I had been about to make a fair copy. I had been about to take forty years of a woman going to doors and asking a question, and put it into a book with ruled columns and a calf cover, so that it would not be lost. I had been about to preserve it.

And a preserved thing is a thing in a book, and a thing in a book is a thing a parish may consult and may answer, and the whole of Esther's life had been spent making sure that one woman was never in a book that a parish could answer.

If I copied those names, I would be doing to forty years what the vestry had tried to do to Tace. I would be putting a woman's whole argument into the record and calling it a kindness, and in twenty years a man in a good coat would read the column headed *would not say* and would say that it proved something.

I closed the blank book.

Edward was in the doorway with a cup in his hand and he had been there long enough to have seen the one line.

"You are not going to copy it," he said.

"No."

"Then why did you rule the page."

"Because ruling a page is what I do when I do not know what to do," I said. "It is the only prayer I have ever been able to say."

He came and looked at the page over my shoulder: the neat columns, the one name, the empty space where the mark should go.

"It will look like an unfinished book," he said.

"It is an unfinished book. It is the most unfinished book I have ever owned and I am going to keep it that way." I put the pen down. "If it is never finished, nobody can use it. An unfinished book is the only kind that cannot be quoted."

He stood there a while.

"That is either the wisest thing you have said in three years or the most cowardly," he said, "and I am not going to be the man who decides which."

"Both," I said. "It is usually both. That is why it has taken me so long to learn to do anything at all."

I put the copybook in the drawer, on top of the paper, and shut it with the flat of my hand, and the drawer went in easily because the handle was mended and the runners had been greased, and a drawer that shuts easily is a drawer a person can leave alone.

That night I took the blank book out again and looked at the one line.

*Mary Ann Ashby, the ford.*

My mother had been asked once, by a woman with a copybook, and she had not answered, and the not-answering had gone into a column and had been carried a hundred and eleven miles in a box and had come to me at the age of twenty-one in a room with a mended drawer.

I had spent four years wanting to know what my mother wanted. I had read her thimble and her comb and her boots and the elbow of her coat, and I had got a great deal out of them and none of it was the answer. The answer had been in a copybook in a cottage beyond a ford the whole time, and it was not an answer. It was the mark of a woman who had decided not to give one.

That is not nothing. A woman who will not answer has answered something. She has said: it is mine, and I am not putting it in your book.

I blew the candle out and went up to bed, and the window over the bed was open the width of a hand, and the room was cool, and I lay in it and listened to the pear tree.

***

At eleven o'clock I took the paper out of the box.

I had not looked at it since the day we left, and the fold had gone a little softer and the hole at the corner was the size it had been.

I put it on the table and put the pen beside it and sat down.

*What I want is*

I sat there for the length of time it takes a candle to burn an inch.

I want to be exact about what happened in that inch, because it was not what I had expected and it was not what I had been afraid of.

I had expected to fail. I had failed at that sentence in an inn at Thorneley in the spring of the year before, and I had failed at it in the dark of an inn at two in the morning, and I had failed at it in a mill yard with a man asking me a plain question. Failure was the thing I had come to expect from it, the way you come to expect a stair to give.

What happened was different. I sat with the pen and the sentence and I found that I did not want to write on it.

Not could not. Did not want to.

The sentence was a thing I had made in a bad hour to hold something I could not say. It had done its work. It had held the thing for thirteen months and it had gone with me to Ely and to a toll house and to a lane at the end of a village and it had come home in a box. And now, sitting at my own table with my husband six feet away and the window open, I found that the thing it was holding had got out.

It had got out in a lane, in front of a woman who asked me who I was and would not take my husband's name for an answer.

It had got out in a room where an old woman said, *because you would answer*, and I had understood, for the first time in my life, that being answered is not the same as being asked, and that I had spent three years giving people the first and calling it the second.

I put the pen down.

"Edward."

"I am here."

"I am not going to write on it."

He looked up. He looked at the paper and then at me and then at the paper again, and he did not say anything for long enough that I heard the clock tick twice.

"Say why," he said.

"Because if I write it down, it becomes a thing I have asked for," I said. "And I do not want to have asked for it. I want to be asked. Those are not the same and I have only just found out that they are not the same, and if I write on this paper tonight I will have turned the one into the other, and I will have done it myself, with my own hand, at my own table, and nobody will have made me."

Edward put his pencil down.

"Then what is the paper for," he said.

"Nothing. It is for nothing. It is a piece of paper with half a sentence on it and it has done the whole of its work and it is not going to do any more."

I folded it. I folded it the way it had been folded, twice, and the folds went into the old creases, which is a thing paper does and people cannot.

Then I opened the drawer and put it in, and pushed the drawer shut with the flat of my hand, and the drawer stuck, the way it had stuck since January, and I put my shoulder against it and it went in.

***

Edward got up and went out of the room and came back with a screwdriver and the saucer off the shelf.

"What are you doing?"

"I am putting the handle on."

"At midnight."

"It is not midnight. It is a quarter past eleven and I am a man of forty-seven and I have been looking at that saucer for six months." He knelt down at the drawer with the screwdriver. "You would not let me do it in January. You did not want it mended, and I did not ask why, because I had a theory about why and the theory was that a drawer that sticks is a drawer you have to think about, and a woman who has a paper in a drawer wants to have to think about it."

He put the screw in and turned it.

"That is a very good theory," I said.

"It is a very good theory and it is now a wrong one, because the paper is not a thing you have to think about any more, and a drawer may have a handle." He turned the screw another half turn and tested the handle with his thumb. "There. Now it opens like a drawer in a house where people live."

I knelt down beside him on the floor and put my hand on the handle.

It opened.

There was the tin box and there was the paper under it and the paper was a folded half sheet with half a sentence on it and it was the only thing in the house that was mine and not his, and it was in a drawer that opened properly.

"Edward."

"What?"

"Tomorrow you are going to ask me something."

He sat back on his heels.

"Yes," he said. "Tomorrow I am going to ask you something."

"And you have been waiting to ask it since the spring."

"Since before the spring," he said. "Since a night in November in a house in Barrowfield when you fell asleep in a chair with your boots on and I sat up and looked at you and thought, that woman has never once in her life been asked what she wants, and if I ask her now she will think it is a thing she owes me."

"That was two years ago."

"It was two years and seven months ago," Edward said, "and I have been waiting for a night when you would put the paper down and not write on it, and tonight you put the paper down and did not write on it, and so tomorrow I am going to ask you, and you will answer me, and if you cannot answer me tomorrow you will answer me on Thursday, and I will not be angry either way."

I stayed on the floor with my hand on the drawer handle.

"You have been very patient with me," I said.

"I have not been patient," he said. "I have been frightened. They look the same from the outside and they are not the same, and it has taken me two years and seven months to be able to say the difference out loud, which is that patience is a virtue and fright is only a fact."

---

## Thirty · Ask Me What I Want

The window in the bedroom stuck at the top, the way it had stuck every summer since we came to the house, and on the first of July I put my shoulder to the frame and got it up a hand's width before the paint let go and it came down again.

I stood with my hands on the sill.

A year ago I would have left it. I would have told myself that the room was cool enough and that a window is not a thing worth a quarrel with a house, and both of those things would have been true, and the leaving of it would have been a lie.

"Edward."

He was in the doorway with his collar in his hand.

"The window is stuck."

"It has been stuck since we came here."

"It has been stuck since we came here and I have left it stuck for two years and four months, and I am not going to leave it stuck this morning." I stepped back. "Come and put your shoulder to it. You are heavier than I am and I have been doing it wrong."

He put his collar down on the chest and came and put his shoulder to the frame and the frame went up a foot and then two feet and then the whole of the sash went up and the morning came into the room.

It came in green. That is the thing about that window. The pear tree at the end of the garden is against it and the light comes through the leaves and lands on the floor in a shape that moves, and I had not seen the shape on the floor for two years and four months because I had never once had the window far enough up to let it in.

I stood in the middle of the room with the light moving on the boards.

"Now," I said.

Edward turned round.

"Now what."

"Now ask me."

***

I want to put this down exactly, because I have read a great many accounts of a great many things and I have noticed that the writers of them tidy the moment up.

I did not stand in the middle of the room with my hands folded. I stood with my back to the window because the light was behind me and I had decided that without deciding it, the way I decide most things about a room before anybody has opened their mouth.

He did not come closer. He stood by the chest with his collar in his hand and his shirt open at the neck and he looked at me and waited, and the waiting was the thing he was best at and the thing I had found hardest to live with for two years.

"You said you were going to ask me something," I said.

"I did."

"Then ask me."

"Not yet," Edward said. "First you are going to say the thing you have been carrying since Barrowfield. I have watched you not say it for six weeks and I am not going to ask you a question while you are still holding that one, because you will answer me with half of yourself and then you will spend a month being angry with me for a thing I did not do."

I looked at him.

"There is nothing left to be angry with you about."

"There is always something left," he said. "It is usually the thing that is not the other person's fault."

I put my hands behind me and took hold of the sill.

"All my life I have asked for other people," I said. "Ask me. I will answer."

The room was quiet. The pear leaves moved on the floor.

"That is three things," Edward said at last.

"It is."

"The first one is not true. You have not asked for other people your whole life. You have asked for other people since you were old enough to notice that nobody was asking them, and before that you did not ask for anything at all, because a child in that house learned very early that a question is a thing that has to be paid for." He put his collar down. "Say the rest of it again. The last two. And say them as if you were not making a speech at your own funeral."

I stood with my hands on the sill and the light behind me.

"Ask me," I said. "I will answer."

"Yes," he said. "That is the whole of what I have been waiting for, and it has taken you a year and more, and I want you to know that I would have waited longer." He came across the room and stopped a foot away from me. "What do you want?"

***

I have thought since about what I might have said, and there is a version of that morning in which I say something large.

I could have said: I want to be a person in a book that is not a list of what I am for. I could have said: I want to be asked in every room I go into, by everybody, for the rest of my life. I could have said: I want my mother back, and I want the four years I spent learning to read dead things instead of learning to want anything, and I want the twenty pounds a woman lost in 1794 to buy me a place where nobody could move me.

All of those are true. I have said them to myself since, in the dark, and they are all true, and not one of them is an answer to a man standing a foot away at seven in the morning.

What I said was: "I should like the window open."

Edward did not move.

"The window is open."

"Not this one." I turned and put my hand flat on the sash and it was warm from the sun already. "That one. In the bedroom at the back, the small one over the bed, the one that has been painted shut since before we came. I have wanted it open every night for two years and four months and I have never once said so, and in the summer I have lain there in the dark and told myself that a woman of my age does not want a window open, she wants a room that is not damp, and I have been very sensible about it, and every night I have been a little bit less able to sleep."

He stood looking at me.

"That is what you want."

"That is what I want."

"Not the register. Not the case. Not the woman in the turning. Not to be asked by a bishop." His voice did something at the end of it that I had not heard in it before. "A window."

"A window," I said. "I have spent three years asking for large things on behalf of other people and I find that when I am asked for myself the answer is a window, and I am not ashamed of it, and if you laugh at me I will push you out of it."

Edward put his hand over his mouth and his shoulders went, and I understood that he was not laughing, or not only laughing, and that a man of forty-seven can stand in a bedroom at seven in the morning with his collar off and cry about a window.

"I am not laughing at you," he said, when he could.

"You are."

"I am laughing because I have spent two years and seven months frightened of a question, and the answer is a window, and the window is a thing I could have done in an afternoon in the first week." He wiped his face with the back of his wrist. "And I am crying because you have been lying in the dark in the heat for two summers with your mouth shut, and I did not know, and I slept beside you every night."

"You did not know because I did not say."

"That is what I am crying about," he said.

***

The small window at the back had been painted shut by a man who had painted the whole of that wall in the spring of 1832, and the paint had gone into the gap and set like stone. Edward worked at it for the whole of the morning with a knife and then a chisel and then, at eleven o'clock, with a mallet and a block of wood, and I held the block and he hit it and neither of us said anything for a long time.

At noon it gave.

It gave all at once, the way they do, and the sash came up six inches and a smell came in, which was the smell of the yard and the water butt and the pear tree and the summer, and Edward sat back on the floor with the mallet in his hand and his shirt through at the shoulder.

"There," he said.

I knelt down in front of him and took the mallet out of his hand and put it on the floor.

"Thank you," I said.

"You are welcome."

"No," I said. "Thank you. I am saying the words. I have said them to servants and to clerks and to a woman who gave me bread in a lane, and I have not said them to you for a thing that mattered, and I am saying them now, and I am not going to explain them, and you are not going to make a thing of them."

He looked at me.

Then he put his hand on the back of my neck the way he did, and I went to him, and we stayed on the floor of that room in the middle of the day with the window open and the yard coming in.

***

What happened after that is not a thing I mean to put down in detail, and the reason is not modesty.

The reason is that I have spent my life being exact about other people's business, and the one place where I have been allowed to be inexact is here, and I have come to think that the allowance is the whole of what a marriage is. There is a room in a house where the accounts do not go.

I will say this much, because it belongs to the book and not to the room.

He asked me again that afternoon. He asked me in the doorway with the light behind him and he did not make a ceremony of it, and I answered him, and the answer was different from the window, because a person's answer is different at four in the afternoon from what it is at seven in the morning, and that is not inconstancy. That is a person being asked twice.

And I understood, somewhere in the middle of that day, the thing about the second life that I had been carrying since the winter without knowing what it was for.

A woman read me once, in a lane by a ford, when I was a child. She read what I was for, and what I was for had never been used, and so I was not allowed to end. I had spent three years thinking of that as a gift, and it was not a gift. It was a woman refusing to close a case. She had been closed herself, in 1793, by a parish and a pen and a lawful order, and she had looked at a child in a lane and had declined to do the same thing, and the declining had cost her forty years of bread from seven households and one afternoon of a girl of nineteen putting down twenty pounds.

She never told me. I found out in a cottage at the end of a lane at the age of twenty-one, from a woman of seventy-one who had to be asked twice.

I do not know what to do with a thing like that except spend it.

***

I went back to the turning in August, and I went back in September, and by October the boy on the hedge had stopped reporting it.

The bread still went on the stone on Mondays. That arrangement did not change and was not written down anywhere, and Michaelmas came and the vestry met and the line that had said *the turning* was struck out of the overseers' book, and nothing was entered in its place, and a woman of seventy-one in a cottage at the end of a lane received, from seven households and one subscription from Collow, the sum of eight pounds a year, in quarterly payments, and no book in England recorded it.

Hesketh had been right about one thing and wrong about another, and the wrong one was the thing he had been most sure of. He had said that a thing done in the dark for forty years would not survive being brought into the light. It had survived it easily. What it could not survive was being written down. The moment it went into a book it became a charge, and a charge is a thing a parish may lawfully end. Left where it was, in seven pairs of hands and no ledger, it turned out to be the one arrangement in the county that no one had the power to stop.

***

In October I was at the door with the bread, and she opened it before I knocked, which she had begun to do in September, and she looked at the loaf and then at me.

"Will you come next week," Tace said.

I stood in the lane with the cold coming down off the field.

She had never asked me anything. In four months she had answered questions and refused to answer questions and once, at the end of a long afternoon, she had told me about a ring. She had never asked me a thing in her life. That was the whole of what Esther had written in the copybook and never put into words: forty years of asking, and not one question put to the woman who would have answered.

"Next week," she said again, and there was something in her voice that I had not heard in it before, which was the sound of a person who has asked a question and is waiting, and does not know yet whether the waiting will be worth it.

"Yes," I said. "I will come next week."

She nodded once, and took the bread, and went in, and shut the door with her hand flat on it the way she did.

I went back up the lane. It was the middle of October and the hedge was going over and there was a man on the road below with a barrow and the barrow had a wheel that wanted grease, and I could hear the wheel from the top of the lane, which meant the wind was coming up the valley rather than down it.

At the gate I stopped and stood for a while.

I asked for other people all my life. I said so to a man in a bedroom in July and he told me that it was not true, and he was right, and I have thought about it since and I have come to this: I did ask, once, when I was very small, and the asking was answered in a way that taught me not to do it again, and I spent thirty years being very good at not doing it again, and I got a trade out of it and a name for myself and a great deal of other people's gratitude, and none of it was the thing I wanted, and the thing I wanted turned out to be a window and a question and a woman at a door asking me whether I would come next week.

The wind came up the valley. The wheel complained.

I went in and shut the gate and the window over the bed was open, and it stayed open all night, and in the morning there was a little rain on the sill and I did not wipe it up, because a sill with rain on it is a sill that has been doing its work.

---

## After the End · A Note for the Reader

Thank you for reading. A few questions, none of which require you to remember the numbers:

1. Where did you stop reading, even for a moment? What was happening on that page?
2. Did the ending pay for the wait, or did you want more of it, or less?
3. Esther's copybook and Tace's refusal are the same argument from two directions. Did that land, or did it feel tidy?
4. How did you find the pace of the last six chapters?
5. If you could cut one scene from this book, which would it be?
6. If you described this book to a friend in one sentence, what would the sentence be?

Reply in any form – a list, a rant, notes read off the back of an envelope. All of it helps.
